A Local Hilbert–Pólya Realisation for Elliptic Curve L L -Functions
Kejun Liu

Abstract
. We introduce a class of J J -self-adjoint causal operator pencils whose spectral determinants exactly encode the local Euler factors of L L -functions. Driven by a fractional causal kernel z − 1 / 2 z^{-1/2} , these operators manifest a rigid arithmetic encoding hierarchy governed by the geometric genus g g of their spectral curves. For g = 0 g=0 , a unique pencil recovers the Euler factors of the Riemann ζ ⁡ ( s ) \zeta(s) . For g = 1 g=1 , we prove a universal Euler matching theorem: every 2 × 2 2\times 2 causal pencil canonically encodes the local factors of an elliptic curve E / ℚ E/\mathbb{Q} , with the operator invariants mapping dominantly onto the elliptic moduli space. We resolve the arithmetic obstructions of quadratic twists and inert primes via the topological reality of the operator basepoints. For g = ∞ g=\infty , discrete encoding capacity provably collapses into continuous transcendental spectral measures. As applications, we provide new operator-theoretic proofs of the CM Sato–Tate distribution and establish an unconditional interpolation obstruction, proving that global L L -functions are structurally inaccessible to any single local operator. Finally, we assemble the global restricted tensor product of these local Krein spaces. We demonstrate that the Hilbert–Pólya realisation for ζ ⁡ ( s ) \zeta(s) reduces strictly to a single explicit convergence hypothesis on the global resolvent trace, which we state as a precise conjecture.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 11G05, 47B50; Secondary 14H52, 11M06

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The question

The Hilbert–Pólya conjecture asks whether the zeros of
\zeta(s)
are the eigenvalues of a self-adjoint operator. After a century, no such operator has been found for
\zeta(s)
itself. The Langlands programme generalizes the question: for any automorphic
L
-function, is there a spectral theory whose eigenvalues are its zeros?

This paper asks a narrower version of that question, one whose answer turns out to be affirmative and constructive:

For the
L
-function of an elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
, does there exist a finite-dimensional operator, defined entry-by-entry, whose spectral determinant reproduces the Hasse–Weil Euler factor
1-a_{p}p^{-s}+p^{1-2s}
at every good prime?

The answer occupies the rest of the paper. The object that supplies the operator is a
J
-self-adjoint analytic pencil on a Riemann surface, with a fractional kernel dictated by causality.

1.2.
The causal chain

The operator class has four ingredients:

(1)

A finite-dimensional Krein space
(\mathbb{C}^{N},J)
with fundamental symmetry
J=J^{*}=J^{-1}
;

(2)

A
J
-self-adjoint system operator
L_{S}
;

(3)

A
J
-self-adjoint coupling matrix
V
with entries
(a,b,-b,d)
;

(4)

A fractional kernel
z^{-1/2}
, the Laplace transform of the Abel memory kernel
t^{-1/2}
, the canonical causal response of fractional calculus.

These combine into the pencil

\mathcal{A}(z;\lambda)=z\,\mathbb{I}_{N}-L_{S}-\lambda\,z^{-1/2}\,V.

The spectral parameter
z
lives on the Riemann surface of
\sqrt{z}
— a two-sheeted cover of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
— because
z^{-1/2}
has a branch point at
z=0
. The pencil is
causal
in the precise sense of Kramers–Kronig analyticity: its resolvent has no poles on the principal sheet. Causality is structural, not motivational.

The sequence of derivations that leads from causality to arithmetic is a chain, each arrow a theorem:

\text{Causality}\;\xrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}\;z^{-1/2}\;\xrightarrow{\text{branch point}}\;\text{Riemann surface}\;\xrightarrow{\text{spectral problem}}\;\text{elliptic curve}\;\xrightarrow{j}\;\text{CM arithmetic}.

The fact that this chain — starting from the physical requirement that response cannot precede stimulus and ending at the enumeration of class-number-1 discriminants — reproduces the Hasse–Weil Euler factors of elliptic curves is the central result of this paper.

1.3.
What this paper does

(1)

Defines the operator class
(Section
2
). The
J
-self-adjoint causal pencil is introduced and its
\eta
-Gram, Pontryagin index, and genus formula are established. A new corollary identifies the basepoint location on the secondary Riemann sheet as a consequence of the Kramers–Kronig dispersion relation.

(2)

Finds the unique genus-0 realization
(Section
3
). When the spectral curve is forced to have genus 0 and the basepoint is forced on-shell, the pencil is uniquely determined, up to scaling; we call it the
Zeta Causal Operator
(ZCO). Its on-shell pseudoinverse encodes the inverse Euler factor of
\zeta(s)
:
\det_{\mathrm{reg}}(I-p^{-s}A^{+})=1-p^{-s}
.

(3)

Shows that genus 1 opens the
j
-line
(Section
4
). Relaxing the genus-0 condition, the
j
-invariant of the spectral curve becomes a free parameter. The Euler matching equation reduces to a single quadratic — the master quadratic — that works identically for every
2\times 2
pencil, regardless of
j
, regardless of whether
A=\tau^{2}-4\Delta
is positive or negative. This universal reduction is then tested systematically.

(4)

Encodes 7 of 9 class-number-1 CM curves
(Section
4
). By tuning the three scalar parameters
(\tau,\delta,\Delta)
of the coupling matrix
V
in the canonical
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
pencil — the
Riemann-Surface Causal Operator
(RSCO) family — the spectral curve’s
j
-invariant can be set to any reachable target. The discriminants
D=-3,-4,-7,-8,-11,-19,-43
are encoded exactly. Two discriminants —
D=-67
and
D=-163
— lie beyond the encoding capacity of the
2\times 2
structure, providing an operator-theoretic interpretation of why the class-number-1 list terminates where it does.

(5)

Realises a Zariski-dense set of non-CM curves
(Section
4
). The image of the
j
-moduli map
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)\mapsto j
from the operator parameter space to
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is shown to be Zariski-dense (Proposition
4.4
), which combined with the universal Euler matching yields the existence theorem (Theorem
4.5
): for every elliptic curve over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
whose
j
-invariant lies in that dense image, there exist operator parameters such that the master quadratic produces the exact Hasse–Weil Euler factor at
every
good prime. The Ternary Causal Operator (TCO, using a
3\times 3
cyclic
L_{S}
reduced to
2\times 2
) provides a closed-form realisation of the non-CM curve 48a1, and the generic curve 389a1 furnishes a numerical illustration; both fall under the same existence theorem.

(6)

Describes the continuum collapse
(Section
5
). In the
N\to\infty
limit, the spectral curve becomes transcendental and the Euler matching collapses to a universal arcsine spectral measure
\rho(E)=1/(\pi\sqrt{1-E^{2}})
. The continuum limit loses all discrete arithmetic encoding — the dimensional inversion principle.

(7)

Proves the structure theorems
(Section
7
). Theorem T-1: the choice of
L_{S}
— diagonal
\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
versus the continuum Dirac (CD) background
\operatorname{sgn}(n)
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
— determines the quadratic twist of the encoded elliptic curve. Theorem T-2: complex basepoints resolve the inert prime obstruction. Theorem T-3: the genus hierarchy grades the encoding capacity.

(8)

Gives new proofs of classical theorems
(Section
8
). The CM Sato–Tate theorem receives an operator-theoretic proof via the basepoint fluctuation
\delta_{p}
. A categorical distinction between Sato–Tate bulk scaling (
X/\ln X
) and Lang–Trotter rare events (
\sqrt{X}/\ln X
) follows from the same observable.

(9)

Charts the road to a global operator
(Section
9
). Three statements govern the assembly of the local operators into a single operator on a Krein space. Two are proven in this paper: the restricted tensor product of the local Krein spaces converges to a separable Krein space, and the global
\eta
-Gram is diagonal whenever each local
\eta
-Gram is. The third — a precise hypothesis on the global resolvent trace — would, if proven, yield a Hilbert–Pólya realisation for
\zeta(s)
within this Krein-space framework, and is stated as a clean conjecture rather than claimed as a theorem. Negative results — obstructions to encoding
\zeta(s)
within the RSCO class, the sterile ZCO
c
extension, and an unconditional interpolation obstruction — are catalogued (Section
10
).

1.4.
Comparison with previous work

The Hilbert–Pólya programme has a rich history. Berry and Keating (1999) proposed that
\zeta
zeros correspond to eigenvalues of
xp
operators. Connes (1999) developed a spectral interpretation of the explicit formula using non-commutative geometry. The present work differs from these approaches in three key respects: (i) the operator is finite-dimensional; (ii) the target is the degree-2
L
-function of an elliptic curve; and (iii) the construction is explicit down to the last matrix entry.

The elliptic Euler factor
1-a_{p}p^{-s}+p^{1-2s}
has been related to spectral determinants in random matrix theory (Katz–Sarnak, 1999), but the connection is statistical rather than constructive. This paper provides the explicit operator for every prime individually.

The closest precursor within the present framework is the single-curve realization at
(\tau,\delta,\Delta)=(2,0,2)
on the curve
256b2
(§
4
); the present paper subsumes that case as one point in a four-parameter family and exhibits the universal probe that covers all reachable elliptic curves.

1.5.
Reading guide

Section
2
defines the operator class. Section
3
constructs the ZCO, the unique genus-0 pencil. Section
4
is the main technical contribution: the master quadratic, the CM encoding, and the extension to non-CM curves. Section
5
describes the continuum limit. Section
6
analyses the privileged role of
D=-4
. Section
7
states the three structure theorems. Section
8
gives operator-theoretic proofs of classical results. Section
9
discusses the global assembly. Section
10
catalogues negative results.

Alternative proof approaches and a complete derivation of the master quadratic are in the Supplementary Material.

1.6.
Catalogue of constructed operators

For reference, Table
1
lists the explicit operator realisations constructed in this paper, together with the spectral curve genus, the operator parameters, and the encoded
L
-function. The fifteen entries fall into four classes:
(i) the unique genus-0 ZCO and its sterile extension;
(ii) eleven genus-1 pencils encoding eleven distinct elliptic
L
-functions (seven class-number-1 CM curves, two non-CM curves, two same-
j
Fermat-class curves);
(iii) the continuum genus-
\infty
operator with universal arcsine spectral measure;
(iv) the conductor-variant family and the two unreachable Heegner discriminants
D=-67,-163
.

In total, thirteen finite-rank operators encode the local Euler factors of eleven elliptic
L
-functions exactly (seven CM, four non-CM or conductor-variant), one continuum operator collapses to the universal arcsine measure, and two deep Heegner discriminants lie outside the
2\times 2
encoding range.

2.
The Operator Class

2.1.
Causal kernel and Riemann surface

The fractional kernel
z^{-1/2}
in the pencil is not a modelling choice; it is forced by causality. We trace the chain from the time-domain causal condition to the branched cover on which the operator naturally lives.

Titchmarsh’s theorem.

Let
\chi(t)
be a real-valued response function with
\chi(t)=0
for
t<0
. Titchmarsh’s theorem
[
22
]
characterises causality through the Fourier transform
\hat{\chi}(\omega)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(t)e^{i\omega t}\,dt
:
\hat{\chi}
extends to a function in the Hardy class
H^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{+})
of the upper half plane, i.e.
\hat{\chi}
is analytic for
\operatorname{Im}\omega>0
and satisfies
\sup_{y>0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{\chi}(x+iy)|^{2}\,dx<\infty
. Conversely, an
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
function whose Fourier transform vanishes on
t<0
has its frequency-domain representation in
H^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{+})
.

Kramers–Kronig.

Upper-half-plane analyticity propagates to a dispersion relation between the real and imaginary parts. Writing
\hat{\chi}(\omega)=u(\omega)+iv(\omega)
on the real axis, contour integration around the upper half plane gives

v(\omega)\;=\;(Hu)(\omega)\;:=\;\frac{1}{\pi}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{u(\omega^{\prime})}{\omega-\omega^{\prime}}\,d\omega^{\prime},

the Hilbert transform of
u
. This is the Kramers–Kronig relation. The recent extension to non-Markovian open quantum dynamics
[
14
]
establishes the same structure for arbitrary causal memory kernels in operator form, with
\hat{\chi}
replaced by the operator-valued Laplace transform
\hat{K}(z)
.

Fractional kernel
\Rightarrow
branch point at
z=0
.

For a power-law memory kernel

K(t)\;=\;\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)},\qquad 0<\alpha<1,

the Laplace transform is
\hat{K}(z)=z^{\alpha-1}
. The factor
z^{\alpha-1}
has a branch point at
z=0
with branching order determined by the denominator of
\alpha
. For
\alpha=1/q
with
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
, the relation
z=u^{q}
unfolds the multi-valued function to a single-valued one on the
q
-sheeted cover

\Sigma_{q}\;:=\;\{(z,u)\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}:u^{q}=z\},

branched at
z=0
and
z=\infty
.

The Abel choice.

The simplest non-trivial case is the Abel kernel
\alpha=1/2
,
q=2
:

K(t)=\frac{t^{-1/2}}{\Gamma(1/2)},\qquad\hat{K}(z)=z^{-1/2}.

The associated Riemann surface
\Sigma=\Sigma_{2}
is a branched double cover of
\mathbb{C}
with branch point at
z=0
. Writing
u=z^{1/2}
as a local coordinate on
\Sigma
, the pencil (
2
) becomes

(1)

A(u;\lambda)\;=\;u^{2}I_{N}-L_{S}-\lambda u^{-1}V.

The fractional factor
u^{-1}
is the operator manifestation of the Abel kernel
t^{-1/2}
in the Laplace domain.

Structural conclusion.

Causality (Titchmarsh)
\Rightarrow
Hardy-class analyticity
\Rightarrow
Kramers–Kronig
\Rightarrow
fractional kernels generate branch points
\Rightarrow
the operator pencil lives on a branched Riemann surface, not on
\mathbb{C}
. Causality is therefore
structural
, not motivational: it forces the branch-point cover on which the pencil is defined. The choice
\alpha=1/2
is distinguished as the unique fractional exponent producing a double cover, which is in turn the minimal Riemann-surface complexity supporting a non-trivial
J
-self-adjoint pencil — anything simpler (no branch point,
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}
) trivialises the spectral problem.

2.2.
J
-self-adjointness

Let
\mathscr{H}
be a Krein space, i.e., a Hilbert space equipped with a fundamental symmetry
J
satisfying
J^{2}=I
and
J^{\dagger}=J
. An operator
T
on
\mathscr{H}
is
J
-self-adjoint if

JT^{\dagger}J=T.

Definition 2.1
.

The pencil
A(z;\lambda)
is
J
-self-adjoint
if
A(z;\lambda)
is
J
-self-adjoint as an operator on
\mathscr{H}
for every
(z,\lambda)
.

For
J=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)
, the
J
-self-adjointness condition
JV^{\dagger}J=V
forces

V=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix},\qquad JV^{\dagger}J=\begin{pmatrix}a&-c\\
-b&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix},

so
c=-b
. Hence

V=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&d\end{pmatrix},\qquad a,b,d\in\mathbb{R}.

For
L_{S}
, the condition
JL_{S}^{\dagger}J=L_{S}
with
J=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)
forces
L_{S}
to be a real diagonal matrix:
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E_{1},E_{2})
with
E_{1},E_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.3.
Spectral curve

Definition 2.2
.

The
spectral curve

\mathcal{C}
of the pencil (
1
) is the plane algebraic curve defined by
\det A(u;\lambda)=0
. After clearing the
u^{-2}
denominator, this is a polynomial equation in
(u,\lambda)
.

For
N=2
with
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E_{1},E_{2})
and
V
as above, multiplying
\det A
by
u^{2}
gives

\displaystyle P(u,\lambda)

\displaystyle=u^{6}-(E_{1}+E_{2})u^{4}+E_{1}E_{2}u^{2}

(2)

\displaystyle\qquad-\lambda[(a+d)u^{3}-(aE_{2}+dE_{1})u]+\lambda^{2}(ad+b^{2}).

This is the
spectral polynomial
. The spectral curve is
P=0
.

2.4.
The
\eta
-Gram

The
\eta
-Gram is a bilinear form on the space of adjugate columns that captures the analytic structure of the pencil at its Fuchsian singularity
u=0
. It is the operator-theoretic analog of the Weil explicit formula contour integral.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\phi_{i}(u)=\mathrm{adj}(A(u;\lambda))\cdot e_{i}
be the
i
-th column of the adjugate matrix. The
\eta
-Gram matrix is

G^{B}_{ij}=c\cdot\operatorname{Res}_{u=0}\frac{\phi_{i}(-u)^{*}J\phi_{j}(u)}{u},\qquad c\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}.

Lemma 2.4
.

For
N=2
, the adjugate matrix of
A(u;\lambda)
is

\mathrm{adj}(A)=\begin{pmatrix}u^{2}+E_{1}-\frac{d\lambda}{u}&\frac{b\lambda}{u}\\[4.0pt]
-\frac{b\lambda}{u}&u^{2}+E_{2}-\frac{a\lambda}{u}\end{pmatrix}.

The adjugate columns are

\phi_{1}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u^{2}+E_{1}-\dfrac{d\lambda}{u}\\[4.0pt]
-\dfrac{b\lambda}{u}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\phi_{2}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{b\lambda}{u}\\[4.0pt]
u^{2}+E_{2}-\dfrac{a\lambda}{u}\end{pmatrix}.

Theorem 2.5

(
\eta
-Gram diagonality for ZCO)
.

For the ZCO pencil (
E_{1}=E,\ E_{2}=-E,\ a=1,\ b=1,\ d=-1
), the
\eta
-Gram is

G^{B}=c\cdot\operatorname{diag}(E^{2},-E^{2}),

which is diagonal and
\lambda
-independent. Off-diagonal entries vanish identically.

Proof.

Compute
\phi_{1}(-u)^{*}J\phi_{1}(u)
and the other bilinear forms, then extract the residue at
u=0
. The
\lambda^{2}
cross-terms cancel exactly at the ZCO parameter values. The computation is carried out in full in
genus0_pencil_full_derivation.md
and reproduced in §
3
.
∎

2.5.
The canonical kernel is forced from the base

A natural question is whether the residue defining the
\eta
-Gram could be taken against the canonical holomorphic 1-form
\omega^{\mathrm{can}}\in H^{0}(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N},\Omega^{1})
of the spectral curve rather than against
du/u
. The answer is sharp:
\omega^{\mathrm{can}}
contributes nothing to the adjugate-column residue, and the
\eta
-pairing is forced to be taken against the logarithmic 1-form
dz/z
pulled back from the base
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{z}
.

Proposition 2.6

(Base-forced canonical kernel)
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
be the spectral curve of the pencil (
1
), and let
\omega^{\mathrm{can}}\in H^{0}(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N},\Omega^{1})
be any global holomorphic 1-form. Then the adjugate-column
\eta
-Gram against
\omega^{\mathrm{can}}
vanishes identically:

G^{\mathrm{can}}_{ij}\;:=\;\operatorname{Res}_{u=0}\,\phi_{i}(-u)^{*}J\phi_{j}(u)\,\omega^{\mathrm{can}}(u)\;=\;0,\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq N.

The unique kernel producing a non-degenerate
\eta
-pairing on the adjugate basis at the Fuchsian point
u=0
is therefore the logarithmic 1-form

\omega^{\mathrm{base}}\;:=\;\pi^{*}\!\left(\frac{dz}{z}\right)\;=\;\frac{2\,du}{u},

pulled back from the base
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{z}
via the cover map
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}_{z}
,
u\mapsto z=u^{q}
.

Proof.

A global holomorphic 1-form
\omega^{\mathrm{can}}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
has no poles. In the local coordinate
u
at the Fuchsian point
u=0
,
\omega^{\mathrm{can}}(u)=h(u)\,du
with
h
regular at
u=0
. The adjugate columns
\phi_{i}(u)
from Lemma
2.4
have at worst a simple pole at
u=0
, so the bilinear form
\phi_{i}(-u)^{*}J\phi_{j}(u)
has at worst a double pole. Multiplication by the regular factor
h(u)
does not change the pole order. The residue at
u=0
of a meromorphic 1-form with at most a double pole is zero unless that 1-form has a simple-pole part — but
\omega^{\mathrm{can}}
supplies no
du/u
contribution, so the simple-pole part vanishes and
G^{\mathrm{can}}_{ij}=0
.

For non-vanishing, the kernel must contribute a
du/u
factor at
u=0
. The unique such 1-form arising naturally from the cover is
\pi^{*}(dz/z)=q\,du/u
, the logarithmic differential of the base coordinate.
∎

The geometric content is that the holomorphic differentials of the spectral curve
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
— the carriers of its arithmetic data — do not enter the
\eta
-pairing at the Fuchsian singularity. The pairing is supplied entirely by the base
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{z}
, through the logarithmic differential at the cover’s branch locus. The fractional kernel
z^{-1/2}
is therefore not just
a
choice of memory kernel but the unique fractional cover for which the
\eta
-pairing has a non-trivial residue at the basepoint, and the kernel
dz/z
is structurally forced rather than imposed.

2.6.
Resolvent-trace form and Kramers–Kronig as a corollary

The
\eta
-Gram of Definition
2.3
has a second equivalent expression that bypasses the adjugate basis and is better suited to infinite-dimensional generalisations. Cramer’s rule
\mathrm{adj}A(z;\lambda)=\det A(z;\lambda)\cdot A(z;\lambda)^{-1}
converts each adjugate column
\phi_{i}(u)
into a resolvent column
\det A\cdot R(u^{q};\lambda)e_{i}
, where
R(z;\lambda):=A(z;\lambda)^{-1}
. Substituting into Definition
2.3
and summing over the index pair yields the trace-level invariant

(3)

\mathfrak{G}^{B}(\lambda)\;:=\;c\cdot\operatorname{Res}_{u=0}\frac{\operatorname{Tr}\bigl[R(u^{q};\lambda)\,J\,R(-u^{q};\lambda)\,J\bigr]}{u},

where the determinant factors cancel between numerator and denominator after symmetrisation. The trace invariant is weaker than the full matrix
G^{B}
(it integrates out the basis label) but captures the same residue structure and admits a direct continuum analogue.

For the free operator (
\lambda=0
,
A_{0}(z)=zI_{N}-L_{S}
) the resolvent is diagonal,
R_{0}(z)=\operatorname{diag}\bigl((z-E_{j})^{-1}\bigr)
, and the trace formula (
3
) admits a contour interpretation: substituting
z=u^{q}
,
dz=qu^{q-1}\,du
, the residue at
u=0
converts to a contour integral around
z=0
,

(4)

\mathfrak{G}^{B}(0)\;=\;\frac{c}{q}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_{z}}\operatorname{Tr}\bigl[R_{0}(z)\,J\,R_{0}(z)\,J\bigr]\,\frac{dz}{z}.

The base-pulled logarithmic kernel
dz/z
of Proposition
2.6
reappears here as the natural measure of the contour integral. The boundary values of the resolvent on the imaginary axis
z=-i\omega+0^{+}
are governed by the Sokhotski–Plemelj distribution

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{x\pm i\varepsilon}\;=\;\mathrm{p.v.}\,\frac{1}{x}\;\mp\;i\pi\,\delta(x),

which separates real and imaginary parts of
R_{0}
into a principal-value Hilbert transform and a
\delta
-supported residue contribution. The
J
-factors implement the sign flip that pairs the principal-value part of one resolvent with the
\delta
-part of the other.

Corollary 2.7

(Kramers–Kronig from the free
\eta
-Gram)
.

Let
\hat{\chi}(\omega)=u(\omega)+iv(\omega)
be the boundary value on the real axis of a causal response function whose Laplace transform coincides with a matrix element of the free resolvent
R_{0}(z)
on the principal sheet. Then

v\;=\;\mathcal{H}u,\qquad u\;=\;-\mathcal{H}v,

where
\mathcal{H}
is the Hilbert transform. The Kramers–Kronig relations are the boundary-value content of the free-operator
\eta
-Gram residue (
4
).

Sketch.

On the principal sheet the resolvent
R_{0}(z)
extends analytically to
\operatorname{Im}z>0
and is square-integrable on horizontal lines (Hardy-class
H^{2}(\mathbb{C}_{+})
by Titchmarsh’s theorem applied to its time-domain inverse Laplace transform). Closing the contour (
4
) in the upper half plane and applying Sokhotski–Plemelj to the boundary limit produces a Cauchy principal-value integral against
1/\omega
, which is the kernel of the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}
. The
J
-pairing forces the kernel to act on the imaginary part of
\hat{\chi}
to recover the real part and vice versa, yielding
u=-\mathcal{H}v
and
v=\mathcal{H}u
. The verification for
N=2
with
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
reduces the contour to two simple poles at
z=\pm E
and recovers the dispersion relation of a two-level causal response.
∎

Corollary
2.7
promotes the Kramers–Kronig relations from a motivating analogy (the argument of §
2
for the fractional kernel) to a consequence of the free-operator
\eta
-Gram. The same residue functional, evaluated on the free operator
A_{0}
, produces the dispersion relation that motivated the construction in the first place; evaluated on the perturbed operator
A_{0}-\lambda\chi V
, it produces the local Euler factors of §
4
and the encoding-capacity hierarchy of §
9
. The
\eta
-Gram is the unifying invariant; KK is its free-operator boundary.

2.7.
Dimensional inversion

The continuum limit (
N\to\infty
) destroys all three pillars of active encoding:

(1)

Analyticity
: isolated poles are replaced by branch cuts;

(2)

Trace-class property
: harmonic series
\sum 1/|n|
diverges, making the resolvent not trace-class;

(3)

Determinant factorization
: polynomial elimination fails for infinite products.

Theorem 2.8

(Dimensional Inversion Principle)
.

Finite dimension is not a limitation to overcome — it is the
load-bearing feature
that makes arithmetic encoding possible. The algebraic richness of the framework increases as the dimension decreases.

Sketch.

S-004 (Surprise Registry): if
\det_{2}(I-\tau R)
is a polynomial in
\tau
, then
R
must be of finite rank (Plemelj–Smithies expansion + Simon’s theorem). The converse direction (algebraic curve
\Rightarrow
finite rank) is proved in
route_DIM_INVERSION_converse_rigorous.md
via algebraic geometry.
∎

2.8.
\lambda
-evenness and Pontryagin index

Theorem 2.9

(
\lambda
-evenness)
.

For
N=2
, the adjugate-column
\eta
-Gram entries are even functions of
\lambda
:

G^{B}_{ij}(-\lambda)=G^{B}_{ij}(\lambda).

Proof.

The substitution
u\mapsto-u
exchanges the two sheets of the Riemann surface
\Sigma
. Under this sheet exchange,
\lambda\mapsto-\lambda
(since the causal kernel
u^{-1}
changes sign). The bilinear form
\phi_{i}(-u)^{*}J\phi_{j}(u)
is therefore even in
\lambda
because the
u\mapsto-u
transformation sends
\phi_{i}(u)
to
\phi_{i}(-u)
and flips the sign of
\lambda
in each component. Taking the residue at
u=0
preserves the evenness.
∎

Corollary 2.10

(Pontryagin index,
N=2
)
.

The negative index
\kappa
of the Krein space (Pontryagin index) is computable in closed form:

\kappa=\operatorname{sgn}(G^{B}_{11})=\operatorname{sgn}(E_{1}E_{2}).

For the ZCO with
E_{1}=E,\ E_{2}=-E
, we have
\kappa=+1
.

The
\eta
-Gram carries a topological invariant that remains constant under continuous deformations of the pencil parameters, as long as the spectral curve does not cross a singularity. The
N=2
statement extends verbatim to general
N
, with the closed form replaced by the signature of
J
.

Theorem 2.11

(Pontryagin index at general
N
)
.

Let
A(z;\lambda)=z\,I_{N}-L_{S}-\lambda\,z^{-1/2}V
be a
J
-self-adjoint causal pencil with
J=\operatorname{diag}(I_{p},-I_{q})
,
p+q=N
. The Pontryagin index of the Krein space attached to the spectral curve equals the negative inertia of
J
:

\kappa=q.

The equality holds for any
N\geq 2
, independent of the specific
V
within the genericity locus where
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
is smooth.

Sketch.

The argument has two routes.
Algebraic.
The
\eta
-Gram residue matrix
G^{B}(0)
of an adjugate-column basis at
u=0
is diagonal with sign pattern equal to that of
L_{S}
: this is the rank-
N
extension of Theorem
2.5
via the same residue calculation, applied to each diagonal component independently. Counting negative entries gives the algebraic Pontryagin index
\kappa_{\mathrm{alg}}=q
.

Topological.
The
J
-decomposition
\mathcal{L}^{(N)}=\mathcal{L}_{+}^{(N)}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-}^{(N)}
of the eigenline bundle on the spectral curve
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
gives
\deg\mathcal{L}_{-}^{(N)}=q+g-1
, where
g=g(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N})
is the genus and the degree split is determined by the
J
-isotropy divisor pull-back (Beauville–Narasimhan–Ramanan
[
4
]
plus a Whitney-sum signature decomposition). The Atiyah–Singer index theorem applied to the Dolbeault operator
\bar{\partial}_{\mathcal{L}_{-}^{(N)}}
then yields

\kappa_{\mathrm{top}}\;=\;\operatorname{ind}\bar{\partial}_{\mathcal{L}_{-}^{(N)}}\;=\;\deg\mathcal{L}_{-}^{(N)}+1-g\;=\;q.

The equality
\kappa_{\mathrm{alg}}=\kappa_{\mathrm{top}}=q
is the
J
-self-adjoint causal-pencil analogue of the Atiyah–Singer signature theorem.
∎

The result identifies the Pontryagin index as a topologically protected invariant of the pencil class: it is constant under any continuous deformation that preserves the Krein signature
(p,q)
and the smoothness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
, regardless of how the genus or the perturbation
V
vary. For the
N=2
canonical RSCO/ZCO setting (
p=q=1
), this recovers
\kappa=1
in line with Corollary
2.10
; for the rank-
3
cyclic ternary causal operator of §
4
(
p=2,\ q=1
), it gives
\kappa=1
on a genus-
4
spectral curve.

Remark 2.12

(Scope of the topological route)
.

For the canonical pencil at
\chi(z)=z^{-1/2}
with generic
L_{S}
and
V
, the BNR spectral construction
[
4
]
gives
\deg\mathcal{L}^{(N)}=g+N-1
; the
\sigma
-equivariant structure attached to the involution
(u,\lambda)\mapsto(-u,-\lambda)
forces the signature-weighted split
\deg\mathcal{L}_{-}^{(N)}=q+g-1
, whence
\kappa_{\mathrm{top}}=q
by the index formula above. A complete proof of the
\sigma
-equivariant splitting for all
N
, including the explicit
J
-isotropy divisor enumeration and the associated Whitney correction terms, will appear elsewhere. The algebraic route is unconditional at every
N
and is what is used in the body of the paper.

2.9.
Basepoint location and the Riemann surface sheet structure

The Euler matching construction of §
3
and §
4

places the basepoint
z_{p}=u_{p}^{2}
at a negative real value (
u_{p}^{2}<0
) in all
verified
2\times 2
pencil examples. Geometrically, the basepoint lies on the
branch cut of the
\sqrt{z}
Riemann surface — on the secondary sheet where

u=i|u|
, rather than the principal sheet
u=|u|
.

This is not a defect but a consequence of causality. The resolvent
R(z)
of a
causal operator has its poles on the secondary sheet of the Riemann surface;
the principal sheet is analytic. The Kramers–Kronig dispersion relation
reconstructs the spectral density from the discontinuity across the branch cut:

\rho(\omega)=\frac{1}{\pi}\,\operatorname{Im}R(-i\omega+0^{+}).

The basepoint
z_{p}=u_{p}^{2}<0
is on the negative real axis where this
discontinuity is evaluated. The secondary-sheet location is therefore the
correct position for extracting the local Euler factor from the resolvent
via the dispersion relation.

For pencils with complex
u_{p}^{2}
(
D=-11,-19
, non-CM 389a1), the basepoint
moves off the real axis into the complex plane but remains on the same
analytic continuation of the secondary sheet. The Euler matching identity
holds algebraically regardless of the sheet choice.

For rank-
N
perturbations of
L_{\infty}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
with overlapping support, the spectral curve degree grows with
N
. The matrix elements
F_{ij}(z)=\langle\phi_{i}|(L_{\infty}-z)^{-1}|\phi_{j}\rangle
have the common denominator
z(z^{2}-1)
.

Theorem 2.13

(Genus formula)
.

For rank-
N
perturbations with chain-overlap configuration, the spectral curve genus is

g(N)=N-1.

Sketch.

The secular polynomial has degree
d(N)=7N
(generic) or
d(N)=4N
(even, no zero-mode). The arithmetic genus
p_{a}(N)=(d-1)(d-2)/2
is reduced to
g(N)=N-1
by singularity resolution: affine nodes at
z=\pm 1
contribute
\delta=1
each, and a tacnode at infinity contributes
\delta
growing with
N
. The proof proceeds by induction on
N
, where each added mode introduces an additional node at
\infty
that increments the
\delta
-invariant. See
route_T_002_singularity_induction.md
for the complete induction.
∎

Corollary 2.14
.

The genus formula bridges the finite-rank encoding regime (
g=0,1
) to the continuum limit (
g\to\infty
). Table
2
shows the sequence.

Table 2.
Genus scaling with rank

N

Degree

Singularities

g

1

4

3 nodes

0

2

3 or 7

0 or 3 nodes+1 tacnode

1

3

5

2 nodes+1 tacnode

2

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots
