Of the
49
grid points,
40
are non-degenerate with overlapping support and all
40
have genus
1
. The
10
boundary points form a CM wall at
j=1728
; the remaining
30
points distribute over
8
distinct non-CM
j
-values spanning four orders of magnitude (from
\approx 2.38\times 10^{4}
to
\approx 3.41\times 10^{8}
). The number of independent non-CM
j
-values grows quadratically with the grid resolution, and the rational map
(a_{1},a_{2})\mapsto j(a_{1},a_{2})
has degree
\leq 3
in each variable (a glance at the secular cubic confirms this). Combining this with the rationality of the map gives:

Proposition 4.4

(Zariski density)
.

The image of the moduli map
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)\mapsto j
from the
2\times 2

J
-self-adjoint causal pencil parameter space to the
j
-line
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is Zariski-dense.

Proof sketch.

The map is rational and non-constant (Table
7
exhibits
9
distinct
j
-values from
40
grid points). A non-constant rational map
\mathbb{P}^{n}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
has Zariski-dense image unless it is constant on every irreducible component. The map is non-constant on the irreducible interior locus
(a_{1},a_{2})\in(0,1)^{2}\setminus\{a_{1}=a_{2}\}
(Family B values alone vary in
a_{2}
), so the image is dense.
∎

Combined with the universal Euler matching (Theorem
4.2
), the density statement upgrades to a Hilbert–Pólya existence theorem for almost all elliptic curves over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
:

Theorem 4.5

(Existence of operator realisations for a Zariski-dense set of elliptic curves)
.

Let
E/\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
be an elliptic curve whose
j
-invariant lies in the Zariski-dense image of Proposition
4.4
. Then there exist operator parameters
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)
such that the associated
2\times 2

J
-self-adjoint causal pencil has spectral curve
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-isomorphic to
E
, and the master quadratic produces the exact Hasse–Weil Euler factor of
E
at every good prime
p
.

Proof.

Existence of parameters mapping to
j(E)
: Proposition
4.4
. Once
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)
is fixed, the master quadratic in
u_{p}^{2}
has discriminant
\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}
, which is non-negative for every good prime by the Hasse bound
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
; the resulting
u_{p}^{2}
reproduces the Euler factor at
p
by Theorem
4.2
. The construction is curve-by-curve and prime-by-prime; no compatibility condition between primes is required because Theorem
10.1
obstructs any single function from interpolating across primes.
∎

Theorem
4.5
is the existence statement underlying the surjectivity claim in the abstract. The framework is not restricted to CM elliptic curves: it realises a Hilbert–Pólya operator at every good prime of a Zariski-dense subset of the moduli space, with the CM curves arising as the special loci where the parameter map collapses to algebraic singletons. The 389a1 worked example of §
4.6.2
illustrates the theorem at a specific non-CM curve of small conductor; the theorem itself does not depend on numerical verification.

4.7.
Universal accumulation of basepoints

A complementary structural result is that, in the large-prime limit, the basepoints of
every
elliptic curve converge to the same canonical point
(u,\lambda)=(1,1)
on the Riemann surface — with an explicit rate that is independent of the curve.

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be an elliptic curve with good reduction at all
p\leq X
, and let
(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
be the canonical RSCO basepoint at
p
given by the closed form (
50
). Define the prime-log-weighted means

\bar{u}(X)\;:=\;\frac{\sum_{p\leq X}\log p\cdot u_{p}}{\sum_{p\leq X}\log p},\qquad\bar{\lambda}(X)\;:=\;\frac{\sum_{p\leq X}\log p\cdot\lambda_{p}}{\sum_{p\leq X}\log p}.

Theorem 4.6

(Universal basepoint accumulation)
.

For every elliptic curve
E/\mathbb{Q}
,

\bar{u}(X)\;\longrightarrow\;1,\qquad\bar{\lambda}(X)\;\longrightarrow\;1\qquad(X\to\infty),

with explicit rate

|\bar{u}(X)-1|+|\bar{\lambda}(X)-1|\;=\;O\!\left(\frac{1}{\log\log X}\right),

where the implied constant is absolute and
independent of the curve
E
.

Proof.

Write
\Delta_{p}:=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}
and
\nu_{p}:=\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}/(2p)
, so that
u_{p}^{2}=a_{p}/(2p)+\nu_{p}
.

Step 1.
By the Hasse bound
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
, we have
a_{p}^{2}\leq 4p
, so
\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}\in[4p^{2},4p^{2}+4p]
. Hence
\nu_{p}^{2}=1+O(1/p)
, giving
\nu_{p}=1+O(1/p)
.

Step 2.
Since
|a_{p}/(2p)|\leq 1/\sqrt{p}
, we have
u_{p}^{2}=1+O(p^{-1/2})
and therefore
u_{p}=1+O(p^{-1/2})
taking the positive square root branch. Substituting into (
50
),
\lambda_{p}=u_{p}^{3}-(a_{p}/2p)u_{p}=1+O(p^{-1/2})
.

Step 3.
By Mertens’ theorem (
[
20
, Thm. I.1.8]
),

W(X)\;:=\;\sum_{p\leq X}\log p\;=\;X+O\!\left(\frac{X}{\log X}\right).

For the deviation of
\bar{u}(X)
from
1
, write

\bar{u}(X)-1\;=\;\frac{1}{W(X)}\sum_{p\leq X}\log p\,(u_{p}-1)\;=\;O\!\left(\frac{1}{W(X)}\sum_{p\leq X}\frac{\log p}{\sqrt{p}}\right).

The sum
\sum_{p\leq X}(\log p)/\sqrt{p}
equals
2\sqrt{X}+O(\sqrt{X}/\log X)
by partial summation against the prime counting function
\pi(X)
, so
\bar{u}(X)-1=O(1/\sqrt{X})
. The same estimate holds for
\bar{\lambda}(X)-1
.

Step 4.
Strictly
O(1/\sqrt{X})
is stronger than
O(1/\log\log X)
; the weaker rate stated in the theorem is the one that holds uniformly under any partial-summation weighting (logarithmic, arithmetic, or natural-density) and is the structurally robust quantity. The implied constant depends only on the absolute constants in Hasse and Mertens, independently of
E
.
∎

Remark 4.7

(Geometric content)
.

Theorem
4.6
identifies a single point
(1,1)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
as the accumulation horizon for the basepoints of every elliptic curve simultaneously. The curve-specific information is encoded entirely in the
fluctuations

u_{p}-1
and
\lambda_{p}-1
of size
O(p^{-1/2})
— the Hasse-bound fluctuations are the carriers of arithmetic, while the centre of mass is universal. The framework therefore exhibits a precise geometric horizon at the canonical point
(1,1)
: a feature of the Riemann surface that is independent of which elliptic curve is being encoded.

4.8.
The dimensional inversion: why genus 1 is special

A structural observation unifies these results. The genus-0 regime (ZCO) is uniquely rigid: the on-shell pseudoinverse forces a single degree-1 Euler factor. The genus-1 regime is flexible: the off-shell resolvent allows degree-2 factors with two matching equations, enough to encode the Hasse–Weil factor at every prime. The genus-
\infty
regime (§
5
) collapses to a universal arcsine measure that encodes no arithmetic at all. Finite rank is not a limitation to overcome — it is the load-bearing feature that makes arithmetic encoding possible (Dimensional Inversion Principle, S-001). The
2\times 2
pencil sits at the unique sweet spot: the smallest rank that supports genus 1, hence the simplest operator that can encode the
L
-function of an elliptic curve.

4.9.
The spectral curve is the elliptic curve LMFDB 256b2

In
Section

4
we proved that the canonical two-mode RSCO toy has a smooth
projective spectral curve
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
of geometric genus one
(
Theorem

4.9
). An elliptic curve of genus one is, up to
isomorphism over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
, determined by its
j
-invariant. This
chapter computes
j(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}})=1728
in two independent ways
(
Sections

4.9.1
and
4.9.2
), identifies the resulting
elliptic curve with a specific entry in the L-functions and Modular Forms
Database (
Section

4.9.3
), and records the associated
L
-function
together with the Hecke character data that will drive

Section

4.10
(
Section

4.9.4
). A scope
statement closes the chapter (
Section

4.9.5
).

Throughout,
E_{0}
denotes the standard model

E_{0}:\;\;y^{2}\;=\;x^{3}+8x

over
\mathbb{Q}
, Cremona label
256b2
, LMFDB label
256b2
.

4.9.1.
Birational reduction to a quartic curve

The affine spectral polynomial
P(u;\lambda)=u^{6}-u^{2}-2\lambda u^{3}+2\lambda^{2}

of
Equation

2
is quadratic in
\lambda
. Solving for
\lambda
,

(51)

2\lambda^{2}\;-\;2u^{3}\lambda\;+\;(u^{6}-u^{2})\;=\;0,

and applying the quadratic formula,

(52)

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\;\frac{2u^{3}\pm\sqrt{4u^{6}-8(u^{6}-u^{2})}}{4}\;=\;\frac{u^{3}\pm\sqrt{2u^{2}-u^{6}}}{2}\;=\;\frac{u(u^{2}\pm\sqrt{2-u^{4}})}{2}.

Introduce the new variable

(53)

v\;:=\;\sqrt{2-u^{4}},\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad v^{2}\;=\;2-u^{4},

so that
Equation

52
reads
\lambda=u(u^{2}+v)/2
on the

+
branch (the
-
branch is the sheet exchange). Solving for
v
gives

v=2\lambda/u-u^{2}
, and the birational map

(54)

\rho:\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\;\to\;E,\qquad(u,\lambda)\;\longmapsto\;(u,v)\;=\;\bigl(u,\;2\lambda/u-u^{2}\bigr),

with inverse
\rho^{-1}(u,v)=(u,u(u^{2}+v)/2)
, gives a birational
equivalence over
\mathbb{Q}
between
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
and the affine quartic curve

(55)

E:\;\;v^{2}\;=\;2-u^{4}.

Lemma 4.8

(Birational equivalence)
.

The map
\rho
of
Equation

54
is a birational
isomorphism between the smooth projective spectral curve
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
and the
smooth projective model of the affine quartic
E:v^{2}=2-u^{4}
. In
particular,
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong E
over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

The inverse map
E\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
is given by
\rho^{-1}(u,v)=(u,u(u^{2}+v)/2)
.
We verify
P(u,u(u^{2}+v)/2)=0
on
E
. Substituting

\lambda=u(u^{2}+v)/2
into
P(u;\lambda)
:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=u^{6}-u^{2}-2\cdot u^{3}\cdot\tfrac{u(u^{2}+v)}{2}+2\cdot\tfrac{u^{2}(u^{2}+v)^{2}}{4}

\displaystyle=u^{6}-u^{2}-u^{4}(u^{2}+v)+\tfrac{u^{2}}{2}(u^{2}+v)^{2}

\displaystyle=u^{6}-u^{2}-u^{6}-u^{4}v+\tfrac{u^{2}}{2}(u^{4}+2u^{2}v+v^{2}).

Using
v^{2}=2-u^{4}
from
Equation

55
:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=-u^{2}-u^{4}v+\tfrac{u^{6}}{2}+u^{4}v+\tfrac{u^{2}}{2}(2-u^{4})

\displaystyle=-u^{2}+\tfrac{u^{6}}{2}+u^{2}-\tfrac{u^{6}}{2}\;=\;0.

Both directions of the map are algebraic and well-defined on a dense open
subset; they are therefore birational. Birational isomorphism of smooth
projective curves is isomorphism, so
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong E
over
\mathbb{Q}
.
∎

The quartic
Equation

55
is the working model on which the

j
-invariant is most directly computed.

4.9.2.
Theorem 4.1: the
j
-invariant is
1728

We now establish the central identification of this chapter, the master
inventory’s Theorem C.

Theorem 4.9

(
j
-invariant;
Theorem C of the master inventory
)
.

The smooth projective curve
E:v^{2}=2-u^{4}
of
Equation

55
,
and therefore the spectral curve
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
of the canonical two-mode toy of

Definition

2.2
, has
j
-invariant

(56)

j(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}})\;=\;j(E)\;=\;1728.

Moreover,
E
has complex multiplication by the ring of Gaussian integers

\mathbb{Z}[i]
.

We give two independent proofs.

Proof 1 (order-four automorphism).

The curve
E
admits the automorphism

(57)

\sigma:E\;\to\;E,\qquad(u,v)\;\longmapsto\;(i\,u,\;v),\qquad i^{2}=-1.

We verify
\sigma
preserves
E
:

\sigma^{*}(v^{2}-2+u^{4})\;=\;v^{2}-2+(iu)^{4}\;=\;v^{2}-2+u^{4},

which vanishes on
E
. The order of
\sigma
is four since

\sigma^{4}(u,v)=(i^{4}u,v)=(u,v)
and
\sigma^{k}
for
k=1,2,3
is
not the identity (it sends
u
to
i^{k}u\neq u
for
u\neq 0
).
The automorphism
\sigma
itself has no fixed branch point. For the
elliptic-curve automorphism argument we use instead the order-four
automorphism

\widetilde{\sigma}:(u,v)\longmapsto(iu,-v).

It preserves
E
by the same calculation. In the local coordinates

t=1/u
and
w=v/u^{2}
at infinity, the projective closure is

w^{2}=2t^{4}-1
, and
\widetilde{\sigma}
acts by

t\longmapsto-it,\qquad w\longmapsto w.

Thus both points at infinity are fixed. Taking one of them as the
identity
O
, the automorphism
\widetilde{\sigma}
lies in
\operatorname{Aut}(E,O)

and has order four.
(Proof 2 below independently verifies
j=1728
via the Legendre
cross-ratio.) By the classification of finite automorphism groups of
elliptic curves
[
18
, Theorem III.10.1]
, an
elliptic curve
E/\mathbb{C}
has automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}(E)\supseteq\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}

if and only if
j(E)=1728
, in which case
E
has complex multiplication
by the Gaussian integers
\mathbb{Z}[i]
. The order-four automorphism
\widetilde{\sigma}

therefore certifies both
j(E)=1728
and
\operatorname{CM}(E)=\mathbb{Z}[i]
.
∎

Proof 2 (Legendre cross-ratio of the branch points).

The branch points of
E
over the
u
-line are the four solutions of

v=0
, i.e.
u^{4}=2
:

p_{1}\;=\;2^{1/4},\qquad p_{2}\;=\;-\,2^{1/4},\qquad p_{3}\;=\;i\cdot 2^{1/4},\qquad p_{4}\;=\;-\,i\cdot 2^{1/4}.

Writing
\alpha:=2^{1/4}
, the cross-ratio is

\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}\;:=\;\frac{(p_{1}-p_{3})(p_{2}-p_{4})}{(p_{1}-p_{4})(p_{2}-p_{3})}\;=\;\frac{\alpha(1-i)\cdot(-\alpha)(1-i)}{\alpha(1+i)\cdot(-\alpha)(1+i)}\;=\;\frac{(1-i)^{2}}{(1+i)^{2}}\;=\;\frac{-2i}{2i}\;=\;-1.

The
j
-invariant in terms of the Legendre cross-ratio

[
18
, §III.1.7]
is

(58)

j(\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}})\;=\;256\cdot\frac{(\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}^{2}-\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}+1)^{3}}{\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}^{2}(\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}-1)^{2}}.

At
\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}=-1
the numerator factor is

\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}^{2}-\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}+1=1+1+1=3
, cubed yields

27
; the denominator is

\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}^{2}\cdot(\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}-1)^{2}=1\cdot 4=4
.
Therefore

j(E)\;=\;256\cdot\frac{27}{4}\;=\;64\cdot 27\;=\;1728.\qed

The two proofs are independent and agree.

Remark 4.10

(CM by
\mathbb{Z}[i]
)
.

The fixed-point order-four automorphism
\widetilde{\sigma}
above gives the
Gaussian multiplication after one of the points at infinity is chosen as
the elliptic origin. Since
j(E)=1728
in characteristic zero, the
corresponding endomorphism ring is
\mathrm{End}(E)=\mathbb{Z}[i]
, the maximal
order in the imaginary quadratic field
\mathbb{Q}(i)
. This is the CM data that
drives the explicit
a_{p}
-formula recorded in
Section

4.9.4
.

4.9.3.
The
\mathbb{Q}
-model of the spectral curve

The birational equivalence
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong E
of
Lemma

4.8

identifies the spectral curve with the smooth projective model of the
affine quartic
E:v^{2}=2-u^{4}
over
\mathbb{Q}
. The
j
-invariant
computation (
Section

4.9.2
) gives
j(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}})=1728
, which
determines the
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-isomorphism class. To determine the curve
over
\mathbb{Q}
—and hence its conductor and
L
-function—we need the
Weierstrass model of
v^{2}=2-u^{4}
over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proposition 4.11

(Weierstrass model and LMFDB identification)
.

The smooth projective model of the affine quartic
E:v^{2}=2-u^{4}

is isomorphic over
\mathbb{Q}
to the elliptic curve

(59)

E_{0}:\;\;y^{2}\;=\;x^{3}+8x,

of conductor
N(E_{0})=256=2^{8}
, Cremona label
256b2
, and
LMFDB label
256b2

[
21
]
. The spectral curve

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
of the canonical two-mode toy is therefore
E_{0}
.

Proof.

The quartic
v^{2}=2-u^{4}
has the
\mathbb{Q}
-rational point
(u,v)=(1,1)
.
For a binary quartic

f(X,Z)=aX^{4}+4bX^{3}Z+6cX^{2}Z^{2}+4dXZ^{3}+eZ^{4}

the classical invariants give the Jacobian in the form

Y^{2}=X^{3}-27IX-27J,\qquad I=12ae-3bd+c^{2},\qquad J=72ace+9bcd-27ad^{2}-27b^{2}e-2c^{3}.

For
v^{2}=2-u^{4}
, the corresponding quartic is

f(X,Z)=-X^{4}+2Z^{4}
, so

I=-24,\qquad J=0.

Its Jacobian is therefore

Y^{2}=X^{3}+648X.

The change of variables
X=9x
,
Y=27y
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and gives

27^{2}y^{2}=9^{3}x^{3}+648\cdot 9x,\qquad\text{hence}\qquad y^{2}=x^{3}+8x.

Thus the Jacobian of the quartic is
E_{0}
over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Since the quartic has the rational point
(1,1)
, it is a genus-one
curve with a chosen
\mathbb{Q}
-point and is therefore isomorphic over
\mathbb{Q}
to
its Jacobian. Combining this with the birational equivalence

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong E
from
Lemma

4.8
identifies the
canonical spectral curve itself with
E_{0}
over
\mathbb{Q}
. The conductor,
Cremona label, and LMFDB label are those of the minimal model

y^{2}=x^{3}+8x
, namely
256b2
.
∎

Remark 4.12

(The canonical 2-mode toy lands on a specific CM curve)
.

Proposition

4.11
replaces the abstract genus-one statement of

Theorem

4.9
with a concrete arithmetic identification: the
canonical two-mode toy realises the elliptic curve
E_{0}=
LMFDB

256b2
(
y^{2}=x^{3}+8x
), with CM by
\mathbb{Z}[i]
, conductor
256
, and
discriminant
2^{9}
. The curve
E_{0}
shares the same
j
-invariant
1728

and the same CM field
\mathbb{Q}(i)
as the curve
32.a3
(
y^{2}=x^{3}-x
),
but is not a quadratic twist of it (Sage returns

is_quadratic_twist
=
0
) and has a different
a_{p}
-sequence
(the correct values are tabulated in
Example

4.15
below).
Modular form data, point counts, and the local
L
-factors below are all
verifiable from the LMFDB entry
256b2

[
21
]
; this is
the hook by which the rest of the manuscript becomes computational.

4.9.4.
The attached
L
-function

Modularity (free for CM curves)

By a classical theorem of Deuring
[
10
]
(refined and unified
with the general modularity theorem of
[
25
,
19
,
6
]
), every elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
with complex multiplication is
modular. The attached modular form of
E_{0}
is

(60)

f_{E_{0}}\;\in\;S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(256)),

the unique normalised cuspidal Hecke eigenform of weight two and level
256

with the prescribed eigenvalues, corresponding to LMFDB curve
256b2

[
21
]
. Concretely,
f_{E_{0}}
has Fourier expansion

f_{E_{0}}(\tau)\;=\;\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}\,q^{n},\qquad q=e^{2\pi i\tau},

with
a_{1}=1
and
a_{n}
multiplicative; the prime values
a_{p}
are
recorded in
Example

4.15
below.

The Hecke Größencharakter on
\mathbb{Q}(i)

For a CM elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
with CM by an order in an imaginary
quadratic field
K
, the modular form
f_{E_{0}}
admits a second description
as the theta lift of a Hecke Größencharakter on the idele class group
of
K

[
17
, §II.10]
. In the case at hand,
K=\mathbb{Q}(i)
and

E_{0}
(LMFDB
256b2
) is one of the CM curves with
j=1728
.
The relevant Größencharakter
\psi_{E_{0}}
has infinity type
(1,0)
:
for a split prime
p=\mathfrak{p}\bar{\mathfrak{p}}
in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
, the
local factor of
L(E_{0},s)
at
p
is
(1-a_{p}p^{-s}+p^{1-2s})^{-1}

with

a_{p}\;=\;\psi_{E_{0}}(\mathfrak{p})+\psi_{E_{0}}(\bar{\mathfrak{p}}),

where
\psi_{E_{0}}(\mathfrak{p})
is a generator
\pi_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}[i]
of

\mathfrak{p}
(up to a unit) whose norm is
p
.
For
p=5
, the generator is
\pi_{5}=-2+i
and
a_{5}=-4
;
for
p=13
,
\pi_{13}=-2+3i
and
a_{13}=-4
.
The
L
-function therefore coincides with the Hecke
L
-function

L(E_{0},s)\;=\;L(\psi_{E_{0}},s).

This description —
L(E_{0},s)
as a Hecke L-function on the

quadratic
field
\mathbb{Q}(i)
— is exactly the input that gates the
Langlands degree ladder of
Section

4.9.5
: descending from

L(E_{0},s)
to a Dirichlet
L
-function would require a
\mathrm{GL}_{2}\to\mathrm{GL}_{1}

automorphic descent which, for a CM curve, would amount to factoring the
Hecke character
\psi_{E_{0}}
through the norm
N_{K/\mathbb{Q}}
. No such
factorisation exists because
\psi_{E_{0}}
has non-trivial infinity type.

Local Euler factors

The
L
-function of
E_{0}
admits the Euler product

(61)

L(E_{0},s)\;=\;\prod_{p}L_{p}(E_{0},s),

with the local factors specified as follows.

Definition 4.13

(Good and bad primes)
.

A prime
p
is
good
for
E_{0}
if
p\nmid N(E_{0})=256
, i.e.
p\neq 2
. The prime
p=2
is
bad
(in fact,
E_{0}
has additive
reduction at
2
).

Proposition 4.14

(Local Euler factors)
.

At a good prime
p
, the local
L
-factor of
E_{0}
is the degree-two
polynomial

(62)

L_{p}(E_{0},s)\;=\;\bigl(1-a_{p}\,p^{-s}+p\cdot p^{-2s}\bigr)^{-1},

where the Frobenius trace
a_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}
is determined by the CM action of

\mathbb{Z}[i]
:

(63)

a_{p}\;=\;\begin{cases}2\,\operatorname{Re}(\pi_{p})&\text{if }p\equiv 1\pmod{4}\text{, with }p=\pi_{p}\bar{\pi}_{p}\text{ in }\mathbb{Z}[i]\text{ (split)},\\
0&\text{if }p\equiv 3\pmod{4}\text{ (inert)},\end{cases}

where
\pi_{p}
is the generator of the prime ideal

\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathbb{Z}[i]
of norm
p
determined by the Hecke character

\psi_{E_{0}}
(§
4.9.4
). At the bad prime

p=2
, the local factor is
L_{2}(E_{0},s)=1
, corresponding to the
additive reduction of
E_{0}
at
2
.

Proof.

The split/inert dichotomy at
p\neq 2
for a CM elliptic curve with
endomorphism ring
\mathbb{Z}[i]
is the standard theorem of Deuring

[
10
]
; see also
[
17
, §II.10]
for a modern
exposition. The split case
p\equiv 1\pmod{4}
factors as

p=\pi_{p}\bar{\pi}_{p}
in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
, where the generator
\pi_{p}
is
selected by the Hecke character
\psi_{E_{0}}
as described in
§
4.9.4
. Then
a_{p}=\psi_{E_{0}}(\pi_{p})+\psi_{E_{0}}(\bar{\pi}_{p})=2\,\operatorname{Re}(\pi_{p})
. Explicitly, for
p=5
the
Hecke character picks
\pi_{5}=-2+i
(
\operatorname{Re}=-2
) rather than its
associate
1+2i
, giving
a_{5}=-4
; for
p=13
it picks

\pi_{13}=-2+3i
(
\operatorname{Re}=-2
), giving
a_{13}=-4
.
The inert case
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
has no factorisation
of
p
in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
and forces
a_{p}=0
by the conjugation symmetry of the
Hecke Größencharakter. The additive reduction
at
p=2
is read off from the conductor exponent

\mathrm{ord}_{2}(N(E_{0}))=8>1
and Tate’s algorithm; consequently

L_{2}(E_{0},s)=1

[
17
, §IV.10]
.
∎

Example 4.15

(Numerical values from SageMath verification)
.

The first few Frobenius traces of
E_{0}
(LMFDB
256b2
), verified
by both point-counting on
y^{2}=x^{3}+8x
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and SageMath 10.9,
are

p

3

5

7

11

13

17

19

23

a_{p}

0

-4

0

0

-4

-2

0

0

p

29

31

37

41

43

47

a_{p}

-4

0

12

-10

0

0

The vanishing of
a_{p}
at every
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
(inert primes:

3,7,11,19,23,31,43,47,\ldots
) is the CM signature predicted by

Proposition

4.14
; the non-zero values at split primes
(
5,13,17,29,37,41,\ldots
) admit the closed-form

a_{p}=2\,\operatorname{Re}(\pi_{p})
with the specific generator
\pi_{p}
selected by

\psi_{E_{0}}
. For example,
p=5
factors as
5=(2+i)(2-i)
in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
;
the Hecke character selects
\pi_{5}=-2+i
(rather than its associate

2+i
), giving
\operatorname{Re}(\pi_{5})=-2
and
a_{5}=-4
; for
p=13
,

13=(2+3i)(2-3i)
selects
\pi_{13}=-2+3i
, giving

\operatorname{Re}(\pi_{13})=-2
and
a_{13}=-4
. All values are confirmed by
direct point-counting on
y^{2}=x^{3}+8x
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and by
SageMath 10.9.

Propositions

4.14
and
4.15
supply the input
data for
Section

4.10
: the closed-form Euler-factor matching of

Theorem

4.2
’s resolvent against
L_{p}(E_{0},s)
at every good
prime, via the off-shell basepoint family

u_{p}^{2}=(a_{p}\pm\sqrt{4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}})/(2p)
.

Frobenius eigenvalues and the Hasse bound

For each good prime
p
, the local
L
-factor of
Equation

62

factors as

(64)

1-a_{p}\,p^{-s}+p\,p^{-2s}\;=\;(1-\alpha_{p}\,p^{-s})(1-\beta_{p}\,p^{-s}),

with Frobenius eigenvalues
\alpha_{p},\beta_{p}
satisfying

\alpha_{p}+\beta_{p}\;=\;a_{p},\qquad\alpha_{p}\,\beta_{p}\;=\;p.

By the Hasse bound
[
18
, §V.1]
(which is the case-of-genus-one
Weil conjecture, originally due to Hasse in 1933 for elliptic curves),

(65)

|a_{p}|\;\leq\;2\sqrt{p},

the discriminant of
Equation

64
satisfies

a_{p}^{2}-4p\leq 0
, so
\alpha_{p},\beta_{p}
are complex conjugate of
absolute value
\sqrt{p}
. Equivalently, the Frobenius eigenvalues lie
on the circle
|w|=\sqrt{p}
in
\mathbb{C}
, and admit the parametrisation

\alpha_{p}=\sqrt{p}\,e^{i\theta_{p}}
,
\beta_{p}=\sqrt{p}\,e^{-i\theta_{p}}

with
\cos\theta_{p}=a_{p}/(2\sqrt{p})\in[-1,1]
.

Equation

65
is the arithmetic input that, in

Section

4.10
, will ensure that the discriminant

\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}
of the off-shell basepoint quadratic is
strictly positive at every good prime, hence that the basepoint

u_{p}^{2}
is real. In short: the Hasse bound for
E_{0}
controls
when the operator-pencil matching is real, and Weil’s RH for elliptic
curves controls the locus on which the matching exists.

Remark 4.16

(Sato–Tate distribution)
.

For non-CM elliptic curves the angles
\theta_{p}
of
\alpha_{p}=\sqrt{p}\,e^{i\theta_{p}}
equidistribute with respect to the Sato–Tate measure

(2/\pi)\sin^{2}\theta\,d\theta
on
[0,\pi]
. For a CM curve such as
E_{0}

this is
not
the case: at inert primes
\theta_{p}=\pi/2

deterministically, and at split primes the distribution is the uniform
measure on the unit circle pushed down to
[0,\pi]
. The bimodal
inert/split structure of
Proposition

4.14
is the
fingerprint of CM and will surface in the off-shell matching of

Section

4.10
as a basepoint that lies on the rational curve

\lambda=u^{3}
at inert primes (where
a_{p}=0
forces

\lambda_{p}=u_{p}^{3}-0=u_{p}^{3}
) and on a more general algebraic locus at
split primes.

4.9.5.
Scope

Theorems

4.9
and
4.11
establish that the
canonical two-mode operator class of
Section

2
realises a
specific elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
— namely
E_{0}
= LMFDB

256b2
— and consequently has, attached to it, a specific
degree-two
L
-function
L(E_{0},s)
. We close this chapter with a precise
statement of what this identification does, and does not, claim.

What is claimed.

The construction supplies a closed-form local Euler-factor correspondence
at the level of
L(E_{0},s)
: for every good prime
p
, the characteristic
polynomial of the
2\times 2
resolvent at an explicit algebraic basepoint
reproduces the local factor
L_{p}(E_{0},s)^{-1}
identically in
s

(
Section

4.10
). The construction of
Section

4
together
with the identification of this chapter supplies a Pontryagin-space pencil
whose spectral curve is
E_{0}
and whose resolvent local data is shown in

Section

4.10
to match the local Euler factors of
L(E_{0},s)
at
every good prime. This is a substantive arithmetic-geometric statement:
the
\eta
-non-degenerate operator pencil of
Definition

2.2
sits
naturally in the L-function geometry of a specific CM curve.

What is not claimed.

The L-function
L(E_{0},s)
is
not
the Riemann zeta function

\zeta(s)
. Indeed,
L(E_{0},s)
has degree two as an Euler product
(local factors
L_{p}(E_{0},s)^{-1}
are degree-two polynomials in
p^{-s}
;
see
Equation

62
), whereas
\zeta(s)=\prod_{p}(1-p^{-s})^{-1}
has degree one (local factors are degree-one polynomials).
The path from elliptic
L
-functions (degree two) to
\zeta(s)
(degree
one) is governed by the Langlands degree ladder: it would require a

descent
from a
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
automorphic
L
-function to a
\mathrm{GL}_{1}

Dirichlet
L
-function, which is not a free operation. In the
operator-pencil framework of this manuscript, that descent is structurally
barred — the obstructions are catalogued in

Section

10
as five closed-form obstruction lemmas,
each pinning down an explicit reason why a candidate descent path fails.

What this chapter does provide.

The identification
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong E_{0}
over
\mathbb{Q}
together with the
modularity / Hecke-Größencharakter data of

Proposition

4.14
make
L(E_{0},s)
available for the
detailed off-shell Euler matching of
Section

4.10
. The headline
of the manuscript is therefore: the operator pencil supplies a closed-form
local Euler-factor correspondence for
L(E_{0},s)
, with every local Euler
factor reproduced exactly by the resolvent characteristic polynomial at an
explicit algebraic basepoint, and with the Hasse bound for
E_{0}
implying
the existence of real basepoints (
Section

4.10
). This is, to our
knowledge, the first construction with this property for any specific CM
elliptic L-function; the comparison with prior partial precedents
(Berry–Keating, Bender–Brody–Müller, Bellissard, Moxley) is deferred
to
Section

9
.

In summary, the structural sequence is

\displaystyle\text{RSCO pencil}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\text{ (genus 1)}\longrightarrow j=1728,

\displaystyle\longrightarrow E_{0}=\text{{256b2}}\longrightarrow L(E_{0},s).

Remark 4.17

(Origin of
j=1728
)
.

The value
j=1728=12^{3}
is not a numerical coincidence.
The canonical toy parameters
(\tau,\delta,\Delta)=(2,0,2)
produce a

\mathbb{Z}_{4}
automorphism of the spectral curve

(u,\lambda)\mapsto(iu,-i\lambda)
,
inherited from the
J
-self-adjointness condition
JV^{*}J=V
at these specific
parameters.
Over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
, the only elliptic curves that support a

\mathbb{Z}_{4}
automorphism are those with
j=1728
(the
\mathbb{Z}[i]
-CM
locus). The
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry of the pencil forces the spectral curve
onto this CM point;
j=1728
is not an accident but a consequence of the
discrete symmetry encoded in
V
.

Remark 4.18

(CM field and the inert/split dichotomy)
.

The CM field
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D_{p}})
generated by the Frobenius discriminant

D_{p}=a_{p}^{2}-4p
and the field
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}})
generated by
the basepoint discriminant
\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}
are different for every
good prime
p\neq 2
of
E_{0}
; their compositum

\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}},\sqrt{D_{p}})
is a degree-4 extension.
For inert primes (
a_{p}=0
),
\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)
and
D_{p}=-4p
;
for split primes (
a_{p}\neq 0
),
D_{p}=a_{p}^{2}-4p\leq 0
(strictly negative
except at the Hasse–Weil boundary
|a_{p}|=2\sqrt{p}
, which is not
populated by any odd good prime of
E_{0}
).
The specialised resolvent and the Frobenius action therefore live over
different quadratic fields, while their characteristic polynomials agree
after the Euler-factor matching of Section
4.10
.
This arithmetic dichotomy is a direct imprint of the class field theory
of
\mathbb{Q}(i)
.

The next chapter extracts the local Euler-factor matching from this
sequence in closed form.

4.10.
Closed-form Euler matching at good primes

This section contains the headline computational result of the paper.
For every good prime
p\neq 2
of
E_{0}=
LMFDB
256b2
, we
exhibit an
explicit, off-shell, closed-form
basepoint

(w_{p}^{+},\lambda_{p}^{+})
with
w_{p}^{+}>0
(giving two real basepoints
\pm\sqrt{w_{p}^{+}}
) at which the
characteristic polynomial of the
2
-mode resolvent reproduces the
local Euler factor of
L(E_{0},s)

exactly
, as polynomials in

p^{-s}
. The reality of the basepoint at every prime is governed by a
quadratic discriminant

\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}
, which is positive at every prime

because
the Hasse bound
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
holds.
The implication is one-way, with
\sqrt{p}
-order slack: the
operator-side reality condition
a_{p}^{2}\leq 4p(p+1)
is strictly
weaker than the Hasse-Weil bound
a_{p}^{2}\leq 4p
. We say that the
local Riemann hypothesis (Weil’s theorem on the Riemann hypothesis
for elliptic curves over finite fields) is
consumed as
input
; it is not derived as a consequence of the operator
construction.

What is and is not structural in the matching.

The
matching system is, at a counting level, two scalar equations in
two unknowns (§
4.10.2
); generic

2
-equation-
2
-unknown systems are solvable, so the bare
existence of a basepoint is not by itself the structural content.
The structural content of
Theorem

4.24
is rather the
conjunction of three facts about the solution: (i) the basepoint

(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
is
real
, which is the one-way Hasse-Weil
input above; (ii) the basepoint is
algebraic
over
\mathbb{Q}
in a
controlled extension — the natural
p
-torsion / CM field of

E_{0}
— so that the Frobenius-trace identity
\mathrm{tr}R=a_{p}
has
arithmetic meaning beyond a numerical coincidence; (iii) the
elliptic curve identified on the operator side (the spectral curve

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong E_{0}
of Section
4.9
, by an independent

j
-invariant computation) is
the same
elliptic curve whose
local L-factor appears on the right-hand side of the matching
identity. Theorem E and the spectral-curve identification of

Theorem

4.9
are both consequences of the same
underlying polynomial
P(u,\lambda)
of
Equation

2
;
their
compatibility
— that the
L
-function appearing in
(ii) is attached to the curve identified in (iii) — is the
load-bearing structural surprise, independent of either fact taken
in isolation.

The construction is local (one prime at a time), not global: there is
no single basepoint
(u_{\ast},\lambda_{\ast})
that recovers the full

L
-function in one stroke, and we explain in
Section
4.10.8
why no such global basepoint can exist in the
finite-dimensional setting. The global
L
-function appears here only
as an infinite product over primes of finite-dimensional determinants
at
p
-dependent basepoints.

Remark 4.19

(Joint-consequence structure — and its explanation)
.

The elliptic-curve identification
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong E_{0}
of

Theorem

4.9
and the closed-form Euler matching
of
Theorem

4.24
below are
not
linked by a logical chain
in their proofs. The identification is established by computing the

j
-invariant of
P(u,\lambda)
in closed form; the Euler matching is
established by computing
\det R
,
\mathrm{tr}R
at the off-shell basepoint
and solving a quadratic in
u^{2}
. Each step uses only the explicit
form of
P(u,\lambda)
and the resolvent algebra; neither step invokes
the other as a hypothesis.

The
reason
they point to the same curve is a structural
identity of discriminants developed in
Section
4.10.10
below: the basepoint discriminant

\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{\,2}
(emerging from the matching system)
and the Frobenius discriminant
D_{p}=a_{p}^{\,2}-4p
(emerging from
the arithmetic of
E_{0}
) satisfy
\Delta_{p}+D_{p}=4p^{2}
exactly.
Both discriminants are constructed from the same arithmetic data

(a_{p},p)
, and the identity linking them is the algebraic mechanism
that forces the operator-side construction and the arithmetic-side
construction to land on the same elliptic curve. The structural
content of the headline correspondence is not that two independent
computations happen to agree—it is that they
must
agree,
because they process the same input through two channels linked by
a closed-form identity.

The arrangement of the section is as follows.
Section
4.10.1
recalls the operator pencil from
Section
2
and the elliptic-curve identification
from Section
4.9
, and writes down the matching question
in its sharpest form.
Section
4.10.2
derives the closed-form trace and
determinant of the resolvent at an off-shell basepoint and reduces the
matching question to two scalar equations in two unknowns.
Section
4.10.3
solves this system, reducing it to a
quadratic in
w=u^{2}
with rational coefficients in
a_{p}
and
p
.
Section
4.10.4
states and proves the main theorem
of the section. Section
4.10.5
discusses the
off-shell geometry of the basepoint family. Section
4.10.6

verifies the theorem in closed form at
p=3
and
p=5
.
Section
4.10.7
records the branch and sign conventions.
Section
4.10.8
delineates precisely what the theorem does
and does not claim. Section
4.10.9
reflects on
the significance of the Hasse-bound coincidence as a structural
feature.

4.10.1.
Setup recall

We work with the
2
-mode operator pencil of Section
2
,

(66)

A(z;\lambda)\;=\;z\,\mathbbm{1}_{2}\,-\,L_{S}\,-\,\lambda\,z^{-1/2}\,V,\qquad L_{S}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad V=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad J=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},

on the Krein space
(\mathbb{C}^{2},J)
of signature
(1,1)
. The branch

z^{-1/2}
is principal: cut along
\mathbb{R}_{<0}
, with
z^{-1/2}\bigl|_{z=1}=1
.
Write
u:=z^{1/2}
, so
\operatorname{Re}u>0
on the principal sheet. The
resolvent is

(67)

R(z;\lambda)\;:=\;A(z;\lambda)^{-1}\;=\;\frac{\mathrm{adj}A(z;\lambda)}{\det A(z;\lambda)}.

By the calculation of Section
4
, one has the explicit expressions

(68)

\displaystyle\det A(z;\lambda)

\displaystyle\;=\;z^{2}-1-2\lambda\,z^{1/2}+2\lambda^{2}\,z^{-1},

(69)

\displaystyle\mathrm{adj}A(z;\lambda)

\displaystyle\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}z+1-\lambda z^{-1/2}&+\lambda z^{-1/2}\\
-\lambda z^{-1/2}&z-1-\lambda z^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}.

It is convenient to clear fractional powers by multiplying through by

u^{2}=z
. Define

(70)

\boxed{\;P(u;\lambda)\;:=\;u^{2}\det A(u^{2};\lambda)\;=\;u^{6}\,-\,u^{2}\,-\,2\lambda\,u^{3}\,+\,2\lambda^{2}.\;}

The locus
\{P(u;\lambda)=0\}
is the affine spectral curve, whose
smooth projective model
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
is the elliptic curve
E_{0}:y^{2}=x^{3}+8x

by Section
4.9
(Theorem C).
For later use we record

(71)

R(u^{2};\lambda)\;=\;\frac{1}{P(u;\lambda)}\begin{pmatrix}u^{4}+u^{2}-\lambda u&+\lambda u\\
-\lambda u&u^{4}-u^{2}-\lambda u\end{pmatrix},

obtained by multiplying numerator and denominator of
(
67
) by
u^{2}
and using (
70
).

The elliptic curve
E_{0}
has complex multiplication by
\mathbb{Z}[i]
,
conductor
256
, and corresponds to LMFDB label
256b2

[
21
]
. Its Hasse–Weil
L
-function admits the Euler
product

(72)

L(E_{0},s)\;=\;\prod_{p}L_{p}(E_{0},s),\qquad L_{p}(E_{0},s)\;=\;\bigl(1-a_{p}\,p^{-s}+\varepsilon_{p}\,p^{1-2s}\bigr)^{-1},

with
\varepsilon_{p}=1
for
p\neq 2
and
\varepsilon_{2}=0
; the Fourier
coefficients
a_{p}
are those of the weight-
2
newform

f_{E_{0}}\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(256))
, LMFDB label modular form of
256b2

[
21
,
10
]
. By Grothendieck–Lefschetz applied to

\mathrm{Frob}_{p}
on
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})
for any
\ell\neq p

[
9
]
,
the local factor at every good prime
p\neq 2
equals

(73)

L_{p}(E_{0},s)^{-1}\;=\;\det\bigl(I_{2}-p^{-s}\mathrm{Frob}_{p}\big|_{H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})}\bigr)\;=\;1-a_{p}\,p^{-s}+p\cdot p^{-2s},

which is a polynomial of degree exactly
2
in
p^{-s}
. The
characteristic polynomial of
\mathrm{Frob}_{p}
on
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})
is

T^{2}-a_{p}T+p
.

The matching question.

The space
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})
is two-dimensional, and our resolvent

R(z;\lambda)
acts on the two-dimensional fibre
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. This dimensional
coincidence motivates the matching question. For each good prime

p\neq 2
, can one find
(z_{p},\lambda_{p})
such that

(74)

\det\bigl(I_{2}\,-\,p^{-s}\,R(z_{p};\lambda_{p})\bigr)\;=\;L_{p}(E_{0},s)^{-1}\;=\;1-a_{p}\,p^{-s}+p^{1-2s}

holds
exactly
as polynomials in the variable
\tau:=p^{-s}
?
Note that both sides are polynomials of degree
2
in
\tau
, so the
question is whether their three coefficients (in degrees
0
,
1
,
2
)
agree. The constant term is
1
on both sides automatically.

Remark 4.20
.

The matching question (
74
) is the

eigenvalue-level
version of the conjecture that
R(z_{p};\lambda_{p})
,
up to a rational rescaling, is conjugate over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
to the
geometric Frobenius
\mathrm{Frob}_{p}
on
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})
. The latter, full

\mathrm{GL}_{2}
conjugacy, is strictly stronger; we do not address it here.
The eigenvalue match alone suffices for the Euler factor identity, and
this is the content of the theorem below.

4.10.2.
The matching system

Since
R
is a
2\times 2
matrix, the spectral determinant expands
as

(75)

\det(I_{2}-\tau R)\;=\;1-\tau\,\mathrm{tr}R+\tau^{2}\,\det R,\qquad\tau\in\mathbb{C}.

Setting
\tau=p^{-s}
and comparing coefficients in
(
74
) with the right-hand side

1-a_{p}\,p^{-s}+p\cdot p^{-2s}
, we obtain the

matching system

(76)

\boxed{\;\mathrm{tr}R(z_{p};\lambda_{p})\;=\;a_{p},\qquad\det R(z_{p};\lambda_{p})\;=\;p.\;}

This is a system of two scalar equations in two unknowns

(z_{p},\lambda_{p})
, or, after the substitution
z=u^{2}
, in

(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
. We now compute the trace and determinant of
R
in
closed form and reduce (
76
) to a polynomial
system over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Lemma 4.21

(Closed-form trace and determinant of the resolvent)
.

For any
(u,\lambda)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
with
P(u;\lambda)\neq 0
,

(77)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}R(u^{2};\lambda)

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{2u(u^{3}-\lambda)}{P(u;\lambda)},

(78)

\displaystyle\det R(u^{2};\lambda)

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{u^{2}}{P(u;\lambda)}.

Proof.

From (
71
), the trace is

\mathrm{tr}R(u^{2};\lambda)\;=\;\frac{(u^{4}+u^{2}-\lambda u)+(u^{4}-u^{2}-\lambda u)}{P(u;\lambda)}\;=\;\frac{2u^{4}-2\lambda u}{P(u;\lambda)}\;=\;\frac{2u(u^{3}-\lambda)}{P(u;\lambda)},

which is (
77
).

For the determinant: the
(1,1)
and
(2,2)
entries of the matrix
in (
71
) are
u^{4}+u^{2}-\lambda u
and

u^{4}-u^{2}-\lambda u
, whose product is

(u^{4}-\lambda u)^{2}-(u^{2})^{2}\;=\;u^{8}-2\lambda u^{5}+\lambda^{2}u^{2}-u^{4}.

The
(1,2)
and
(2,1)
entries are
+\lambda u
and
-\lambda u
, with
product
-\lambda^{2}u^{2}
. Therefore

\displaystyle P(u;\lambda)^{2}\det R(u^{2};\lambda)

\displaystyle\;=\;(u^{4}+u^{2}-\lambda u)(u^{4}-u^{2}-\lambda u)-(\lambda u)(-\lambda u)

\displaystyle\;=\;u^{8}-2\lambda u^{5}+\lambda^{2}u^{2}-u^{4}+\lambda^{2}u^{2}

\displaystyle\;=\;u^{8}-u^{4}-2\lambda u^{5}+2\lambda^{2}u^{2}

\displaystyle\;=\;u^{2}\,\bigl(u^{6}-u^{2}-2\lambda u^{3}+2\lambda^{2}\bigr)\;=\;u^{2}\,P(u;\lambda).

Hence
\det R(u^{2};\lambda)=u^{2}/P(u;\lambda)
, which is
(
78
).

Independently,
\det R=1/\det A
, and

\det A(u^{2};\lambda)=P(u;\lambda)/u^{2}
by (
70
), giving the
same answer. This is the consistency check between the matrix-entry
computation and the direct
\det
-of-inverse computation.
∎

Corollary 4.22

(Spectral determinant of
R
at an off-shell basepoint)
.

For any
(u_{0},\lambda_{0})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
with
P(u_{0};\lambda_{0})\neq 0
, the
spectral determinant of the resolvent at the basepoint
(u_{0},\lambda_{0})

is the explicit quadratic

(79)

D(\tau;u_{0},\lambda_{0})\;:=\;\det\bigl(I_{2}-\tau R(u_{0}^{2};\lambda_{0})\bigr)\;=\;1\,-\,\tau\,\frac{2u_{0}(u_{0}^{3}-\lambda_{0})}{P(u_{0};\lambda_{0})}\,+\,\tau^{2}\,\frac{u_{0}^{2}}{P(u_{0};\lambda_{0})}.

Proof.

Combine (
75
) with
Lemma
4.21
.
∎

The polynomial
D(\tau;u_{0},\lambda_{0})
in (
79
) is the

finite-dimensional
spectral determinant of
R
at a single
basepoint
(u_{0},\lambda_{0})
. Its degree in
\tau
is exactly
2
,
matching the degree of
L_{p}(E_{0},s)^{-1}
as a polynomial in
p^{-s}
.

Restatement of the matching system.

Substituting (
77
)–(
78
) into
(
76
) yields

(80)

\boxed{\;\frac{2u_{p}(u_{p}^{3}-\lambda_{p})}{P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})}\;=\;a_{p},\qquad\frac{u_{p}^{2}}{P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})}\;=\;p.\;}

This is a system of two polynomial equations in the two unknowns

(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
. The denominator
P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})
appears in both
equations, so the system is equivalent to two polynomial identities
after clearing
P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})
.

Remark 4.23
.

The second equation of (
80
) immediately
implies
P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})=u_{p}^{2}/p\neq 0
whenever
u_{p}\neq 0
.
Hence any solution
(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
of the matching system is

off-shell
: it does
not
lie on the spectral curve

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}=\{P=0\}
. We return to this point in
Section
4.10.5
.

4.10.3.
Solving the matching system

We solve (
80
) in two steps: first eliminate

\lambda_{p}
using the trace equation, then substitute into the
determinant equation and reduce to a single polynomial equation in

u_{p}
.

Step 1: eliminate
\lambda_{p}
.

From the second equation,
P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})=u_{p}^{2}/p
. Substituting this
into the first equation gives

(81)

2u_{p}(u_{p}^{3}-\lambda_{p})\;=\;a_{p}\cdot\frac{u_{p}^{2}}{p},

which we rearrange (using
u_{p}\neq 0
, dividing by
2u_{p}
) as

(82)

\boxed{\;\lambda_{p}\;=\;u_{p}^{3}\,-\,\frac{a_{p}}{2p}\,u_{p}.\;}

Equation (
82
) expresses
\lambda_{p}
as an explicit polynomial
in
u_{p}
with rational coefficients in
a_{p}
and
p
.

Step 2: substitute into
P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})=u_{p}^{2}/p
.

We substitute (
82
) into

P(u;\lambda)=u^{6}-u^{2}-2\lambda u^{3}+2\lambda^{2}
and expand. We compute
the two intermediate expressions
\lambda_{p}u_{p}^{3}
and
\lambda_{p}^{2}

separately.

For
\lambda_{p}u_{p}^{3}
:

(83)

\displaystyle\lambda_{p}u_{p}^{3}

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{p}^{3}\cdot\Bigl(u_{p}^{3}-\frac{a_{p}}{2p}u_{p}\Bigr)\;=\;u_{p}^{6}\,-\,\frac{a_{p}}{2p}\,u_{p}^{4}.

For
\lambda_{p}^{2}
, expand the square of (
82
):

(84)

\displaystyle\lambda_{p}^{2}

\displaystyle\;=\;\Bigl(u_{p}^{3}-\frac{a_{p}}{2p}\,u_{p}\Bigr)^{2}\;=\;u_{p}^{6}\,-\,\frac{a_{p}}{p}\,u_{p}^{4}\,+\,\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{4p^{2}}\,u_{p}^{2}.

Substituting (
83
)–(
84
) into

P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})=u_{p}^{6}-u_{p}^{2}-2\lambda_{p}u_{p}^{3}+2\lambda_{p}^{2}
:

\displaystyle P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{p}^{6}-u_{p}^{2}\,-\,2\Bigl(u_{p}^{6}-\frac{a_{p}}{2p}u_{p}^{4}\Bigr)\,+\,2\Bigl(u_{p}^{6}-\frac{a_{p}}{p}u_{p}^{4}+\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{4p^{2}}u_{p}^{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{p}^{6}-u_{p}^{2}\,-\,2u_{p}^{6}+\frac{a_{p}}{p}u_{p}^{4}\,+\,2u_{p}^{6}-\frac{2a_{p}}{p}u_{p}^{4}+\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{2p^{2}}\,u_{p}^{2}

\displaystyle\;=\;(1-2+2)\,u_{p}^{6}\,+\,\Bigl(\frac{a_{p}}{p}-\frac{2a_{p}}{p}\Bigr)\,u_{p}^{4}\,+\,\Bigl(-1+\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{2p^{2}}\Bigr)\,u_{p}^{2}

(85)

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{p}^{6}\,-\,\frac{a_{p}}{p}\,u_{p}^{4}\,+\,\Bigl(\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{2p^{2}}-1\Bigr)\,u_{p}^{2}.

The matching system (
80
) requires

P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})=u_{p}^{2}/p
. Subtracting
u_{p}^{2}/p
from both sides
of (
85
) gives

(86)

u_{p}^{6}\,-\,\frac{a_{p}}{p}\,u_{p}^{4}\,+\,\Bigl(\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{2p^{2}}-1-\frac{1}{p}\Bigr)\,u_{p}^{2}\;=\;0.

Equation (
86
) is a degree-
6
polynomial in
u_{p}

which is even (it contains only even powers
u_{p}^{2},u_{p}^{4},u_{p}^{6}
).
Factor out
u_{p}^{2}
:

(87)

u_{p}^{2}\cdot\Bigl[u_{p}^{4}\,-\,\frac{a_{p}}{p}\,u_{p}^{2}\,+\,\frac{a_{p}^{2}-2p-2p^{2}}{2p^{2}}\Bigr]\;=\;0.

We discard the trivial root
u_{p}=0
, which corresponds to the
branch point already excluded by Remark
4.23
(and
which gives the singular
D=1
basepoint).
The remaining factor is a quadratic in
w_{p}:=u_{p}^{2}
:

(88)

\boxed{\;w_{p}^{2}\,-\,\frac{a_{p}}{p}\,w_{p}\,+\,\frac{a_{p}^{2}-2p-2p^{2}}{2p^{2}}\;=\;0.\;}

Step 3: solve the quadratic.

Apply the quadratic formula to (
88
):

\displaystyle w_{p}

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\,\biggl[\frac{a_{p}}{p}\pm\sqrt{\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{p^{2}}-\frac{2(a_{p}^{2}-2p-2p^{2})}{p^{2}}}\biggr]

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{1}{2p}\,\Bigl[a_{p}\pm\sqrt{a_{p}^{2}-2(a_{p}^{2}-2p-2p^{2})}\Bigr]

(89)

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{1}{2p}\,\Bigl[a_{p}\pm\sqrt{4p+4p^{2}-a_{p}^{2}}\Bigr].

The discriminant of (
88
), after the standard
rescaling by
4
, is

(90)

\Delta_{p}\;:=\;4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}.

With this notation,

(91)

\boxed{\;w_{p}^{\pm}\;=\;u_{p}^{2,\pm}\;=\;\frac{a_{p}\,\pm\,\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}}{2p},\qquad\Delta_{p}\;=\;4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}.\;}

The corresponding
\lambda_{p}^{\pm}
are given by
(
82
) with
u_{p}=u_{p}^{\pm}
and

u_{p}^{\pm,2}=w_{p}^{\pm}
:

(92)

\lambda_{p}^{\pm}\;=\;(u_{p}^{\pm})^{3}\,-\,\frac{a_{p}}{2p}\,u_{p}^{\pm}\;=\;u_{p}^{\pm}\,\Bigl(w_{p}^{\pm}-\frac{a_{p}}{2p}\Bigr).

The system (
80
) therefore reduces to the
single algebraic relation (
91
), with
\lambda_{p}^{\pm}

recovered from (
92
). Whether a real, or otherwise
useful, solution exists at each prime
p
depends on the sign of the
discriminant
\Delta_{p}
.

4.10.4.
The Hasse-bound exactness theorem

We can now state the main theorem of the section.

Theorem 4.24

(Closed-form Euler matching at good primes)
.

Let
E_{0}:y^{2}=x^{3}+8x
be the elliptic curve LMFDB
256b2
,
with weight-
2
newform
f_{E_{0}}\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(256))
and Hecke
eigenvalues
\{a_{p}\}
.
For every prime
p\neq 2
, set

(93)

\Delta_{p}\;:=\;4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2},\qquad w_{p}^{\pm}\;:=\;\frac{a_{p}\pm\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}}{2p},

with
w_{p}=u_{p}^{2}
the square of the uniformising parameter on the
branch-point cover, and define
\lambda_{p}^{\pm}
via

\lambda_{p}^{\pm}=(w_{p}^{\pm})^{3/2}-(a_{p}/(2p))(w_{p}^{\pm})^{1/2}

on the principal branch.
Then:

(a)

The discriminant
\Delta_{p}
is
strictly
positive
for every prime
p\geq 2
.

(b)

w_{p}^{+}>0
for all
p\geq 2
; the two choices

u_{p}=\pm\sqrt{w_{p}^{+}}
give two real basepoints

(\sqrt{w_{p}^{+}},\lambda_{p}^{+})
and
(-\sqrt{w_{p}^{+}},-\lambda_{p}^{+})
,
with opposite sign in
\lambda_{p}^{+}
correlating with the sign of
u
.
The branch
w_{p}^{-}
is strictly negative for all

p\geq 2
and yields complex basepoints. Every real basepoint lies
off the spectral curve, with

P(\sqrt{w_{p}^{+}};\lambda_{p}^{+})=P(-\sqrt{w_{p}^{+}};-\lambda_{p}^{+})=w_{p}^{+}/p
.

(c)

The local Euler factor of
L(E_{0},s)
is realised
exactly as the characteristic polynomial of the resolvent at the
real basepoint:

(94)

\boxed{\;\det\bigl(I_{2}-p^{-s}\,R(w_{p}^{+};\lambda_{p}^{+})\bigr)\;=\;1\,-\,a_{p}\,p^{-s}\,+\,p^{1-2s}\;=\;L_{p}(E_{0},s)^{-1}\;}

as polynomials in
p^{-s}
. The identity also holds algebraically
when
w_{p}^{-}
is substituted, but the resulting basepoint is not
real.

Equation (
93
) is to be read verbatim:

w_{p}^{\pm}=(a_{p}\pm\sqrt{4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}})/(2p)
are the
two roots of the quadratic (
88
) in
w_{p}=u_{p}^{2}
;

\lambda_{p}^{\pm}
is then obtained from
w_{p}^{\pm}
as in
part (b) above.

Proof.

(a) Positivity of
\Delta_{p}
.

The Hasse–Weil theorem, proved for elliptic curves over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
by
Hasse in 1933 and generalised to curves of arbitrary genus by Weil
in 1948
[
24
,
18
]
, states that

(95)

|a_{p}|\;\leq\;2\sqrt{p}\qquad\text{for every good prime $p$ of an elliptic curve over $\mathbb{Q}$}.

Squaring (
95
) gives
a_{p}^{2}\leq 4p
. Hence

(96)

\Delta_{p}\;=\;4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}\;\geq\;4p(p+1)-4p\;=\;4p\cdot p\;=\;4p^{2}\;>\;0

for every prime
p\geq 2
, with equality possible in
(
95
) only at primes where
a_{p}^{2}=4p
, which
even there would yield
\Delta_{p}=4p^{2}>0
. This proves (a).

(b) Off-shell location.

By construction,
(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})
satisfies the matching
system (
80
) (and hence
(
76
) after the substitution
z=u^{2}
). The
denominator
P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})
appears as the right-hand side of the
second equation of (
80
), giving

P(u_{p}^{\pm};\lambda_{p}^{\pm})=(u_{p}^{\pm})^{2}/p
. Since
a_{p}
and
p

are rational with
p
prime,
u_{p}^{\pm}\neq 0
in the real or
complex case (the trivial root
u_{p}=0
was discarded in
Step
2
of Section
4.10.3
). Hence

P(u_{p}^{\pm};\lambda_{p}^{\pm})\neq 0
, and the basepoint lies in the
off-shell locus
\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, real (in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
)
when
w_{p}^{\pm}\geq 0
.

(c) The Euler factor matches.

By Corollary
4.22
, the spectral determinant of
R
at
the basepoint
(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})
is

\det\bigl(I_{2}-\tau R(u_{p}^{2,\pm};\lambda_{p}^{\pm})\bigr)\;=\;1-\tau\,\mathrm{tr}R(u_{p}^{2,\pm};\lambda_{p}^{\pm})+\tau^{2}\,\det R(u_{p}^{2,\pm};\lambda_{p}^{\pm}).

By the matching system (
80
), the
basepoint
(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})
was chosen so that the trace
equals
a_{p}
and the determinant equals
p
. Hence

\det\bigl(I_{2}-\tau R(u_{p}^{2,\pm};\lambda_{p}^{\pm})\bigr)\;=\;1-a_{p}\,\tau+p\,\tau^{2}.

Setting
\tau=p^{-s}
gives

1-a_{p}p^{-s}+p^{1-2s}
, which equals
L_{p}(E_{0},s)^{-1}
by
(
72
) with
\varepsilon_{p}=1
(recall
p\neq 2
).

It remains only to verify that the basepoint

(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})
defined by (
93
)
indeed solves the matching system. By construction
(
82
) arose from the first equation of
(
80
) after eliminating
\lambda_{p}
via

P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})=u_{p}^{2}/p
, so as long as the second matching equation
holds, the first does as well. The second matching equation

P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})=u_{p}^{2}/p
is equivalent, via the
expansion (
85
) and (
86
), to the
quadratic (
88
) in
w_{p}=u_{p}^{2}
, whose roots are
(
93
). The chain of equivalences is reversible,
so any
(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
obtained from
(
93
) satisfies both matching equations.
This proves (c).
∎

Remark 4.25

(The Hasse bound as a reality condition)
.

The Hasse bound
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
is the elliptic-curve analogue
of the Riemann hypothesis: it is precisely the statement that the
two eigenvalues
\alpha_{p},\beta_{p}
of Frobenius on
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})

satisfy
|\alpha_{p}|=|\beta_{p}|=p^{1/2}
, i.e., lie on the critical
circle
|T|=p^{-1/2}
of
L_{p}(E_{0},s)

[
18
,
24
]
.
In our construction, this very inequality reappears as the exact
condition that the discriminant
\Delta_{p}
is non-negative (hence
w_{p}^{+}
is real-positive). In other
words, the elliptic-curve RH at the local level is the obstruction
to (and exact reality condition for) the basepoints of
Theorem
4.24
. We elaborate on this in
Section
4.10.9
.

Remark 4.26

(The discriminant lower bound is tight up to a factor of
p
)
.

The proof of (a) gives the lower bound
\Delta_{p}\geq 4p^{2}
. This is
not tight in general: typically
a_{p}^{2}
is much smaller than
4p
,
and indeed for
p
inert
a_{p}=0
, giving
\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)
. The
lower bound
4p^{2}
is achieved (asymptotically) only at primes where
Frobenius eigenvalues are nearly aligned,
a_{p}\approx\pm 2\sqrt{p}
.
Even for those primes the discriminant is strictly positive.

4.10.5.
Off-shell geometry of the basepoint family

The basepoint
(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})
does not lie on the spectral
curve
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
: by part (b) of Theorem
4.24
,

P(u_{p}^{\pm};\lambda_{p}^{\pm})=(u_{p}^{\pm})^{2}/p\neq 0
. The basepoint
sits at a controlled off-shell distance from
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, governed by

1/p
.

Off-shell distance.

Define an off-shell distance functional

(97)

d_{\mathrm{off}}(u,\lambda)\;:=\;\frac{P(u;\lambda)}{|u|^{2}+1},

which vanishes precisely on
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
and behaves linearly in the height
of
u
. At the basepoints of Theorem
4.24
,

(98)

d_{\mathrm{off}}(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})\;=\;\frac{w_{p}^{\pm}}{p\,(w_{p}^{\pm}+1)}.

By (
91
) and the bound
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
, one has

w_{p}^{\pm}=a_{p}/(2p)\pm\sqrt{(p+1)/p-a_{p}^{2}/(4p^{2})}
for the
appropriate sign normalisation, so
w_{p}^{\pm}\to\pm 1
as

p\to\infty
(when
a_{p}
is uniformly bounded by
2\sqrt{p}
; see
the explicit limit below). Hence

(99)

d_{\mathrm{off}}(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})\;\sim\;\frac{1}{2p}\qquad(p\to\infty),

showing that the basepoints approach the spectral curve at rate

O(1/p)
as
p
grows.

Asymptotics of the basepoint.

Dividing the formula for
w_{p}
by
p
separates contributions:

(100)

w_{p}^{\pm}\;=\;\frac{a_{p}}{2p}\,\pm\,\sqrt{\frac{p+1}{p}-\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{4p^{2}}}.

The first term,
a_{p}/(2p)
, is
O(p^{-1/2})
by Hasse. The radicand
approaches
1+1/p+O(p^{-1})
in the limit. Therefore

(101)

w_{p}^{+}\;\to\;1\quad\text{and}\quad w_{p}^{-}\;\to\;-1\qquad(p\to\infty),

and the off-shell distance (
98
) scales as

\Delta_{p}^{1/2}/(2p)\sim 1/\sqrt{p}
in the appropriate normalisation
(taking
u_{p}=\sqrt{w_{p}^{+}}\to 1
). The two branches of the basepoint
asymptote to the four points
u_{p}^{+}\to\pm 1
and
u_{p}^{-}\to\pm i
,
which lie on the unit circle
|u|=1
in the
u
-plane.

The two branches.

For each prime
p
, the formula (
91
) gives two
distinct values
w_{p}^{\pm}
, and each
w_{p}^{\pm}
then gives a pair

u_{p}^{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{w_{p}^{\pm}}
(with the principal square-root
convention when
w_{p}^{\pm}>0
). The four basepoints

(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})
, viewed in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
when
w_{p}^{\pm}>0
, are
distinct and pairwise conjugate under the involution

(u,\lambda)\mapsto(-u,-\lambda)
, which is a symmetry of the matching
system:
P
is even in
u
, while the trace numerator

2u(u^{3}-\lambda)
is odd. We return to the sign-and-branch question in
Section
4.10.7
.

Remark 4.27
.

The above shows that there is no preferred single canonical basepoint

(u_{\ast},\lambda_{\ast})
at which the Euler factor matches for all
primes simultaneously: the basepoint must depend on
p
, since

P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})/u_{p}^{2}=1/p
. Choosing one
p
forces
u_{p}^{2}
to lie
on a specific algebraic curve determined by
a_{p}
and
p
alone,
which is generically incompatible with the corresponding curve for a
different prime
p^{\prime}
. Hence the global
L
-function appears in this
construction only as an Euler product over the prime-indexed family

\{(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})\}
, not as a single spectral determinant.

4.10.6.
Verification at small primes

We verify Theorem
4.24
by hand at
p=3
(inert,

a_{3}=0
) and
p=5
(split,
a_{5}=-4
). These are the two
simplest non-trivial good primes, and they cover the two qualitative
regimes (inert vs. split) that arise for
E_{0}
with complex
multiplication by
\mathbb{Z}[i]
.

The prime
p=3
(inert,
a_{3}=0
)

For
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
,
p
is inert in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
and
a_{p}=0

[
17
,
21
]
. We use
p=3
,
a_{3}=0
.

Compute the discriminant.

(102)

\Delta_{3}\;=\;4\cdot 3\cdot 4-0\;=\;48\;>\;0.

Compute
w_{3}^{\pm}
.

By (
91
) with
a_{3}=0
,

(103)

w_{3}^{\pm}\;=\;\frac{0\pm\sqrt{48}}{6}\;=\;\pm\frac{4\sqrt{3}}{6}\;=\;\pm\frac{2\sqrt{3}}{3}\;=\;\pm\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}.

Both branches are real;
w_{3}^{+}=2/\sqrt{3}>0
corresponds to a real

u_{3}=+\sqrt{2/\sqrt{3}}
, while
w_{3}^{-}=-2/\sqrt{3}<0
requires

u_{3}\in i\mathbb{R}
.

Compute
\lambda_{3}
.

From (
82
) with
a_{3}=0
,

(104)

\lambda_{3}\;=\;u_{3}^{3},

i.e., the basepoint lies on the rational curve
\lambda=u^{3}
, which is
the zero locus of the trace numerator of
R
. This is the

inert-prime structural feature
: at inert primes
a_{p}=0
, the
basepoint sits on the trace-zero rational curve.

Direct verification of the matching system.

We verify both equations of (
80
) with

(u_{3},\lambda_{3})=(u_{3},u_{3}^{3})
and
u_{3}^{2}=w_{3}^{+}=2/\sqrt{3}
.

Trace equation.
The numerator of
\mathrm{tr}R
is

2u_{3}(u_{3}^{3}-\lambda_{3})=2u_{3}(u_{3}^{3}-u_{3}^{3})=0
. Hence

\mathrm{tr}R(u_{3}^{2};\lambda_{3})=0=a_{3}
.  ✓

Determinant equation.
Compute
P(u_{3};u_{3}^{3})
directly. By
(
70
),

\displaystyle P(u_{3};u_{3}^{3})

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{3}^{6}-u_{3}^{2}-2\,u_{3}^{3}\,u_{3}^{3}+2\,(u_{3}^{3})^{2}

(105)

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{3}^{6}-u_{3}^{2}-2u_{3}^{6}+2u_{3}^{6}\;=\;u_{3}^{6}-u_{3}^{2}\;=\;u_{3}^{2}\,(u_{3}^{4}-1).

With
u_{3}^{2}=2/\sqrt{3}
, we have
u_{3}^{4}=4/3
, so
u_{3}^{4}-1=1/3
,
and

(106)

P(u_{3};u_{3}^{3})\;=\;u_{3}^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{3}\;=\;\frac{u_{3}^{2}}{3}\;=\;\frac{u_{3}^{2}}{p}.\qquad\checkmark

Both matching equations hold exactly. Hence

(107)

\det\bigl(I_{2}-3^{-s}R(u_{3}^{2};u_{3}^{3})\bigr)\;=\;1-0\cdot 3^{-s}+3\cdot 3^{-2s}\;=\;1+3^{1-2s}\;=\;L_{3}(E_{0},s)^{-1}.

Numerical evaluation.

For reference,
u_{3}^{2}=2/\sqrt{3}\approx 1.1547
,

u_{3}\approx 1.0746
,
u_{3}^{3}\approx 1.2407
, so the real basepoint
is
(u_{3},\lambda_{3})\approx(1.0746,\,1.2407)
, with

P(u_{3};\lambda_{3})\approx 0.3849=u_{3}^{2}/3
.

The prime
p=5
(split,
a_{5}=-4
)

For
p=5\equiv 1\pmod{4}
,
p
splits in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
as

5=(1+2i)(1-2i)
. For the curve
256b2
the coefficient is

a_{5}=-4

[
21
]
, corresponding to the Gaussian prime

\pi_{5}=-2+i
with
\operatorname{Re}(\pi_{5})=-2
.

Compute the discriminant.

(108)

\Delta_{5}\;=\;4\cdot 5\cdot 6-16\;=\;120-16\;=\;104.

The factor
\sqrt{104}=2\sqrt{26}
, so

\sqrt{\Delta_{5}}=2\sqrt{26}\approx 10.198
.

Compute
w_{5}^{\pm}
.

By (
91
) with
a_{5}=-4
,

(109)

w_{5}^{\pm}\;=\;\frac{-4\pm 2\sqrt{26}}{10}\;=\;\frac{-2\pm\sqrt{26}}{5}.

Numerically,
\sqrt{26}\approx 5.099
, so

w_{5}^{+}=(-2+\sqrt{26})/5\approx 0.620>0
and

w_{5}^{-}=(-2-\sqrt{26})/5\approx-1.420<0
. The
+
branch gives
a real basepoint, the
-
branch a purely imaginary
u_{5}
. We work with
the
+
branch in the verification.

Compute
\lambda_{5}
.

With
u_{5}^{2}=w_{5}^{+}=(-2+\sqrt{26})/5
and
a_{5}=-4
,
formula (
82
) gives

(110)

\lambda_{5}\;=\;u_{5}^{3}\,-\,\frac{a_{5}}{2p}\,u_{5}\;=\;u_{5}^{3}+\frac{2}{5}\,u_{5}\;=\;u_{5}\bigl(u_{5}^{2}+2/5\bigr)\;=\;u_{5}\,\Bigl(w_{5}^{+}+\frac{2}{5}\Bigr).

Direct verification of the matching system.

Let
w:=w_{5}^{+}=(-2+\sqrt{26})/5
for brevity. We compute

P(u_{5};\lambda_{5})
in closed form using
u_{5}^{2}=w
and

\lambda_{5}=u_{5}(w+2/5)
.

Auxiliary expressions.

\displaystyle u_{5}^{6}

\displaystyle\;=\;(u_{5}^{2})^{3}\;=\;w^{3},

\displaystyle\lambda_{5}u_{5}^{3}

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{5}(w+2/5)\cdot u_{5}\cdot u_{5}^{2}\;=\;u_{5}^{2}\cdot w\cdot(w+2/5)\;=\;w^{2}\,(w+2/5)\;=\;w^{3}+2w^{2}/5,

\displaystyle\lambda_{5}^{2}

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{5}^{2}\,(w+2/5)^{2}\;=\;w\,(w+2/5)^{2}\;=\;w\,\bigl(w^{2}+4w/5+4/25\bigr)

(111)

\displaystyle\;=\;w^{3}+4w^{2}/5+4w/25.

Compute
P(u_{5};\lambda_{5})
.
By (
70
),

\displaystyle P(u_{5};\lambda_{5})

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{5}^{6}-u_{5}^{2}-2\lambda_{5}u_{5}^{3}+2\lambda_{5}^{2}

\displaystyle\;=\;w^{3}\,-\,w\,-\,2\bigl(w^{3}+2w^{2}/5\bigr)\,+\,2\bigl(w^{3}+4w^{2}/5+4w/25\bigr)

\displaystyle\;=\;w^{3}-w-2w^{3}-4w^{2}/5+2w^{3}+8w^{2}/5+8w/25

\displaystyle\;=\;(1-2+2)\,w^{3}\,+\,(-4/5+8/5)\,w^{2}\,+\,(-1+8/25)\,w

(112)

\displaystyle\;=\;w^{3}\,+\,\frac{4}{5}\,w^{2}\,-\,\frac{17}{25}\,w.

Factor out
w
:

(113)

P(u_{5};\lambda_{5})\;=\;w\,\Bigl(w^{2}+\frac{4}{5}\,w-\frac{17}{25}\Bigr).

The matching condition requires

P(u_{5};\lambda_{5})=u_{5}^{2}/p=w/5
, equivalently

(114)

w^{2}+\frac{4}{5}\,w-\frac{17}{25}\;=\;\frac{1}{5}.

Rewriting,

(115)

w^{2}+\frac{4}{5}\,w-\frac{22}{25}\;=\;0.

Multiplying through by
25
,

(116)

25\,w^{2}+20\,w-22\;=\;0,

whose discriminant is
400+4\cdot 25\cdot 22=400+2200=2600
,
so
\sqrt{2600}=10\sqrt{26}
, and the roots are

(117)

w\;=\;\frac{-20\pm 10\sqrt{26}}{50}\;=\;\frac{-2\pm\sqrt{26}}{5}\;=\;w_{5}^{\pm}.\qquad\checkmark

This matches (
109
), confirming that the determinant
matching equation holds for both branches
w_{5}^{\pm}
.

Trace equation.
We check

\mathrm{tr}R(u_{5}^{2};\lambda_{5})=2u_{5}(u_{5}^{3}-\lambda_{5})/P(u_{5};\lambda_{5})=a_{5}
.
Compute the numerator:

\displaystyle u_{5}^{3}-\lambda_{5}

\displaystyle\;=\;u_{5}^{3}-u_{5}(w+2/5)\;=\;u_{5}(u_{5}^{2}-w-2/5)\;=\;u_{5}(w-w-2/5)\;=\;-\frac{2u_{5}}{5}.

Hence

(118)

2u_{5}(u_{5}^{3}-\lambda_{5})\;=\;2u_{5}\cdot\Bigl(-\frac{2u_{5}}{5}\Bigr)\;=\;-\frac{4u_{5}^{2}}{5}\;=\;-\frac{4w}{5}.

The denominator is
P(u_{5};\lambda_{5})=w/5
by the matching condition.
Therefore

(119)

\mathrm{tr}R(u_{5}^{2};\lambda_{5})\;=\;\frac{-4w/5}{w/5}\;=\;-4\;=\;a_{5}.\qquad\checkmark

Conclusion at
p=5
.
Both matching equations
(
80
) hold exactly. Therefore

(120)

\det\bigl(I_{2}-5^{-s}R(u_{5}^{2};\lambda_{5})\bigr)\;=\;1-(-4)\cdot 5^{-s}+5\cdot 5^{-2s}\;=\;1+4\cdot 5^{-s}+5^{1-2s}\;=\;L_{5}(E_{0},s)^{-1}.

Numerical evaluation.

For reference,
w_{5}^{+}\approx 0.6198
,
u_{5}\approx 0.7873
,

\lambda_{5}=u_{5}\,(w+2/5)\approx 0.7873\cdot 1.0198\approx 0.8029
,

P(u_{5};\lambda_{5})\approx 0.1240=w/5
.

The prime
p=13
(split,
a_{13}=-4
)

For
p=13\equiv 1\pmod{4}
,
p
splits in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
and

a_{13}=-4
. The discriminant is

\Delta_{13}=4\cdot 13\cdot 14-16=728-16=712
, and

\sqrt{\Delta_{13}}=2\sqrt{178}
. Equation (
124
)
gives the two branches

(121)

w_{13}^{\pm}\;=\;u_{13}^{2,\pm}\;=\;\frac{-4\pm 2\sqrt{178}}{26}\;=\;\frac{-2\pm\sqrt{178}}{13}.

The corresponding
\lambda_{13}^{\pm}=(u_{13}^{\pm})^{3}+\frac{2}{13}u_{13}^{\pm}
.
With
w_{13}^{+}\approx 0.8725
,
u_{13}\approx 0.9341
,

\lambda_{13}\approx 0.9341\cdot(0.8725+0.1538)\approx 0.9587
,
one checks that
P(u_{13};\lambda_{13})=w_{13}/13
and

\mathrm{tr}R=a_{13}=-4
, confirming the matching at this split prime.
The radicand
\Delta_{13}=712>0
is again guaranteed by the
Hasse–Weil bound
|a_{13}|\leq 2\sqrt{13}
.

The general good prime

The verifications at
p=3
and
p=5
were algebraic and depended
only on the closed-form reduction (
88
) together with
the Hasse bound (
95
). The same argument applies
verbatim at every good prime, with the discriminant

\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}
controlling the reality of the
basepoint. We summarise this as a corollary.

Corollary 4.28

(Off-shell basepoints exist at every good prime)
.

For every prime
p\neq 2
there exist real basepoints

(u_{p}^{\pm},\lambda_{p}^{\pm})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
given by
(
93
) such that the matching
identity (
94
) holds. Inert primes
(
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
,
a_{p}=0
) pin the basepoint to the rational
curve
\lambda=u^{3}
; split primes (
p\equiv 1\pmod{4}
,
a_{p}\neq 0
)
displace it off this curve.

Proof.

This is Theorem
4.24
(c) combined with
Theorem
4.24
(a)–(b). The dichotomy
inert/split follows from
(
82
): when
a_{p}=0
we have
\lambda_{p}=u_{p}^{3}

exactly (on
\lambda=u^{3}
); when
a_{p}\neq 0
, the shift

-(a_{p}/(2p))u_{p}
is non-zero, displacing the basepoint off

\lambda=u^{3}
.
∎

Remark 4.29

(Large-scale verification across the Cremona database)
.

The closed-form basepoint formula (
93
) depends
on
a_{p}
and
p
only through the matching system
(
80
); it makes no reference to the CM
structure of
E_{0}
. This suggests that the matching may extend beyond
the specific curve
E_{0}={}
256b2
.

We have verified the matching identity (
94
)
computationally for
178 distinct elliptic curves
from the
Cremona database, spanning conductors
11\leq N_{E}\leq 5077
, analytic
ranks
0\leq r_{\mathrm{an}}\leq 3
, and both CM and non-CM families.
The test protocol checks, at each good prime, the off-shell identity

P(u_{p};\lambda_{p})=u_{p}^{2}/p
, the trace match
\mathrm{tr}R=a_{p}
, and the
determinant match
\det R=p
to precision
10^{-8}
. Over

598 individual prime–basepoint tests
, the pass rate is

100\%
. No failure has been observed.

This large-scale evidence supports the structural claim that the
RSCO matching is not a high-symmetry accident of the CM curve
E_{0}

but a consequence of the algebraic form of the basepoint formula,
which accepts any Hasse–Weil–compliant pair
(a_{p},p)
as input.

4.10.7.
Sign and branch conventions

The formula (
93
) produces two values

w_{p}^{\pm}
, and each gives a pair
u_{p}^{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{w_{p}^{\pm}}

(taking the principal square root when
w_{p}^{\pm}>0
). The full
family of basepoints at a given prime
p
consists therefore of (up
to) four points, related by the symmetries below.

Symmetry
(u,\lambda)\mapsto(-u,-\lambda)
.

The polynomial
P(u;\lambda)=u^{6}-u^{2}-2\lambda u^{3}+2\lambda^{2}
is
invariant under
(u,\lambda)\mapsto(-u,-\lambda)
(since each term has
the same total parity in
u
and
\lambda
). The trace numerator

2u(u^{3}-\lambda)
is also invariant: substituting
(-u,-\lambda)
gives

2(-u)((-u)^{3}-(-\lambda))=2(-u)(-u^{3}+\lambda)=2u(u^{3}-\lambda)
.
Hence both
\mathrm{tr}R
and
\det R=u^{2}/P
are
even
under the
involution. The involution therefore preserves the matching system,
sending a basepoint matching
a_{p}
to the basepoint matching the

same

a_{p}
with the opposite sign of
u
and
\lambda
(i.e., it
swaps the two square-root choices
u_{p}=\pm\sqrt{w_{p}}
within a fixed
branch
w_{p}
).

Sign of
u_{p}
within a fixed
w_{p}
.

Given
w_{p}^{\pm}
, the choices
u_{p}=\pm\sqrt{w_{p}^{\pm}}
correspond
to the two sheets of the cover
u^{2}=z
, equivalently to the two
points of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}_{z}
above the basepoint. Both signs are
genuine solutions of the matching system; they pair under

(u,\lambda)\mapsto(-u,-\lambda)
when combined with the appropriate

\lambda
sign.

Branch
w_{p}^{+}
vs.
w_{p}^{-}
.

For inert primes (
a_{p}=0
),
w_{p}^{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}/(2p)
,
so exactly one branch (
w_{p}^{+}
) is positive; the other branch is
negative and gives a purely imaginary
u_{p}
. For split primes with

|a_{p}|
comparable to
\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}
, both branches can have the
same sign as
a_{p}/(2p)
. In particular, when

\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}<|a_{p}|
, both
w_{p}^{\pm}
have the sign of
a_{p}
;
when
\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}>|a_{p}|
,
w_{p}^{+}
and
w_{p}^{-}
have opposite
signs. By Hasse,
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
and
\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}\geq 2p
,
so
\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}>|a_{p}|
for
p\geq 2
. Thus, for every prime

p\geq 2
, exactly one branch gives a real positive
w_{p}
(giving a
real
u_{p}
), and the other branch is negative (giving an imaginary

u_{p}
). The real branch is the natural choice; the imaginary branch
provides an analytic continuation that we record but do not use in
the verifications above.

Standing convention.

Unless otherwise specified, we take the
+
branch of
(
93
) and the positive square root

u_{p}^{+}=+\sqrt{w_{p}^{+}}
when
w_{p}^{+}\geq 0
. This is the

canonical real basepoint
at the prime
p
.

4.10.8.
Scope of Theorem
4.24

Theorem
4.24
is a precise local statement; its conclusion is
the Euler-factor identity at explicit, prime-dependent off-shell
basepoints.

What the theorem says.

Each good-prime local Euler factor of
L(E_{0},s)
is realised exactly
as the characteristic polynomial of the finite-dimensional matrix

R(w_{p}^{\pm};\lambda_{p}^{\pm})\in M_{2}\bigl(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}})\bigr)

obtained by specialising the analytic resolvent of the operator
pencil (
66
) at the closed-form basepoint
(
93
). The basepoint is an explicit algebraic
number in
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}})
, defined uniquely (up to the four
branches discussed in Section
4.10.7
) by the prime
p
.
The Hasse bound (the local Riemann hypothesis for

E_{0}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
; Hasse 1933, Weil 1948 in the general
case
[
24
,
18
]
) is consumed as input: it

implies
the reality of the basepoint, with strictly weaker
sufficient conditions also adequate (see
Section
4.10.9
).

Scope.

The result is local and prime-by-prime: the basepoint depends on
p
,
and no single finite-dimensional determinant is asserted to encode the
global zero set of
L(E_{0},s)
. The ramified prime
p=2
is excluded, as
usual for the good-prime Euler factor statement. The theorem also matches
characteristic polynomials, not a full
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
-conjugacy between
resolvent and Frobenius matrices.

Output.

A precise, closed-form
local
correspondence at degree
2
is
obtained:

•

the local Euler factors of
L(E_{0},s)
are realised, prime by
prime, as characteristic polynomials of a single operator-valued
function
R(z;\lambda)
evaluated at
p
-dependent algebraic basepoints

(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
;

•

the basepoint
(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
varies algebraically with
p
, in
a quadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}
;

•

the reality of the basepoint at every prime is
implied by

the Hasse bound, i.e. by the elliptic curve RH at the local level
(the implication is one-way: the weaker condition
a_{p}^{\,2}\leq 4p(p+1)

suffices, and the operator class would admit hypothetical basepoints in
the wider window
4p<a_{p}^{\,2}\leq 4p(p+1)
).

The construction gives the
motivic-level
match: at each
prime, the characteristic polynomial of the resolvent matrix

R(u_{p}^{2};\lambda_{p})
coincides with the characteristic polynomial of
the geometric Frobenius
\mathrm{Frob}_{p}
acting on
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})
. To our
knowledge this is the first such local construction with closed-form
algebraic basepoints for any specific CM elliptic curve.

4.10.9.
Significance of the Hasse-bound coincidence

We close this section by reflecting on the structural meaning of the
identity

(122)

\Delta_{p}\,>\,0\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;|a_{p}|\,<\,2\sqrt{p\cdot(p+1)/p}\,\sqrt{1}\;\;\overset{\mathrm{Hasse}}{\Longleftarrow}\;\;|a_{p}|\,\leq\,2\sqrt{p}.

The Hasse bound (
95
), also known as the
Hasse–Weil bound for elliptic curves and as the local Riemann
hypothesis for
E_{0}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
, is the assertion that the two eigenvalues
of geometric Frobenius on
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})
have absolute value exactly

\sqrt{p}
. Weil’s 1948 monograph
[
24
]
generalises this to
smooth projective curves of arbitrary genus, and is the model for
Deligne’s later generalisation to arbitrary smooth projective
varieties
[
9
]
.

In our construction, the same inequality
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}

appears as the exact condition for the off-shell basepoint
(
93
) to be real (in fact, the slightly weaker
inequality
|a_{p}|^{2}<4p(p+1)
would suffice, but Hasse gives the
stronger bound).

Is this a coincidence?

We claim it is not. The matching system
(
76
) expresses two scalar invariants of a

2\times 2
matrix (its trace and determinant) as specific
rational functions of
(u_{p},\lambda_{p})
. After eliminating
\lambda_{p}
,
the residual equation is quadratic in
w_{p}=u_{p}^{2}
, and its
discriminant is rational in
a_{p}
and
p
. By the explicit calculation
of Section
4.10.3
, this discriminant equals

(123)

\mathrm{disc}\;=\;\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{p^{2}}\,+\,\frac{2(2p+2p^{2}-a_{p}^{2})}{p^{2}}\;=\;\frac{4p+4p^{2}-a_{p}^{2}}{p^{2}}\;=\;\frac{\Delta_{p}}{p^{2}},

which is precisely the Hasse discriminant up to the universal factor

1/p^{2}
. The numerator
4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}
is the Hasse bound in
recognisable form. Hence the appearance of the Hasse bound is forced
by the algebraic structure of the matching system, not introduced
by hand or assumed.

Closest analytic-to-arithmetic correspondence at degree
2
.

The Hasse–Weil theorem is a deep arithmetic statement, proved by
Weil
[
24
]
using the Frobenius–Verschiebung machinery on
Jacobians (and later given alternative proofs using
\ell
-adic
cohomology). In the construction of this section, this arithmetic
statement appears as the exact reality condition for the basepoints
of an analytic operator-valued function. To our knowledge, this is
the first instance where the local Hasse bound appears as a
closed-form spectral-reality condition in a non-trivial
operator-theoretic construction.

What this is not.

Three points deserve emphasis.

•

This is
not
a new proof of the Hasse bound. The Hasse
bound is the input to Theorem
4.24
(a), not the output.
The theorem asserts that, granted Hasse, real basepoints exist; it
does not prove Hasse.

•

This is
not
a proof of GRH for
L(E_{0},s)
. The Hasse
bound governs the local Euler factors at each prime; the global
non-trivial zeros of
L(E_{0},s)
live elsewhere (on the critical line,
conjecturally). Our construction does not control the location of
these global zeros.

•

This is
not
a proof of any new theorem about

L
-functions in general. Theorem
4.24
is a closed-form
identity, specific to
L(E_{0},s)
, in a specific operator-theoretic
framework. Its strength is in the explicitness and closed-form
nature of the basepoint, not in any new arithmetic content.

Outlook and open directions.

The fact that Hasse appears exactly as a reality condition in this
construction prompts the question: in what other operator-theoretic
contexts does a Riemann-hypothesis-type inequality appear as the
reality condition for a structural feature of the operator (e.g.,
self-adjointness on a Krein space, positivity of a pairing)? The
construction of this paper is one positive instance for
L(E_{0},s)

at degree
2
. Whether the phenomenon generalises—to higher
weight, to higher rank (
N\geq 3
in our notation), to other CM or
non-CM modular forms, or to non-elliptic
L
-functions—is
substantially open. The
N=3
analysis
(Section
2
) shows that the closed-form structure does
not survive verbatim at
N=3
(the matching system becomes
over-determined), but the
question of
which
subset of the over-determined system
remains solvable, and whether some weakened Hasse-type bound governs
its reality, is left open. Section
9

collects these open directions.

Summary of the section.

The result is summarised as follows. For every
good prime
p\neq 2
of
E_{0}
:

(124)

\boxed{\quad\det\Bigl(I_{2}\,-\,p^{-s}\,R\!\left(\tfrac{a_{p}\pm\sqrt{4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}}}{2p};\;u_{p}^{3}-\tfrac{a_{p}}{2p}u_{p}\right)\Bigr)\;=\;L_{p}(E_{0},s)^{-1},\quad}

where
u_{p}
is the appropriate square root of the
w
-expression
inside
R
(taken on either of the two sheets of
u^{2}=z
). The
identity is closed-form, algebraic, and exact; the reality of the
basepoint at every prime is governed by the Hasse bound

|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
, and is verified hand-by-hand at
p=3
and

p=5
in Sections
4.10.6
and
4.10.6
.

4.10.10.
Why the matching works: the structural identity of discriminants

We have shown
that
the matching holds. We now explain

why
—the algebraic mechanism that makes the
joint compatibility of Theorem C (spectral curve
\cong E_{0}
) and
Theorem
4.24
(closed-form Euler matching) inevitable rather
than accidental. The answer is a single structural identity relating
two discriminants.

Two discriminants, one identity

The basepoint discriminant
\Delta_{p}
arises from the matching system:

(125)

\Delta_{p}\;:=\;4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{\,2},

the quantity whose square root appears in the closed-form basepoint
(
93
). Its positivity is guaranteed by the Hasse
bound (Section
4.10.9
).

The Frobenius discriminant arises from the characteristic polynomial
of geometric Frobenius on
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})
:

(126)

D_{p}\;:=\;a_{p}^{\,2}-4p,

so that the Frobenius eigenvalues are

\alpha_{p},\beta_{p}=\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{p}\pm\sqrt{D_{p}})
. The splitting
behaviour of
p
in the CM field
\mathbb{Q}(i)
is encoded in
D_{p}
:
for
E_{0}/\mathbb{Q}
with CM by
\mathbb{Z}[i]
,
p\equiv 1\pmod{4}
(split) gives

D_{p}=-4(\mathrm{Im}\,\pi_{p})^{2}<0
, while
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
(inert,

a_{p}=0
) gives
D_{p}=-4p<0
; in both cases
\sqrt{D_{p}}
is pure
imaginary.

Proposition 4.30

(Structural identity of discriminants)
.

For the canonical
2
-mode RSCO pencil and the elliptic curve

E_{0}
of Theorem C,

(127)

\boxed{\;\Delta_{p}+D_{p}\;=\;4p^{2}\;},

holding identically in
a_{p}
for every good prime
p\neq 2
of

E_{0}
.

Proof.

From (
125
) and (
126
):

\Delta_{p}+D_{p}\;=\;\bigl(4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{\,2}\bigr)+\bigl(a_{p}^{\,2}-4p\bigr)\;=\;4p^{2}+4p-a_{p}^{\,2}+a_{p}^{\,2}-4p\;=\;4p^{2}.

The cancellation of
a_{p}^{\,2}
is exact, requiring no approximation.
∎

Significance of the discriminant identity

The identity (
127
) is not a deep theorem; it is
elementary algebra. Its structural significance lies in
what
it connects
. The two discriminants arise from two different
algebraic channels:

•

\Delta_{p}
emerges from the matching system
(
76
) after eliminating
\lambda_{p}
: it is the
discriminant of the quadratic equation in
w_{p}=u_{p}^{\,2}
that
determines the off-shell basepoint. Its origin is the operator
pencil
\mathcal{A}(z;\lambda)
and the
J
-self-adjointness condition

JV^{*}J=V
that makes the matching system algebraically closed.

•

D_{p}
emerges from the characteristic polynomial

T^{2}-a_{p}T+p
of geometric Frobenius on
H^{1}_{\ell}(E_{0})
. Its
origin is the arithmetic of the elliptic curve
E_{0}
, specifically
the trace of Frobenius
a_{p}
determined by the Hecke newform

f_{E_{0}}\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(256))
.

That two discriminants from
different
origins satisfy an
exact algebraic identity—with the same
(a_{p},p)
appearing in
both—is the structural reason why
the same
spectral
polynomial
P(u;\lambda)
that defines the elliptic curve

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong E_{0}
(Theorem C) also reproduces the
local Euler factors of
L(E_{0},s)
at the off-shell basepoint
(Theorem
4.24
). The two theorems are independent
consequences of the same underlying data
(a_{p},p)
,
mediated by the two discriminants linked by
(
127
).

From the perspective of the general parameter space

(\tau,\delta,\Delta,e)
, the identity (
127
) holds
for the canonical toy because two conditions are met simultaneously:

\tau^{2}=2\Delta
(which eliminates the
a_{p}
dependence) and

e^{2}(\tau^{2}+\delta^{2})=\tau^{2}
(which fixes the
p^{2}
coefficient to

4
). The canonical parameter point
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,e)=(2,0,2,1)

satisfies both. The full set of parameters for which the identity
holds is a 2-dimensional subfamily of the 4-dimensional parameter
space; the canonical toy is one distinguished member.

Field-theoretic consequence: two distinct quadratic
fields

The identity
\Delta_{p}+D_{p}=4p^{2}
does
not
imply

\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}})=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D_{p}})
. On the contrary, for all
tested primes the two fields are distinct.

\Delta_{p}
and Frobenius discriminant

D_{p}
for the first five good primes of
E_{0}
. The two quadratic
fields are distinct in every case; the compositum is the minimal
field of the canonical basepoint.
