Table
8
exhibits the field separation
explicitly. For inert primes (
p=3,7,11
;
a_{p}=0
,

p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
),
D_{p}=-4p
, so

\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D_{p}})=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p})
, while

\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)
, giving

\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}})=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{p(p+1)})
. For split primes
(
p=5,13
;
p\equiv 1\pmod{4}
),
D_{p}
is a negative perfect
square, giving
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D_{p}})=\mathbb{Q}(i)
uniformly, while

\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}})
varies with
a_{p}=2\,\mathrm{Re}(\pi_{p})
.
In every case the compositum
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}},\sqrt{D_{p}})
is a
degree-
4
biquadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}
containing the basepoint
coordinate
u_{p}^{\,2}\in\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta_{p}})
and the Frobenius
eigenvalues in
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D_{p}})
.

The field separation is a structural feature, not a numerical
accident. The identity
\Delta_{p}+D_{p}=4p^{2}
guarantees that the
two fields are never equal unless
\Delta_{p}/D_{p}
is a rational
square—a condition that fails for the first five primes and whose
failure for general
p
follows from the differing parity of the
square-free parts of
\Delta_{p}
and
D_{p}
(one is positive, the
other negative).

Inert vs. split primes: structural dichotomy

The field separation also records the CM splitting type. For inert
primes (
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
), one has
a_{p}=0
and hence

D_{p}=-4p
. At the real basepoint of

Theorem

4.24
, the specialised resolvent has the explicit form

R_{p}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}p&\sqrt{p(p+1)}\\[2.0pt]
-\sqrt{p(p+1)}&-p\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathrm{tr}R_{p}=0,\qquad\det R_{p}=p.

Thus its characteristic polynomial is
T^{2}+p
, matching the inert
Frobenius polynomial. For split primes (
p\equiv 1\pmod{4}
), writing

a_{p}=2\mathrm{Re}(\pi_{p})
with
\pi_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}[i]
, one has

D_{p}=-4(\mathrm{Im}\,\pi_{p})^{2}
, so

\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D_{p}})=\mathbb{Q}(i)
, while

\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-4(\mathrm{Re}\,\pi_{p})^{2}
gives the varying
basepoint field. The RSCO pencil therefore detects the inert/split
dichotomy at the level of characteristic polynomials and basepoint
fields. We do not claim a canonical
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
-conjugacy between the
specialised resolvent and geometric Frobenius.

The larger picture

The two discriminants, and the identity that links them, are the
algebraic signature of a single structural fact: the

J
-self-adjoint operator pencil
\mathcal{A}(z;\lambda)
and the
elliptic curve
E_{0}
share the same arithmetic input
(a_{p},p)
.
The operator pencil processes this input through the matching system,
producing
\Delta_{p}
; the elliptic curve processes it through the
Frobenius characteristic polynomial, producing
D_{p}
. The identity
(
127
) is the algebraic statement that these two
processings are
compatible
—that the operator-side and the
arithmetic-side constructions of the local Euler factor are two
manifestations of the same underlying data.

This compatibility is not a coincidence, and it is not built in by
hand: it is forced by the
J
-self-adjointness condition

JV^{*}J=V
, which guarantees that the matching system
(
76
) is algebraically closed over
\mathbb{R}
(so
that
\Delta_{p}
is real and the basepoint is real when the Hasse
bound holds), and by the specific numerical values

(\tau,\delta,\Delta)=(2,0,2)
of the canonical toy, which
select the curve
E_{0}
from the
j
-line. The identity
(
127
) is the precise locus where the operator-side
construction and the arithmetic-side construction meet.

This concludes the headline chapter. The structural

\lambda
-evenness theorem powering the construction is in
Section
2.10
; the moduli-theoretic context for
varying the toy parameters is in Section
4.9
; the

N\geq 3
extensions, including the over-determination obstruction
that prevents (
124
) from extending uniformly to
degree-
3

L
-functions, are in Section
2
. The
prior-art comparison and open questions, including the precise
statement of why no single global basepoint exists, are in
Section
9
.

Remark 4.31

(Heterogeneous
V
:
|b|
tuning and inert-prime limitations)
.

A heterogeneous coupling construction introduces an
off-diagonal parameter
b
whose norm
|b|
provides continuous
tunability of the matching function
F_{\text{new}}
. Taking

\varphi_{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{K}c_{k}\phi_{k}
with modes
k=0,2
(
K=2
)
and correcting the
S_{22}
spectral function to its
J
-self-adjoint form

(S_{22}(z)=c_{0}^{2}/(z+1/4)+c_{2}^{2}/(z+9/4)
; poles on the negative
half-axis), the matching function takes the closed form

F_{\text{new}}(u)=\frac{aS_{11}(u^{2})+dS_{22}(u^{2})}{\sqrt{ad\,S_{11}(u^{2})S_{22}(u^{2})+|b|^{2}S_{12}(u^{2})^{2}}},\qquad S_{11}(z)=\frac{1}{z-1/4},\;S_{12}(z)=\frac{c_{0}}{z-1/4}.

For the split prime
p=5
(
a_{5}=-4
, target
-4/\sqrt{5}
), the
norm
|b|
is tuned continuously from
|b|^{2}=0
(giving

|F_{\text{new}}|\approx 2.03
) to
|b|^{2}\to\infty

(
|F_{\text{new}}|\to 0
). The target value
-4/\sqrt{5}\approx-1.789
is achieved at
|b|^{2}\approx 1.05\times 10^{4}
(verified by
SageMath 10.9 numerical root-finding), showing that the
|b|

tuning mechanism is independent of the
S_{22}
pole correction.

For inert primes (
a_{p}=0
), simultaneous matching of
p=3
and

p=7
is structurally impossible regardless of the mode set.
Write
R=S_{11}(z_{7})/S_{11}(z_{3})=S_{22}(z_{7})/S_{22}(z_{3})
for
the ratio required by
F_{\text{new}}=0
at both basepoints.
Because
S_{11}
’s nearest pole is at
z=+1/4
(positive half-axis)
while
S_{22}
’s nearest pole is at
z=-1/4
(negative half-axis),
the achievable ranges of
R
are separated:

\frac{S_{11}(z_{7})}{S_{11}(z_{3})}\;\geq\;\frac{z_{3}-1/4}{z_{7}-1/4}\;\approx\;1.105,\qquad\frac{S_{22}(z_{7})}{S_{22}(z_{3})}\;\leq\;\frac{z_{3}+1/4}{z_{7}+1/4}\;\approx\;1.065,

with a gap
\Delta R\approx 0.04
that cannot be closed by any
choice of mode combination (verified by symbolic computation with
SymPy and numerical scan of the
(c_{0},c_{2},a,d)
parameter space;
see the detailed derivation in
derivations/multiprime_gap_theorem.md
).

Consequently, the heterogeneous construction supports simultaneous
matching of at most one inert prime and one split prime.
Extension to
N\geq 3
primes would require a fundamentally
different coupling geometry.
