5.
Genus
\infty
: The Continuum Limit and Its Collapse

The Dirac pencil (§
3
, §
3.6
) took the
J
-self-adjoint structure into infinite dimension with
L_{\infty}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)
. At rank 2, it produced the same
j=1728
spectral curve as RSCO, but with a complex basepoint that resolves the inert prime obstruction. What happens when the rank grows further?

The finite-rank construction occupies the genus values
g=0
and
g=1
. At
g=0
, the pencil is uniquely rigid. At
g=1
, it is flexible enough to encode one Euler factor per prime. The natural progression is to let the rank grow.

5.1.
The three pillars fail together

The genus formula
g(N)=N-1
(§
2
) implies that as
N
increases, the spectral curve genus grows. At
N\to\infty
, the secular equation becomes transcendental. Three structural pillars that make finite-rank encoding possible collapse simultaneously:

(1)

Analyticity.
In finite rank, the resolvent has isolated poles on the secondary sheet of the Riemann surface, and those poles are the spectral data. In the continuum, the poles merge into a branch cut
[-1,1]
on the real axis. The Fredholm determinant becomes an entire function of infinite order — no longer a polynomial, hence no longer factorable by the arguments of its zeros.

(2)

Trace-class property.
The resolvent trace
\mathrm{tr}(R)
in the continuum involves the harmonic series
\sum|n|^{-1}
, which diverges. The Krein regularization would require an infinite subtraction. The determinant formula
\det(I-\tau R)=1-\tau\mathrm{tr}(R)+O(\tau^{2})
that gave the Euler factor in the genus-1 case no longer has a finite coefficient for the
\tau
term.

(3)

Polynomial factorization.
If
\det(I-\tau R)
is a polynomial, then
R
must have finite rank (Surprise Registry S-004). Infinite rank forces a transcendental spectral function. The Euler factor
1-a_{p}\tau+p\tau^{2}
is a polynomial of degree 2, and no infinite-rank operator can produce it.

The dimensional inversion principle (S-001) captures this: finite rank is not a limitation that larger rank improves upon. Finite rank is the load-bearing feature that makes arithmetic encoding possible. The continuum limit is not a regime to be regularized toward — it is the boundary beyond which encoding is impossible.

5.2.
The universality theorem

Despite losing all discrete encoding capacity, the continuum limit produces an analytically remarkable object: a universal spectral measure that is independent of all operator parameters.

Let
a:\mathbb{R}\to(-1,1)
be a smooth odd surjection with
a^{\prime}(\xi)>0
, representing any dispersion relation of the continuum operator. The Plancherel weight is

w(\xi)=\frac{a^{\prime}(\xi)}{\pi\sqrt{1-a(\xi)^{2}}}.

Theorem 5.1

(Universality of the arcsine limit)
.

For any such
a
and any
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\operatorname{Re}(z)>0
,
z\notin[-1,1]
,

I_{a}(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{w(\xi)\,a(\xi)}{z^{2}-a(\xi)^{2}}\,d\xi=\frac{\arcsin(1/z)}{\pi\sqrt{z^{2}-1}}.

The result is independent of the choice of
a(\xi)
.

Proof.

Substitute
u=a(\xi)
,
du=a^{\prime}(\xi)\,d\xi
. The weight
w(\xi)=(a^{\prime}(\xi))/(\pi\sqrt{1-a(\xi)^{2}})
transforms to
1/(\pi\sqrt{1-u^{2}})
. Hence

I_{a}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{u\,du}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}\,(z^{2}-u^{2})}.

Substitute
v=\sqrt{1-u^{2}}
, giving

I_{a}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dv}{v^{2}+(z^{2}-1)}=\frac{\arctan(1/\sqrt{z^{2}-1})}{\pi\sqrt{z^{2}-1}}.

Using
\arctan(1/\sqrt{z^{2}-1})=\arcsin(1/z)
for
\operatorname{Re}(z)>0
gives the result. The integral manifestly does not depend on
a
because the substitution
u=a(\xi)
eliminates the specific dispersion relation.
∎

Corollary 5.2
.

The spectral measure in the continuum limit is the arcsine distribution:

\frac{d\mu}{dE}=\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{1-E^{2}}},\qquad-1<E<1.

Two concrete dispersion relations demonstrate the universality numerically. For
a(\xi)=\tanh\xi
and
a(\xi)=\xi/\sqrt{1+\xi^{2}}
, the integrals collapse to the same value at all test points
z=2.0,1.5,1.1,0.5+0.1i
, with 15-digit precision. The substitution proof above shows this is structural, not coincidental.

5.3.
Operator-theoretic interpretation

The universality theorem has a clean operator-theoretic formulation. Let
A
be the Fourier multiplier by
a(\xi)
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
H
be the Hilbert transform (
H^{*}=-H
) and
J=iH
, satisfying
J^{2}=I
,
J^{*}=J
. Then
A
is
J
-self-adjoint because
a
is real-valued. Define the rank-1
J
-self-adjoint perturbation
V=|\phi\rangle\langle J\phi|
with
|\hat{\phi}(\xi)|^{2}=w(\xi)
. The Stieltjes transform gives

\langle\phi|(z-A)^{-1}|\phi\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{w(\xi)}{z-a(\xi)}\,d\xi=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{w(\xi)a(\xi)}{z^{2}-a(\xi)^{2}}\,d\xi=\frac{2\arcsin(1/z)}{\pi\sqrt{z^{2}-1}}.

By the Aronszajn–Krein formula, the spectral measure of
A+\lambda V
has density
\rho(E)=(1/\pi)\operatorname{Im}m_{0}(E+i0)/(1-\lambda m_{0}(E+i0))
with
m_{0}(z)=2\arcsin(1/z)/(\pi\sqrt{z^{2}-1})
. In the limit
\lambda\to 0
,
\rho(E)=1/(\pi\sqrt{1-E^{2}})
, independently of
a
.

5.4.
The arcsine is the CM Sato–Tate measure

The spectral measure
1/(\pi\sqrt{1-E^{2}})
coincides with the continuous component of the CM Sato–Tate measure (§
8
). The non-CM Sato–Tate measure, the semicircle
\rho(E)=(2/\pi)\sqrt{1-E^{2}}
, never appears in this continuum framework.

The obstruction is structural. The Fourier multiplier
A
generates a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra (isomorphic to
C([-1,1])
). Under any
J
-self-adjoint rank-1 perturbation, the spectral measure inherits commutativity. The semicircle arises from the non-abelian SU(2) structure of random matrix ensembles and requires a non-commutative algebra. The continuum
\mathbb{R}
framework is inherently CM-selective.

5.5.
Transcendence

The function
I(z)=\arcsin(1/z)/(\pi\sqrt{z^{2}-1})
satisfies a first-order linear ODE,

z(z^{2}-1)I^{\prime}(z)+z^{2}I(z)+\frac{1}{\pi}=0,

but is not algebraic:
\arcsin(1/z)
has infinitely many logarithmic branch points at
z=\pm 1
with infinite cyclic monodromy. Any algebraic function has finite monodromy. The transcendence is the analytic signature of the loss of discrete arithmetic encoding: the finite-rank pencil produces polynomial secular equations (algebraic spectral curves); the continuum produces a transcendental function, and all discrete structure — the prime-indexed sequence of Euler factors, the moduli of the spectral curve — averages to a universal distribution that knows only the interval
[-1,1]
.

Lemma 5.3

(Transcendence)
.

I(z)
is not an algebraic function of
z
.

5.6.
The golden ratio: a discrete echo

At the opposite extreme from the continuum — the simplest non-trivial rank-1 perturbation of
L_{\infty}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
— a remnant of the discrete structure survives.

Theorem 5.4

(Golden ratio in the rank-1 spectrum)
.

For
\phi=(e_{1}+e_{-1})/\sqrt{2}
, the perturbed operator
H=L_{\infty}+|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|
has eigenvalues

\lambda_{\pm}=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{5}}{2},

the golden ratio
\varphi\approx 1.618
and its conjugate
-1/\varphi
.

Proof.

The
2\times 2
matrix of
H
on
\operatorname{span}\{e_{1},e_{-1}\}
is

\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}3/2&1/2\\
1/2&-1/2\end{pmatrix}.

The characteristic polynomial is
\lambda^{2}-\lambda-1=0
, the defining equation of the golden ratio.
∎

The relative
\zeta
-function is
\zeta_{\mathrm{rel}}(s)=4\sinh^{2}(s\ln\varphi)
. The golden ratio emerges unsolicited from the simplest non-trivial
J
-self-adjoint perturbation — not because the operator was designed to produce it, but because the perturbation of two adjacent modes with equal coupling forces the matrix entries
3/2
,
1/2
,
1/2
,
-1/2
, which happen to satisfy
\lambda^{2}-\lambda-1=0
. The spectral spacing
\varphi-(-1/\varphi)=\sqrt{5}
is a discrete algebraic remnant that the full continuum washes to transcendental universality.

The continuum limit destroys three things: algebraicity (polynomial secular equation
\mapsto
transcendental function), encoding capacity (discrete Euler factors
\mapsto
universal spectral measure), and arithmetic selectivity (the CM/non-CM distinction collapses because the commutative
C^{*}
-algebra cannot produce the semicircle). What survives is a single function
\arcsin(1/z)/(\pi\sqrt{z^{2}-1})
, independent of all fine details of the operator.

6.
The
D=-4
Unification

The discriminant
D=-4
(Gaussian integers
\mathbb{Z}[i]
) occupies a privileged position in the framework through two independent mechanisms: one geometric, operating on the spectral curve; one analytic, operating on the background operator
L_{\infty}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)
. Neither mechanism was built to target
D=-4
specifically. Each emerges from a different structural feature of the operator. Their convergence on the same arithmetic object is the subject of this section.

6.1.
The geometric channel: order-4 automorphism

Consider the spectral curve of the rank-2 CD pencil,
\mu^{2}=1+24z^{4}
. The four branch points are the roots of
z^{4}=-1/24
, which form a square in the complex plane centered at the origin. The cross-ratio of four points in a square configuration is
-1
, which is the modular invariant for
j=1728
.

Lemma 6.1

(Order-4 automorphism)
.

The map
(z,\mu)\mapsto(iz,\mu)
is an automorphism of order 4 of the spectral curve
\mu^{2}=1+24z^{4}
.

Proof.

(iz)^{4}=i^{4}z^{4}=z^{4}
, leaving
\mu^{2}
unchanged.
i^{4}=1
and
i^{k}\neq 1
for
k=1,2,3
.
∎

Theorem 6.2

(Order-4 forces
j=1728
)
.

If the projective model of a genus-1 spectral curve admits an order-4 automorphism over
\mathbb{C}
, its
j
-invariant is
1728
.

Proof.

An elliptic curve with an order-4 automorphism has endomorphism ring containing
\mathbb{Z}[i]
, hence is CM by
\mathbb{Q}(i)
. For CM by
\mathbb{Z}[i]
, the only
j
-invariant is
1728
(Deuring).
∎

The square branch-point configuration is visible in the RSCO spectral curve
y^{2}=x^{3}-x
as well, after an appropriate projective transformation. The
3\times 3
cyclic pencil (TCO) has order-3 symmetry (branch points forming an equilateral triangle, cross-ratio
e^{\pm i\pi/3}
giving
j=0
), confirming the general principle: the symmetry of the branch-point configuration determines the
j
-invariant.

The order-4 automorphism is a property of the spectral curve alone, independent of how the curve is realized by a
J
-self-adjoint pencil. Both the RSCO pencil and the CD rank-2 pencil produce spectral curves with
j=1728
through this geometric mechanism.

6.2.
The analytic channel: eta-invariant and
\chi_{-4}

The background operator
L_{\infty}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
has eigenvalues
+1
for
n>0
and
-1
for
n<0
. The alternating sign pattern
(-1)^{n-1}
for the spectral asymmetry is, term by term, the primitive quadratic Dirichlet character modulo 4:

\chi_{-4}(n)=\begin{cases}1,&n\equiv 1\pmod{4},\\
-1,&n\equiv 3\pmod{4},\\
0,&n\text{ even}.\end{cases}

Theorem 6.3

(Eta-invariant identity)
.

The Krein-regularized alternating
\eta
-invariant of
L_{\infty}
satisfies

\eta_{\mathrm{Krein}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(s)=2L(s,\chi_{-4}),

where
L(s,\chi_{-4})=1-3^{-s}+5^{-s}-7^{-s}+\dots
is the Dirichlet
L
-function of
\chi_{-4}
.

Proof.

The alternating
\eta
-invariant

\eta^{\mathrm{alt}}(s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^{s}}

converges absolutely for
\operatorname{Re}(s)>1
and admits meromorphic continuation to the whole complex plane via the standard identity
\eta^{\mathrm{alt}}(s)=(1-2^{1-s})\zeta(s)
. The Krein-regularised version on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
counts the spectral asymmetry between the positive and negative eigenvalue branches of
L_{\infty}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)
:

\eta_{\mathrm{Krein}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(s)=\sum_{n>0}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^{s}}-\sum_{n<0}\frac{(-1)^{|n|-1}}{|n|^{s}}\cdot(-1)=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n>0\\
n\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{(n-1)/2}}{n^{s}},

where the factor
2
aggregates the symmetric contributions from positive and negative indices, and the even-
n
terms cancel because
\operatorname{sgn}(n)
has equal positive and negative eigenvalue contributions at even indices that are killed by the alternating sign.

For odd
n
, the sign
(-1)^{(n-1)/2}
equals
+1
for
n\equiv 1\pmod{4}
and
-1
for
n\equiv 3\pmod{4}
, which is exactly the character
\chi_{-4}(n)
. Hence

\eta_{\mathrm{Krein}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(s)=2\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\chi_{-4}(n)}{n^{s}}=2L(s,\chi_{-4}),

where the sum extends over all
n\geq 1
because
\chi_{-4}
vanishes on even
n
.
∎

Remark 6.4

(
\eta
at
s=1
)
.

The special value
L(1,\chi_{-4})=\pi/4
(Leibniz’s series) gives
\eta_{\mathrm{Krein}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(1)=\pi/2
, an analytic invariant of
L_{\infty}
that recovers the geometric content of the Gaussian-integer ring via the class number formula
h(-4)=1
and the regulator equation
L(1,\chi_{-4})=\pi\cdot h(-4)/(w(-4)\sqrt{4})
with
w(-4)=4
units in
\mathbb{Z}[i]^{\times}=\{\pm 1,\pm i\}
. The Krein structure thus encodes the class group of
\mathbb{Q}(i)
through the
s=1
value of its
\eta
-invariant.

Lemma 6.5

(Functional equation)
.

\eta_{\mathrm{Krein}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(s)
satisfies the functional equation

\eta_{\mathrm{Krein}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(s)=2\cdot 2^{s}\pi^{s-1}\sin(\pi s/2)\Gamma(1-s)L(1-s,\chi_{-4}),

inherited from the Dirichlet
L
-function
L(s,\chi_{-4})
via the identity of Theorem
6.3
.

Proof.

The functional equation for
L(s,\chi_{-4})
with the (odd, modulus
4
) primitive character is standard
[
8
]
; multiplying by
2
gives the stated equation.
∎

The character
\chi_{-4}
is not chosen — it is forced by the spectral structure of
L_{\infty}
, whose alternating eigenvalues reproduce the Legendre symbol
(\frac{-1}{p})
. This identity holds for any
J
-self-adjoint causal pencil built from
L_{\infty}
, regardless of the perturbation. The analytic channel is therefore more robust than the geometric channel: deforming
V
moves
j
away from
1728
, but the
\eta
-invariant identity persists as long as the background is
L_{\infty}
.

6.3.
Convergence

The geometry (spectral curve
j=1728
) and the analysis (
\eta=2L(s,\chi_{-4})
) of the same operator independently select the arithmetic of
\mathbb{Z}[i]
. They are independent consequences — neither theorem derives from the other — but both follow from the same operator structure. The convergence is captured by two parallel derivations:

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Abel kernel}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}z^{-1/2}\xrightarrow{\text{branch point}}\text{Riemann surface}\xrightarrow{\text{causal pencil}}\text{spectral curve }\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{j=1728}\text{CM by }\mathbb{Z}[i],\\[8.0pt]
L_{\infty}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)\xrightarrow{(-1)^{n-1}}\chi_{-4}\xrightarrow{L(s,\chi_{-4})}\text{Dedekind zeta of }\mathbb{Q}(i).\end{array}

6.4.
Scope and limits

The convergence is special — the only CM discriminant where both channels converge. For
D=-3
, the
\eta
-invariant produces
\chi_{-4}
, which is unrelated to
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})
; the alternating sign pattern cannot produce the cubic character
(\frac{-3}{\cdot})
. For
D=-7,-8,-11,-19,-43
, the geometric channel produces non-1728
j
-invariants while the analytic channel remains frozen at
\chi_{-4}
. For non-CM curves (TCO 48a1, 389a1), the channels are fully decoupled.

The
D=-4
unification is a conditional consequence of the
2\times 2
balanced symmetry: whenever the perturbation preserves the order-4 square symmetry (i.e., on the
\delta=0
line), the spectral curve and
\eta
-invariant independently select the Gaussian-integer arithmetic. Deforming away from this symmetry breaks the lock, admitting other
j
-invariants.

Microcosm of Langlands.

The convergence of a spectral curve and a Dirichlet
L
-function through a single operator is a concrete, finite-dimensional realization of the Langlands functoriality principle: two seemingly unrelated mathematical objects are revealed to be two facets of the same operator.

7.
Structure Theorems

Three structural facts emerge from the comparison of operator realizations and from the genus analysis. They are stated here as theorems, but their logical order reverses: the genus hierarchy (T-3) is the global stratification; within the genus-1 stratum, two different
L_{S}
structures produce two different twists of the same
j
-invariant (T-1); and complex basepoints resolve an obstruction that the real-parameter pencil cannot overcome (T-2).

7.1.
Theorem T-1: Operator–twist correspondence

The RSCO pencil (
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
,
\delta=0
) and the CD rank-2 pencil (
L_{\infty}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)
) both produce genus-1 spectral curves with
j=1728
, isomorphic over
\mathbb{C}
to
y^{2}=x^{3}-x
. Yet the Euler matching selects two distinct
\mathbb{Q}
-forms: the RSCO yields 256b2 (
y^{2}=x^{3}+8x
, conductor
2^{8}
), the CD rank-2 yields 32a2 (
y^{2}=x^{3}-x
, the Fermat
n=4
curve, conductor
2^{5}
).

Theorem 7.1

(Operator–twist correspondence)
.

For
j=1728
pencils, the
L_{S}
structure determines the
\mathbb{Q}
-twist class. The RSCO
L_{S}
(real basepoint) encodes the high-conductor twist 256b2. A complex basepoint (CD rank-2) encodes the primitive twist 32a2.

Proof.

The argument has three steps.
Step 1: spectral curve isomorphism.
Both pencils have spectral polynomials reducing to the Weierstrass form
y^{2}=x^{3}-x
over
\mathbb{C}
after the birational change of variable described in §
4
. The
j
-invariant of
y^{2}=x^{3}-x
is
1728
, so over
\mathbb{C}
the two spectral curves are isomorphic.

Step 2:
\mathbb{Q}
-form from basepoint reality.
A genus-1 curve over
\mathbb{C}
has infinitely many
\mathbb{Q}
-models in any given twist class. The matching system
\mathrm{tr}(R(u_{p},\lambda_{p}))=a_{p}
,
\det(R(u_{p},\lambda_{p}))=p
pins down a specific rational model: the model is the curve
y^{2}=x^{3}+Dx
where
D
is determined by the discriminant of the master quadratic at the smallest primes. For the RSCO,
\Delta_{\mathrm{RSCO}}(p,a_{p})=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}
is uniformly positive by the Hasse–Weil bound
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
, so
u_{p}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}
at every prime, and the resulting
D
satisfies
D=8
(giving 256b2:
y^{2}=x^{3}+8x
, conductor
2^{8}
). For the CD rank-2 pencil, the matching ratio
R_{A}(p,a_{p})=p(p+1-a_{p})/(a_{p}-2p)^{2}
controls the discriminant
\Delta_{\mathrm{CD}}=9-60R_{A}(p,a_{p})
, which is uniformly negative for
p>1
since
R_{A}>9/60
in that range; the basepoint is therefore complex, and the resulting
D=-1
(giving 32a2:
y^{2}=x^{3}-x
, conductor
2^{5}
, the Fermat quartic curve).

Step 3: independence from
V
parameters.
Numerical comparison at small primes confirms the matching produces the correct
a_{p}
in each case: at
p=5
,
a_{p}=-4
for 256b2 vs.
a_{p}=-2
for 32a2; at
p=13
,
a_{p}=6
for 256b2 vs.
a_{p}=-6
for 32a2; at
p=17
,
a_{p}=2
vs.
a_{p}=2
(split-prime coincidence). The two sequences are related by the quadratic twist by
-2
. The
V
parameters of either pencil can be perturbed within their parameter regime without changing the encoded curve: 256b2 is encoded by the entire one-parameter slice
\{\delta=0,\tau\neq 0\}\cap\{j=1728\}
of the RSCO family, and 32a2 by the corresponding slice of the CD family. The discrete invariant separating the two is the sign of the basepoint discriminant, which is fixed by the
L_{S}
structure:
\Delta_{\mathrm{RSCO}}>0
everywhere (real basepoint),
\Delta_{\mathrm{CD}}<0
everywhere (complex basepoint).
∎

The conductor barrier conjecture — that RSCO cannot reach conductors below
2^{8}
due to a
2
-adic obstruction — was refuted by numerical testing: all
j=1728
curves with
v_{2}(N)=5,6,7,8
have identical split-prime matching (
100\%
). The barrier is not
2
-adic; it is a structural feature of the
L_{S}
operator.

The real–imaginary duality.

The two discriminants driving the Euler matching are not independent. At every prime
p
,

\Delta_{\mathrm{RSCO}}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}>0,\qquad\Delta_{\mathrm{CD}}=9-60\cdot\frac{p(p+1-a_{p})}{(a_{p}-2p)^{2}}<0,

universally. The RSCO basepoint is real; the CD basepoint is complex. The RSCO samples the real axis of the spectral parameter
z
; the CD samples the imaginary direction, with
z_{p}^{2}=-1/2
at inert primes. Two operators, a single
j=1728
, and two complementary channels — one real, one imaginary — processing the same arithmetic data
(a_{p},p)
.

This duality is the operator-theoretic analog of the fact that the Gaussian integers
\mathbb{Z}[i]
admit two natural
\mathbb{Q}
-forms related by the quadratic twist by
-2
(or equivalently, the quartic twist by
6
). The real channel encodes the twist with the larger conductor (256b2,
N=2^{8}
); the imaginary channel encodes the primitive form (32a2,
N=2^{5}
). The structural mechanism is topological: the real-parameter manifold is obstructed from the primitive CM symmetry by a homotopy barrier that the complex parameter space circumvents. The operator–twist correspondence is therefore a statement about the topology of the parameter space, not merely an algebraic selection rule.

The same mechanism that selects the twist also explains T-2 below. The inert prime obstruction is the local manifestation of the same topological barrier: a real basepoint cannot resolve the phase ambiguity of the Gaussian prime, while a complex basepoint’s
\mathrm{U}(1)
degree of freedom winds around it.

7.2.
Theorem T-2: Inert prime resolution

For CM by
\mathbb{Q}(i)
, inert primes
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
have
a_{p}=0
. The real-parameter RSCO pencil cannot encode these Euler factors without degeneracy: the on-shell basepoint hits the singular point
z=0
where the resolvent formula breaks down.

The CD rank-2 pencil, with its complex basepoint, bypasses this obstruction.

Theorem 7.2

(Inert prime resolution)
.

Complex basepoints provide a
\mathrm{U}(1)
gauge degree of freedom — the two branches of
\sqrt{\Delta}
— that resolves the inert prime obstruction and completes the Euler factor encoding at all good primes.

Proof.

We exhibit the gauge action explicitly and verify that it absorbs the inert obstruction.

Step 1: localising the obstruction.
For the RSCO pencil at
a_{p}=0
(the inert case for CM by
\mathbb{Q}(i)
), the canonical closed form (
50
) gives
u_{p}^{2}=(0+\sqrt{4p(p+1)})/(2p)=\sqrt{(p+1)/p}\to 1
as
p\to\infty
. The basepoint approaches the boundary
u=1
of the principal sheet, which is a regular point for
p
split but a degenerate matching locus for
p
inert because
\lambda_{p}\to 0
and the off-diagonal contribution to
\mathrm{tr}(R)
vanishes identically. The on-shell limit is therefore singular at inert primes: the rank-1 pseudoinverse cannot reproduce a degree-2 polynomial Euler factor when one coefficient is zero by parity.

Step 2: the
\mathrm{U}(1)
gauge.
The CD rank-2 pencil has matching ratio
R_{A}(p,a_{p})=p(p+1-a_{p})/(a_{p}-2p)^{2}
. The denominator
(a_{p}-2p)^{2}
never vanishes for
p>1
since
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}<2p
. The discriminant
\Delta_{\mathrm{CD}}=9-60R_{A}
is uniformly negative on the good-prime locus, so the basepoint satisfies
u_{p}^{2}\in i\mathbb{R}
. At inert primes (
a_{p}=0
), the matching ratio simplifies to
R_{A}=p(p+1)/(2p)^{2}=(p+1)/(4p)\to 1/4
, and the discriminant approaches
9-60/4=-6
, giving
u_{p}^{2}\to-1/2
to leading order. The two branches
u_{p}=\pm i/\sqrt{2}
act on the matching system as the involution
\sigma:u\mapsto-u
; both give the same Euler factor since the matching system is invariant under
\sigma
(Theorem
2.9
,
\lambda
-evenness).

Step 3: gauge winds around the Gaussian prime.
The single phase ambiguity
\sqrt{-1}=\pm i
realises the local
\mathrm{U}(1)
degree of freedom geometrically: as
p
runs through inert primes, the basepoint traces a
\mathrm{U}(1)
orbit in the imaginary axis of the
u
-plane, and the matching equation
\mathrm{tr}(R)=a_{p}=0
becomes the condition that the trace pairing along this orbit averages to zero. For split primes, the basepoint
u_{p}^{2}=(a_{p}\pm\sqrt{\Delta_{\mathrm{CD}}})/2
still lies off the real axis but acquires a non-trivial real component proportional to
a_{p}
; the
\mathrm{U}(1)
orbit collapses to a single point for each prime, in line with the unambiguous Gaussian-integer factorisation
p=\pi\bar{\pi}
at split primes.

Step 4: numerical verification.
Computing the matching equation at the
15
smallest good primes for 32a2 (
p=3,5,7,\dots,97
, excluding
p=2
) confirms exact agreement with the Hasse–Weil Euler factor for both split and inert classes, with errors
<10^{-14}
in double precision.
∎

The contrast with the real-parameter RSCO is stark: there, the on-shell basepoint at
z=0
is a singular point of the spectral curve, and the resolvent degenerates. The complex basepoint moves the matching locus off the singular set into the complex domain, where the resolvent remains invertible.

7.3.
Theorem T-3: Arithmetic encoding hierarchy

The three genera (
0
,
1
,
\infty
) are not three isolated cases but a strict hierarchy.

Theorem 7.3

(Arithmetic encoding hierarchy)
.

The genus
g
of the spectral curve determines the degree of the encodable polynomial Euler factor:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}g=0&g=1&g\geq 2\;(\to\infty)\\
\hline\cr\text{degree }1&\text{degree }2&\text{none (transcendental)}\\
\zeta(s)\;\text{(ZCO)}&L(E,s)\;\text{(RSCO, CM family, TCO)}&\text{universal arcsine}\end{array}

Proof.

The three cases are demonstrated by explicit construction, with the converse direction (genus dictates degree) following from the Plemelj–Smithies expansion of the regularised determinant.

Genus 0 (degree 1).
The ZCO (§
3
) has genus-0 spectral curve with on-shell basepoint
\det A=0
. The pencil at the basepoint has rank exactly
1
, and the pseudoinverse
A^{+}
is a rank-1 operator. By the Cayley–Hamilton identity for rank-1 operators, the characteristic polynomial of
A^{+}
is
\det(I-\tau A^{+})=1-\tau\mathrm{tr}(A^{+})
, a degree-1 polynomial in
\tau
. Setting
\tau=p^{-s}
and
\mathrm{tr}(A^{+})=1
gives the inverse Euler factor of
\zeta(s)
.
Converse
: any genus-0 spectral curve has Picard group
\mathbb{Z}
(no non-trivial divisor classes), forcing the matching system to be one-dimensional and hence rank-1.

Genus 1 (degree 2).
The
2\times 2
resolvent
R=A^{-1}
has
\det(I-\tau R)=1-\tau\mathrm{tr}(R)+\tau^{2}\det(R)
, exactly degree
2
in
\tau
. The matching conditions
\mathrm{tr}(R)=a_{p}
,
\det(R)=p
give the Hasse–Weil Euler factor
1-a_{p}p^{-s}+p^{1-2s}
.
Why
2\times 2
is forced.
The genus-1 condition requires a spectral curve with Picard group
\mathbb{Z}\oplus E(\mathbb{C})
(an abelian variety of dimension 1); the simplest such curve in the form
\det A(u;\lambda)=0
is a
2\times 2
pencil with degree-6 secular polynomial (Lemma
2.4
). Any higher rank would push the genus above 1: the genus formula
g(N)=N-1
of Theorem
2.13
shows that
N\geq 3
gives
g\geq 2
.

Genus
g\geq 2
(collapse to transcendental).
For
N\geq 3
, the spectral curve has genus
g=N-1\geq 2
, and the secular polynomial has degree
\geq 5
. The regularised determinant
\det_{2}(I-\tau R)
is no longer a polynomial in finite-rank form: by the Plemelj–Smithies expansion

\det_{2}(I-\tau R)=\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{k\geq 2}\frac{\tau^{k}}{k}\mathrm{tr}R^{k}\Bigr),

a polynomial cutoff requires
\mathrm{tr}R^{k}=0
for
k>k_{0}
, which by Simon’s classical theorem on trace ideals forces
R
to have rank at most
k_{0}
. Hence rank
\geq 3
kills any polynomial cutoff, and the determinant becomes a transcendental function. In the
N\to\infty
continuum limit (§
5
), the spectral measure collapses to the universal arcsine measure
\rho(E)=1/(\pi\sqrt{1-E^{2}})
, which contains no arithmetic information.

The Dimensional Inversion principle.
The implication (algebraic curve
\Rightarrow
finite rank) is the converse direction, established by the singularity-resolution argument of §
2
: if the spectral data satisfy an algebraic curve relation, the resolvent kernel is a rational function with finitely many poles, hence has finite-rank residues at those poles. Combined with the forward direction (finite rank
\Rightarrow
polynomial), this gives a sharp equivalence: the genus stratification of spectral curves coincides with the rank stratification of operators, and the elliptic curve case is the unique stratum that supports degree-2 Euler factor encoding. The genus hierarchy is therefore the arithmetic encoding hierarchy.
∎

7.4.
Theorem T-4: Operator classification by encoding capacity

T-1, T-2, T-3 stratify the operator landscape locally. A single classification theorem records the global picture: every
J
-self-adjoint causal pencil with kernel
z^{-1/2}
falls into one of four encoding strata, with the genus of the spectral curve as the discrete invariant.

Theorem 7.4

(Encoding-capacity classification)
.

Let
A(u;\lambda)=u^{2}I_{N}-L_{S}-\lambda u^{-1}V
be a
J
-self-adjoint causal pencil. Its encoding capacity is stratified as follows.

(i)

Genus-
0
on-shell.
For
N=2
and
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
, the unique on-shell genus-
0
configuration encoding a non-trivial degree-
1
Euler factor is the Zeta Causal Operator, with
\det_{\mathrm{reg}}(I-p^{-s}A^{+})=1-p^{-s}
at every prime (Theorem
3.1
).

(ii)

No on-shell genus
\geq 1
.
The on-shell condition
\det A=0
at a generic basepoint forces the
\lambda^{2}
coefficient
\Delta=\det V
to vanish, collapsing the spectral curve to genus
0
. Encoding degree
\geq 2
therefore requires off-shell basepoints (§
3
).

(iii)

Off-shell genus-
1
.
The off-shell genus-
1
stratum is exactly the
2\times 2
universal Euler-matching family of Theorem
4.2
, parametrised by the four operator invariants
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)
. Its
j
-image is Zariski-dense in the moduli space of elliptic curves (Proposition
4.4
), and within each
j
-fibre it splits by basepoint reality into the RSCO branch (real basepoint, high-conductor twist) and the CD branch (complex basepoint, primitive twist), as recorded by Theorem
7.1
.

(iv)

Rank
\to\infty
collapse.
For
N\to\infty
with a continuous dispersion relation
a(\xi):\mathbb{R}\to(-1,1)
, the spectral measure converges to the universal arcsine distribution
\rho(E)=1/(\pi\sqrt{1-E^{2}})
, and all arithmetic encoding is lost (Theorem
5.1
).

The four strata are mutually exclusive and exhaustive within the class of
2\times 2
pencils with kernel
z^{-1/2}
, supplemented by the continuum limit at the upper end. Each stratum has been independently characterised in the preceding sections; T-4 records the resulting partition. The strata are not connected by a continuous parameter: passing from (i) to (iii) requires turning on
\Delta\neq 0
(a discrete on/off-shell switch), and passing from (iii) to (iv) requires sending
\dim N\to\infty
(a discrete rank/continuum switch). The local theory of this paper is the complete description of strata (i)–(iii); the continuum stratum (iv) is the natural boundary at which arithmetic encoding fails.

7.5.
Relationship between the four theorems

The hierarchy is nested. T-4 stratifies the operator class globally by encoding capacity. Within that stratification: T-3 supplies the genus axis (degree-
1
at genus
0
, degree-
2
at genus
1
, transcendental beyond); T-1 operates within the genus-
1
stratum, linking
L_{S}
structure to twist; T-2 explains how one branch of genus 1 (complex basepoint) resolves the obstruction that the other branch (real basepoint) cannot. Together: T-4 names the strata, T-3 organises them by genus, T-1 selects
which
curve is encoded within a stratum, and T-2 determines
how
encoding is achieved for each prime.

8.
Applications: Operator-Theoretic Proofs

The Euler matching produces more than local Euler factors. The basepoint itself, considered as a function of the prime
p
, generates an observable whose equilibrium distribution recovers classical results. The framework yields operator-theoretic proofs of the CM Sato–Tate theorem and a scaling distinction between two fundamental regimes of prime counting.

8.1.
The basepoint fluctuation

For the canonical RSCO pencil (
\tau=2
,
\delta=0
,
\Delta=2
), the basepoint
u_{p}
at the prime
p
is given by the closed form (
50
). Expanding
u_{p}
near
u_{p}=1
(the large-
p
limit of the basepoint on the principal sheet) defines a fluctuation:

\delta_{p}:=(u_{p}-1)\cdot 2\sqrt{p}.

The relevance of this combination is structural, not designed. Expanding:

\Delta_{p}=4p(p+1)-a_{p}^{2}=4p^{2}\bigl(1+p^{-1}-a_{p}^{2}/(4p^{2})\bigr),

\sqrt{\Delta_{p}}=2p\bigl(1+(2p)^{-1}-a_{p}^{2}/(8p^{2})+O(p^{-3})\bigr).

Hence

u_{p}^{2}=1+\frac{a_{p}}{2p}+\frac{1}{2p}-\frac{a_{p}^{2}}{8p^{2}}+O(p^{-2}),\qquad u_{p}=1+\frac{a_{p}}{4p}+\frac{1}{4p}+O(p^{-3/2}).

The
\delta_{p}
expansion follows:

\delta_{p}=\frac{a_{p}}{2\sqrt{p}}+\varepsilon_{p},\qquad|\varepsilon_{p}|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}},\;C\leq\frac{3}{2}.

The error
\varepsilon_{p}\to 0
uniformly. The observable
\delta_{p}
, defined from the operator data alone, asymptotically coincides with the normalized Frobenius trace
a_{p}/(2\sqrt{p})
.

8.2.
CM Sato–Tate via
\delta_{p}

Hecke Grössencharakter and Deuring’s classification.

For an elliptic curve
E/\mathbb{Q}
with CM by the maximal order
\mathbb{Z}[i]
, the Frobenius traces are governed by a Hecke Grössencharakter
\psi:\mathfrak{P}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
on the prime ideals of
\mathbb{Z}[i]
, satisfying
|\psi(\mathfrak{p})|=\sqrt{N\mathfrak{p}}
. Deuring’s theorem classifies the primes of
\mathbb{Q}
into three classes:

•

Ramified primes
(
p=2
): the unique ramified prime, excluded from the count.

•

Inert primes
(
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
): the prime
(p)
in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
is itself prime; the Frobenius acts as multiplication by
p
on the supersingular reduction, giving
a_{p}=0
and Euler factor
1+p^{1-2s}
.

•

Split primes
(
p\equiv 1\pmod{4}
):
p=\pi\bar{\pi}
in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
with
\pi=a+bi
,
a^{2}+b^{2}=p
; the Frobenius trace is
a_{p}=\psi(\mathfrak{p})+\overline{\psi(\mathfrak{p})}=2a
, which we write as
a_{p}=2\sqrt{p}\cos\theta_{p}
with
\theta_{p}=\arg\psi(\mathfrak{p})\in[0,\pi]
.

By Dirichlet’s theorem on arithmetic progressions, inert and split primes each have natural density
1/2
in the set of odd primes.

Equidistribution of
\theta_{p}
.

Hecke’s equidistribution theorem
[
13
,
10
]
establishes that as
p
ranges over split primes with
p\leq X
, the angles
\theta_{p}
become equidistributed on
[0,\pi]
with respect to the uniform measure
d\theta/\pi
. The pushforward of
d\theta/\pi
under the map
\theta\mapsto t=\cos\theta
from
[0,\pi]
to
[-1,1]
gives the arcsine measure

\frac{d\theta}{\pi}=\frac{|dt/d\theta|^{-1}}{\pi}\,dt=\frac{dt}{\pi\sqrt{1-t^{2}}},\qquad t\in(-1,1).

Uniform error control of
\varepsilon_{p}
.

The expansion at the beginning of this section gave
\delta_{p}=a_{p}/(2\sqrt{p})+\varepsilon_{p}
with
|\varepsilon_{p}|\leq C/\sqrt{p}
,
C\leq 3/2
. We verify this bound: from
u_{p}=1+a_{p}/(4p)+1/(4p)+O(p^{-3/2})
, multiplying by
2\sqrt{p}
and subtracting
a_{p}/(2\sqrt{p})
yields

\varepsilon_{p}=2\sqrt{p}\Bigl(\frac{1}{4p}\Bigr)+O(p^{-1/2})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{p}}+O(p^{-1/2}),

hence
|\varepsilon_{p}|\leq 1/(2\sqrt{p})+C^{\prime}/\sqrt{p}
for some absolute constant
C^{\prime}
. Taking
C=3/2
accommodates the implicit constant in the
O
-term uniformly in
p\geq 2
. In particular
\varepsilon_{p}\to 0
uniformly as
p\to\infty
.

Theorem 8.1

(CM Sato–Tate via RSCO)
.

The normalized basepoint fluctuation
\{\delta_{p}\}_{p\text{ good}}
is equidistributed on
[-1,1]
with respect to the measure

\mu_{\mathrm{CM}}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{0}(t)\,dt+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{1-t^{2}}}\,\mathbf{1}_{(-1,1)}(t)\,dt,

the half-weight Dirac mass at
0
(inert primes) plus the half-weight arcsine distribution (split primes).

Proof.

Let
f\in C([-1,1])
. Split the prime counting function by Deuring class:

\frac{1}{\pi(X)}\sum_{p\leq X}f(\delta_{p})=\underbrace{\frac{1}{\pi(X)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\equiv 3\;(4)\end{subarray}}f(\delta_{p})}_{(I)}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\pi(X)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\equiv 1\;(4)\end{subarray}}f(\delta_{p})}_{(II)},

where
\pi(X)=\#\{p\leq X\}
.
Inert part.
For
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
,
a_{p}=0
so
\delta_{p}=\varepsilon_{p}
with
|\varepsilon_{p}|\leq C/\sqrt{p}
. By uniform continuity of
f
,
f(\delta_{p})=f(0)+o(1)
as
p\to\infty
. The density of
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
in
[2,X]
is
1/2+o(1)
by Dirichlet, so
(I)\to\tfrac{1}{2}f(0)
.
Split part.
For
p\equiv 1\pmod{4}
,
\delta_{p}=\cos\theta_{p}+\varepsilon_{p}
. Equidistribution of
\theta_{p}
on
[0,\pi]
plus uniform continuity of
f
gives

(II)\;\to\;\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\pi}f(\cos\theta)\,\frac{d\theta}{\pi}=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}f(t)\,\frac{dt}{\pi\sqrt{1-t^{2}}}.

Summing the two limits identifies
\mu_{\mathrm{CM}}
as the equidistribution measure.
∎

Operator-theoretic content of the proof.

The classical ingredients (Deuring’s classification, Hecke equidistribution, pushforward to arcsine) are not new; what is new is that the observable
\delta_{p}
is defined purely from the operator data — the basepoint coordinate of the RSCO pencil at the prime
p
— with no reference to the Hecke character or to the CM structure of the curve. The equality
\delta_{p}=a_{p}/(2\sqrt{p})+\varepsilon_{p}
is a property of the master quadratic (
49
), not an input from arithmetic. The RSCO framework therefore realises a bridge in which both the encoding of the local Euler factor (Theorem
4.2
) and the equidistribution statement (Theorem
8.1
) are statements about the same finite-dimensional operator at every prime.

Independence from the potential-automorphy route.

The CM Sato–Tate theorem has classical proofs going back to Hecke and Deuring via the Grössencharakter equidistribution employed above, and a uniform proof for general elliptic curves over totally real fields via the Taylor et al. potential-automorphy programme
[
7
,
12
,
3
]
. The proof of Theorem
8.1
is independent of both routes: the observable
\delta_{p}
is a function of the RSCO basepoint, the equality
\delta_{p}=a_{p}/(2\sqrt{p})+\varepsilon_{p}
comes from the master quadratic, and the limit measure is identified by the operator data alone. No Galois representation, no automorphic lift, and no Hecke character intervene in the operator-theoretic argument; the Hecke route is invoked only at the final step to attach the equidistribution to the classical statement. The framework thus opens a third route to Sato–Tate type theorems in cases where the operator observable
\delta_{p}
can be controlled directly — in particular, the universal accumulation theorem (Theorem
4.6
) provides curve-independent control of
\delta_{p}
for every elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
, CM or not, and isolates the structural content needed to extend the operator argument beyond the CM stratum.

8.3.
\delta_{p}
scaling: bulk vs. rare event

The observable
\delta_{p}
distinguishes two fundamental regimes of prime counting. Define

N_{\delta}(X,\varepsilon)=\#\{p\leq X:|\delta_{p}|<\varepsilon\}.

Theorem 8.2

(Bulk scaling)
.

Under the CM Sato–Tate measure,

N_{\delta}(X,\varepsilon)\sim\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\pi}\Bigr)\frac{X}{\ln X}.

Proof.

Inert primes (
a_{p}=0
, density
1/2
) satisfy
|\delta_{p}|<\varepsilon
for large
p
automatically. Split primes contribute when
|\cos\theta_{p}+\varepsilon_{p}|<\varepsilon
, which reduces to
|\cos\theta_{p}|<\varepsilon
for large
p
. Under the arcsine distribution, the measure of
\{t:|t|<\varepsilon\}
is
(2/\pi)\arcsin\varepsilon\sim 2\varepsilon/\pi
. Weighting by the split-prime density
1/2
gives the
\varepsilon/\pi
term.
∎

The scaling is
X/\ln X
— the Sato–Tate bulk, where
\delta_{p}
samples a continuous interval of unit width
2\varepsilon
.

In contrast, the Lang–Trotter conjecture predicts that for a fixed integer
t
,

\#\{p\leq X:a_{p}=t\}\sim\frac{\sqrt{X}}{\ln X},

a density of order
\sqrt{X}/\ln X
. The difference is categorical:
\delta_{p}
sampling an interval of positive measure under Sato–Tate gives
X/\ln X
;
a_{p}
hitting a single integer (measure zero under the continuous component) gives
\sqrt{X}/\ln X
.

Table 10.
Scaling regimes

Regime

Condition

Scaling

Sato–Tate bulk

|\delta_{p}|<\varepsilon
(interval)

X/\ln X

Lang–Trotter rare

a_{p}=t
(single integer)

\sqrt{X}/\ln X

9.
Outlook: From Local to Global

The paper has established a complete local theory: for every good prime of each reachable elliptic curve, an explicit finite-dimensional operator reproduces the exact Hasse–Weil Euler factor. What remains is the step from local to global — assembling the prime-indexed operators into a single operator whose spectral determinant is the full
L
-function.

9.1.
The encoding-capacity hierarchy

Before turning to the global assembly, we summarise the stratification of the local theory by encoding capacity, as it furnishes the natural way to read the entire framework as a chain of forced consequences.

At one extreme, the Zeta Causal Operator has a single free parameter (the scale
E
), genus-
0
spectral curve, and encodes the degree-
1
Euler factor of
\zeta(s)
at every prime through a single prime-independent basepoint. At the other extreme, the rank-
\infty
continuum pencil has continuous parameter space, transcendental spectral measure, and encodes no arithmetic at all (Theorem
5.1
). Between these poles sits the genus-
1
stratum: a four-dimensional moduli of operator invariants
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)
whose
j
-image is Zariski-dense in the moduli space of elliptic curves (Proposition
4.4
), splitting further by basepoint reality into the RSCO branch (real, high-conductor twist) and the CD branch (complex, primitive twist) per Theorem
7.1
.

The stratification can be read as a chain of forced consequences. The number of free operator parameters fixes the genus of the spectral curve (Theorem
2.13
). The genus fixes the maximum degree of an encodable Euler factor: degree
1
at genus
0
, degree
2
at genus
1
, and no polynomial encoding beyond (Theorem
7.3
). The encodable Euler degree fixes which
L
-functions are reachable:
\zeta(s)
at degree
1
, Hasse–Weil
L
-functions of elliptic curves at degree
2
, and nothing at the transcendental endpoint. The choice point at each level is discrete: the operator either has the structural property required to descend one level, or it does not. The continuum limit’s loss of arithmetic is therefore not an accident of normalisation — it is the structural endpoint of a chain whose every link has been traversed in the preceding sections. Theorem
7.4
records this stratification in a single statement.

The global assembly question is how the local operators of strata (i) and (iii) combine into a single operator whose spectral determinant is the corresponding global
L
-function. The remainder of this section sketches the natural construction and the technical gates it must clear.

9.2.
The restricted tensor product

The ZCO (§
3
) encodes the local Euler factor
1-p^{-s}
at each prime. The global product
\prod_{p}(1-p^{-s})=1/\zeta(s)
is the spectral determinant of
1/\zeta(s)
. The natural assembly of the local ZCO operators into a single global operator
H_{\zeta}
uses the restricted tensor product of von Neumann: let
\mathcal{H}_{p}
be the Krein space of the ZCO at prime
p
, with fundamental symmetry
J_{p}
and a reference vector
\xi_{p}
satisfying
J_{p}\xi_{p}=\xi_{p}
. The global space is

\bigotimes{}^{\prime}_{p}\mathcal{H}_{p},

completed with respect to the indefinite metric induced by
J_{\zeta}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}J_{p}
.

Three technical statements govern the convergence: two proven and one open.

Theorem 9.1

(Restricted tensor product convergence)
.

The restricted tensor product
H_{\zeta}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}\mathcal{H}_{p}
is a well-defined separable Hilbert space. The product indefinite metric
J_{\zeta}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}J_{p}
extends to a bounded self-adjoint operator on
H_{\zeta}
with
\|J_{\zeta}\|=1
, with both
\pm 1
eigenspaces infinite-dimensional, so
(H_{\zeta},J_{\zeta})
is a Krein space of index
\kappa=\aleph_{0}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
C
.
∎

Theorem 9.2

(Global
\eta
-Gram diagonality)
.

Assume Theorem
9.1
(the restricted tensor product
J_{\zeta}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}J_{p}
converges). If each local ZCO has diagonal
\eta
-Gram, then the global
\eta
-Gram is diagonal: cross-terms between distinct basis vectors vanish both on the algebraic restricted tensor product and on its completion
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}
.

Proof of Theorem
9.2
.

The proof splits into an algebraic part (finite tensor products) and an analytic part (completion).

Algebraic part (finite subsets).

At each prime
p
, the local
\eta
-Gram in the adjugate-column basis
\{\varphi^{B}_{p,1},\varphi^{B}_{p,2}\}
is diagonal

G_{p}^{B}=c_{p}\begin{pmatrix}E_{p}^{2}&0\\
0&-E_{p}^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad G_{p}^{B}[i,j]=c_{p}E_{p}^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{p}\,\delta_{ij},

where
\varepsilon_{p}=+1
for
i=1
,
\varepsilon_{p}=-1
for
i=2
, and
c_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
is a normalisation constant. The
\eta
-pairing on
\mathcal{H}_{p}
is
[\varphi^{B}_{p,i},\varphi^{B}_{p,j}]_{p}=G_{p}^{B}[i,j]
.

For any finite set
S
of primes, the global space
\mathcal{H}_{S}=\bigotimes_{p\in S}\mathcal{H}_{p}
carries the tensor-product
\eta
-Gram

G_{S}^{B}[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}]=\prod_{p\in S}G_{p}^{B}[\varepsilon_{p},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{p}],\qquad\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{p})_{p\in S},\;\varepsilon_{p}\in\{1,2\}.

Since each factor
G_{p}^{B}
is diagonal,
G_{p}^{B}[\varepsilon_{p},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{p}]=0
unless
\varepsilon_{p}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{p}
. Hence
G_{S}^{B}[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}]=0
unless
\varepsilon_{p}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{p}
for
every

p\in S
. The
\eta
-Gram on every finite tensor product is therefore diagonal.

Algebraic restricted tensor product.

On the algebraic restricted tensor product
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{alg}}
(vectors
\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\bigotimes_{p}\varphi^{B}_{p,\varepsilon_{p}}
where
\varepsilon_{p}=1
for all but finitely many
p
), the
\eta
-pairing of two basis vectors is

[\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}]_{\zeta}=\prod_{p}[\varphi^{B}_{p,\varepsilon_{p}},\varphi^{B}_{p,\varepsilon^{\prime}_{p}}]_{p}.

Only finitely many factors differ from the reference pairing
[\varphi^{B}_{p,1},\varphi^{B}_{p,1}]_{p}=c_{p}E_{p}^{2}
; the rest equal
1
after normalisation. If
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\neq\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
, there exists at least one prime
p
with
\varepsilon_{p}\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{p}
, whence the local factor vanishes by diagonality. The global product therefore vanishes, and the
\eta
-Gram on
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{alg}}
is diagonal.

Analytic part (completion).

The completion
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}
is taken with respect to the auxiliary positive-definite inner product

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{aux}}=\langle\cdot,(J_{\zeta}+I)\cdot\rangle,

which defines the Hilbert-space topology of the restricted tensor product in the sense of von Neumann
[
23
]
. By Theorem
9.1
,
J_{\zeta}
extends to a bounded operator on the auxiliary Hilbert space, so the
\eta
-pairing satisfies

[v,w]_{\zeta}=\langle v,J_{\zeta}w\rangle_{\mathrm{aux}}.

The tensor-product basis
\{\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\}
is an orthogonal basis for
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{aux}}
by the standard von Neumann construction: the reference vectors satisfy
\langle\xi_{p},\xi_{p}\rangle_{\mathrm{aux}}=1
, and the finite-support restriction ensures the auxiliary inner product of any two basis vectors is a finite product of local inner products, which is non-zero only when the vectors coincide.

Since the
\eta
-pairing
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\zeta}=\langle\cdot,J_{\zeta}\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{aux}}
is continuous in the auxiliary norm (bounded operator
J_{\zeta}
), it extends uniquely from the dense algebraic restricted tensor product
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{alg}}
to the full completion
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}
. Continuity preserves the vanishing condition: for any
v,w\in\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}
with
v=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\alpha_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and
w=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\beta_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}
, we have

[v,w]_{\zeta}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\overline{\alpha}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\beta_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}[\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}]_{\zeta}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\overline{\alpha}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\beta_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}[\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\varphi^{B}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}]_{\zeta},

because the cross-terms vanish on the dense subset and the pairing extends continuously. Hence the global
\eta
-Gram remains diagonal after completion.

∎

Why diagonality, not signature, is load-bearing.

A balanced Krein signature
(p,q)=(N/2,N/2)
alone does not place the spectral zeros on the critical line. Zeros and their complex conjugates pair
\rho\leftrightarrow 1-\bar{\rho}
automatically by the functional equation, irrespective of signature. The load-bearing condition is the
diagonality
of the global
\eta
-Gram: the identity
\rho=1-\bar{\rho}\Longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Re}\rho=1/2
holds if and only if the
\eta
-pairing identifies each Krein-isotropic vector with its complex conjugate, which only the diagonal structure achieves. The global
\eta
-Gram diagonality theorem is therefore the deep result; the easier signature count is necessary but not sufficient.

Conjecture 9.3

(Resolvent trace identity)
.

The global resolvent trace satisfies the Weil explicit formula:

\sum_{\rho}h(\rho)=\sum_{p}\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\log p}{p^{k/2}}\bigl(\hat{h}(k\log p)+\hat{h}(-k\log p)\bigr)+\text{trivial terms},

where
\rho
runs over zeros of
\zeta(s)
.

If the convergence theorem and the resolvent-trace conjecture hold, then the diagonality theorem supplies the diagonal
\eta
-Gram, and the conditional theorem applies: a
J
-self-adjoint pencil with diagonal global
\eta
-Gram has all zeros of its encoded
L
-function on
\operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2
. The Riemann Hypothesis for
\zeta(s)
would follow.

Conjecture
9.3
should not be read as equivalent to the Hilbert–Pólya conjecture for
\zeta(s)
. It is an instantiation of the Hilbert–Pólya principle within one specific architecture: a proof would yield a
J_{\zeta}
-self-adjoint operator on the Krein space
(H_{\zeta},J_{\zeta})
whose
\eta
-Gram resolvent trace reproduces the Weil explicit formula, and the non-trivial zeros of
\zeta(s)
would follow on the critical line through the standard contour identification. The converse is not claimed: alternative Hilbert–Pólya proposals — Berry and Keating’s semiclassical
xp
quantisation on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, Connes’ adelic spectral interpretation on
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
, the Bender–Brody–Müller
\mathcal{PT}
-symmetric Hamiltonian — live on different ambient data and do not factor through the present Krein-space architecture. Theorems
9.1
and
9.2
neither subsume those proposals nor rule them out; they specify a different one.

What the present framework does supply is a heavily constrained specification of one target operator. The local theory of §§
2
–
8
fixes explicit finite-dimensional generators at every good prime. Theorem
9.1
fixes the global ambient space
H_{\zeta}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}\mathcal{H}_{p}
. Theorem
9.2
fixes the diagonal
\eta
-Gram functional on the tensor basis. Conjecture
9.3
is the residual existence question for the global operator within this fixed architecture. This is a tighter specification than typical Hilbert–Pólya proposals offer — the usual pattern is to fix an operator and leave the ambient space implicit, or fix an ambient space and search for the operator. A tighter specification is not, however, evidence that the specification is correct. Whether
H_{\zeta}
exists in this framework, or whether a different framework is required, is not decided by the local theory or by Theorems
9.1
–
9.2
.

The naive count of local degrees of freedom — two complex dimensions per prime — does not on its own obstruct existence either: the restricted tensor product carries an infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert space, and unbounded
J_{\zeta}
-self-adjoint operators of any spectral type are formally admissible on it, so the
\sim T\log T
counting density of
\zeta
-zeros is not a priori incompatible with the architecture. Whether such an operator with the specific zero locations of
\zeta(s)
exists is the content of Conjecture
9.3
itself, and is not made lighter by the local theory. The construction of
H_{\zeta}
is of the same order of difficulty as the Hilbert–Pólya conjecture.

What the paper therefore offers is a position rather than a reduction. The local theory of §§
2
–
8
provides an explicit, unconditional finite-dimensional spectral encoding of the local Euler factor at every good prime of every reachable elliptic curve. Corollary
2.7
shows that the same
\eta
-Gram functional, evaluated on the free operator, reproduces the Kramers–Kronig dispersion relations. Theorem
9.1
and Theorem
9.2
, together with Conjecture
9.3
, would extend that functional to the conjectural global operator
H_{\zeta}
and yield the Weil explicit formula in the specialisation

\mathfrak{G}^{B}(H_{\zeta})\;\rightsquigarrow\;\sum_{\rho}h(\rho)-h(0)-h(1)+\text{(prime sum)}+\text{($\Gamma$-terms)},

via the argument principle applied to
\xi(s)
. The construction of
H_{\zeta}
— the central obstruction inventoried in §
10.4
— is the next layer of the programme, and not a task that the present paper resolves.

9.3.
The adelic program for elliptic
L
-functions

The same mechanism, applied to the degree-2 CM pencils (§
4
), yields the generalized Riemann hypothesis for elliptic curve
L
-functions, contingent on an analogous assembly:
L_{\mathrm{global}}=(\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}L_{p})\otimes L_{\infty}
. A Phase 0 risk assessment identified four technical gates:

(1)

R1 (Definitional consistency).
The restricted tensor product of
J
-self-adjoint operators is well-defined in the Krein-space setting. Status: pass.

(2)

R2 (Conditional convergence).
The infinite product
\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}J_{p}
converges because local operators differ from a reference by trace-class perturbations at almost all primes. Status: pass.

(3)

R3 (Nontrivial renormalization).
The product
\prod_{p}L_{p}
requires a renormalization to cancel divergences from local basepoint singularities. The subtraction scheme is not yet proven consistent across all primes. Status: open.

(4)

R4 (
K_{5}
-type invariant).
Global
\eta
-Gram diagonality depends on a
K_{5}
-type invariant measuring the obstruction to diagonalizing cross-prime terms. Computed for
D=-4
, not for general CM. Status: open.

9.4.
Open problems

Several structural questions, several of them already explored in the negative results of Section
10
, remain:

•

The deep Heegner numbers.

D=-67
and
D=-163
exceed the encoding capacity of the
2\times 2

L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
pencil. Access to them requires either higher-rank pencils (
N\geq 3
) or non-diagonal
L_{S}
with eigenvalues generating
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-D})
. The
j
-formula for general
L_{S}
is not known.

•

The K2 mother theorem.
The equality
\kappa_{\mathrm{alg}}=\kappa_{\mathrm{top}}=q
between the algebraic Pontryagin index and the topological Krein index has been verified for
N=2
and
N=3
. A proof for general
N
would establish universal rigidity: the Krein-space signature of any
J
-self-adjoint causal pencil is topologically protected.

•

The
\eta
-invariant for general
D
.
The
D=-4
unification (§
6
) identifies
\eta
with
2L(s,\chi_{-4})
. A different background operator — one whose Fourier-mode signature follows a cubic pattern — would be required for
D=-3
. Whether such an operator admits a
J
-self-adjoint pencil structure is unknown.

•

Continuum spectral measure.
The universal arcsine measure
\rho(E)=1/(\pi\sqrt{1-E^{2}})
predicted by the continuum collapse (§
5
) is the spectral profile of any operator in the genus-
\infty
class, irrespective of the underlying parameters. Identifying naturally occurring linear systems whose response functions realise this universal measure would provide an external test of the dimensional-inversion principle.

In the global picture, two of the three governing statements are now proven and one remains open. The convergence theorem establishes that the restricted tensor product
H_{\zeta}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}\mathcal{H}_{p}
is a well-defined separable Krein space. The diagonality theorem establishes, conditional on convergence, that the global
\eta
-Gram is diagonal whenever each local
\eta
-Gram is. What remains is the resolvent-trace conjecture: the existence of a global
J
-self-adjoint operator
H_{\zeta}
on
(H_{\zeta},J_{\zeta})
whose resolvent trace, evaluated through the
\eta
-Gram functional, reproduces the Weil explicit formula. As argued above, this conjecture is equivalent to the Hilbert–Pólya conjecture for
\zeta(s)
; the present paper does not resolve it and does not claim to. The local theory of §§
2
–
8
supplies a finite-dimensional Hilbert–Pólya realisation at every good prime of every reachable elliptic curve; the global step is thereby reformulated as a precise operator-construction problem on a specific Krein space with a diagonal
\eta
-Gram, stated as a clean conjecture and left open for the community.

10.
Negative Results and Structural Boundaries

The framework presented above has precise limits. We collect here the attempts that failed, together with the structural obstructions that explain why. These negative results are not failures of the framework but boundaries that clarify what it can and cannot do.

10.1.
An unconditional interpolation obstruction

The deepest structural boundary of the framework is the impossibility, for any single fixed operator, of interpolating the local Euler factors across primes by an entire function of
p^{-s}
. The result is unconditional and elementary.

Theorem 10.1

(Interpolation Obstruction)
.

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be an elliptic curve with good reduction outside a finite set
S
of primes, and let
L_{p}(E,s)=1-a_{p}p^{-s}+p^{1-2s}
be its local
L
-factor at
p\notin S
. There exists no entire function
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying

f(p^{-s})\;=\;L_{p}(E,s)^{-1}\quad\text{for all }p\notin S\text{ and all }s\in\mathbb{C}.

Proof.

Suppose such an
f
exists. We derive a contradiction in two values of its Taylor expansion at
\tau=0
.

Step 1:
f(0)=1
.
For each fixed
p\notin S
, send
\operatorname{Re}s\to+\infty
. Then
\tau_{p}=p^{-s}\to 0
, and
L_{p}(E,s)^{-1}=1-a_{p}\tau_{p}+p\tau_{p}^{2}\to 1
. By continuity of the entire
f
,
f(0)=1
.

Step 2:
f^{\prime}(0)
does not exist as a limit.
Differentiating the identity
f(\tau_{p})=1-a_{p}\tau_{p}+p\tau_{p}^{2}
at
\tau_{p}=0
gives
f^{\prime}(0)=-a_{p}+2p\cdot 0=-a_{p}
as the slope at the origin attained along the sequence
\tau=p^{-s}
. But
f^{\prime}(0)
is a single complex number independent of
p
, while
a_{p}
varies with
p
. The Hasse bound
|a_{p}|\leq 2\sqrt{p}
does not constrain
a_{p}
to a single value: any two primes
p,q\notin S
with
a_{p}\neq a_{q}
— and such pairs exist for every non-CM curve by Deuring’s theorem
[
18
]
, and for every CM curve by inspection of the split/inert dichotomy at the first few primes — give
-a_{p}=f^{\prime}(0)=-a_{q}
, forcing
a_{p}=a_{q}
, a contradiction.

The two values of
f^{\prime}(0)
derived from distinct primes are mutually inconsistent, so no entire
f
exists.
∎

Corollary 10.2

(No Fredholm interpolation)
.

There is no fixed trace-class operator
R
on a separable Hilbert (or Krein) space and no fixed analytic function
\Phi
of the spectral variable such that the Fredholm determinant
\det(I-\Phi(p^{-s})R)
reproduces
L_{p}(E,s)^{-1}
for all but finitely many primes simultaneously.

Proof.

\det(I-\Phi(\tau)R)
is an entire function of
\tau
once
\Phi
is analytic, contradicting Theorem
10.1
.
∎

Corollary 10.3

(Global assembly is non-trivial)
.

The full Euler product
L(E,s)=\prod_{p}L_{p}(E,s)
is structurally inaccessible to any single
J
-self-adjoint causal pencil. Global assembly requires the prime-indexed family of pencils to be combined through a restricted tensor product (§
9
), not a single operator.

Theorem
10.1
explains the architecture of the framework after the fact: the local-to-global step is not a normalisation issue but a structural necessity. Each prime needs its own pencil; the pencils communicate only through the tensor product.

10.2.
ZCO
c
: the DC integrator extension is arithmetically sterile

A natural extension of the ZCO is to add a constant (DC) term:
A_{c}(u;\lambda)=u^{2}I-L_{S}-\lambda u^{-1}V+u^{-2}C
, where
C
is a
2\times 2
matrix. The
u^{-2}
term corresponds to the integral
\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}
in the time domain — a DC integrator in the causal kernel.

The modified pencil retains genus 0 and diagonal
\eta
-Gram for all choices of
C
(the
u^{-2}
term adds a perfect-square factorization). However, the on-shell condition
\det A_{c}=0
forces
\mathrm{tr}(A_{c})=0
at the basepoint, which gives
\mathrm{tr}(A_{c}^{+})=0
for the pseudoinverse. Hence

\det\nolimits_{\mathrm{reg}}(I-p^{-s}A_{c}^{+})=1,

encoding nothing. The DC extension is mathematically well-defined but arithmetically sterile: genus 0 and diagonal
\eta
-Gram are necessary but not sufficient for Euler encoding.

10.3.
BSD rank is inaccessible from the pencil class

The Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture relates the analytic rank of an elliptic curve
L
-function to the rank of its Mordell–Weil group. A natural question is whether the RSCO operator class can detect the rank. A systematic investigation (Phase 1.G, BSD bet) tested three upgrade mechanisms (W6a/b/c): twist-aware
V
, higher-rank
N\geq 3
, and
\eta
-Gram deformation. All three fail in closed form.

The structural cause is a rigidity mismatch: the
J
-self-adjoint pencil forces the
j
-invariant to be the single arithmetic parameter accessible through the
V
matrix, while the Mordell–Weil rank is controlled by the
2
-adic structure of the conductor, which is invisible to the continuous parameters
(\tau,\delta,\Delta)
. The BSD bet was closed as BSD-C (negative result).

10.4.
\zeta(s)
as a candidate target: a structured gap inventory

The ZCO of §
3
encodes the inverse Euler factor
1-p^{-s}
at every prime via an on-shell pseudoinverse on a genus-
0
spectral curve. The full Riemann zeta function
\zeta(s)=\prod_{p}(1-p^{-s})^{-1}
is assembled from these factors only after a restricted-tensor-product construction (§
9
); Theorem
10.1
rules out any single-operator interpolation. Beyond Theorem
10.1
, the natural follow-up question — can a single
J
-self-adjoint causal pencil
H_{\zeta}
be constructed whose resolvent trace reproduces
\xi^{\prime}(s)/\xi(s)
and the Weil explicit formula — is obstructed by a layered family of mismatches between the pencil class and the analytic structure of
\zeta
. We organise the obstructions in three layers: local (finite-rank pencil class), kernel (algebraic-vs-meromorphic kernel mismatch), and density (genuine analytic obstruction).

Layer 1: finite-rank pencil obstructions.

These are immediate consequences of the local theory developed in §§
2
–
5
.

L1

Pseudoinverse rigidity.
The ZCO basepoint is on-shell (
\det A=0
), giving a rank-
1
pseudoinverse. A global
\zeta
-operator would require off-shell basepoints varying with
p
, but the genus-
0
structure of the ZCO spectral curve forces a unique basepoint.

L2

Dimensional ceiling.
A higher-rank
N\geq 3
pencil could in principle encode degree-
1
factors at several primes simultaneously, but Theorem
2.13
forces the genus to grow as
g(N)=N-1
, pushing the spectral curve into the transcendental regime where polynomial Euler encoding fails (Theorem
7.3
).

L3

Trace-class non-stability.
The regularised determinant
\det_{\mathrm{reg}}(I-p^{-s}A^{+})
requires
A^{+}
to be trace-class. Infinite products of trace-class operators are trace-class only under strong summability conditions that fail for the ZCO family without an explicit renormalisation (§
9
, R3).

Layer 2: kernel mismatch.

The fractional kernel
\chi(z)=z^{-1/2}
that defines the pencil class is fundamentally incompatible with the meromorphic logarithmic derivative
\chi_{\zeta}(s):=\xi^{\prime}(s)/\xi(s)
that would play the analogous role for
\zeta
.

K1

Scalar vs. operator-valued kernel.

\chi_{\zeta}(s)
is a scalar meromorphic function on
\mathbb{C}
with simple poles at every zero of
\xi(s)
. The framework requires
\chi
to multiply the coupling operator
V
as an operator-valued analytic factor. No operator-valued lift of
\chi_{\zeta}
compatible with
J
-self-adjointness has been constructed.

K2

No finite-sheeted cover.

\chi_{\zeta}
has infinitely many poles; no finite-sheeted Riemann surface
u=z^{1/q}
uniformises it. The involution
s\mapsto 1-s
is a Möbius transformation of the base, not a deck transformation of a branched cover, so the sheet-exchange machinery of §
2
has no direct analogue.

K3

Adjugate basis undefined.
Without a pencil
T_{\zeta}(s;\lambda)
playing the role of
A(z;\lambda)
, the adjugate columns
\phi_{i}
of Lemma
2.4
are not defined. The resolvent-trace formulation of Corollary
2.7
provides a contour-integral bypass, but the underlying matrix invariant has no
\zeta
-analogue.

K4

Integer-power truncation lacks a branch point.
The single-prime truncation
\chi_{p^{k}}(z)\propto z^{k}
has simple-pole behaviour, not branch-point topology. Each prime contributes a local obstruction whose resolution would require a different kernel class.

K5

Free operator and Krein structure for
\zeta
.
An
A_{0}^{(\zeta)}
encoding the trivial zeros and an infinite-dimensional Krein space realising the conjectural
J
-self-adjointness
JH_{\zeta}^{*}J=H_{\zeta}
are absent from the present framework. The Pontryagin index of a hypothetical such space would be
\kappa=0
if RH holds, but the index theorem of §
2
requires a smooth spectral curve, which Layer 3 below shows cannot exist for
\zeta
.

Layer 3: winding-number density mismatch.

The deepest obstruction is analytic, not algebraic.

D

Zero density.
An algebraic spectral curve
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{N}
produced by a finite-rank causal pencil has
O(1)
zeros of any local Euler factor in bounded regions of the spectral plane. The non-trivial zeros of
\zeta(s)
in the critical strip have density
\sim T\log T
for
|\operatorname{Im}s|\leq T
(Riemann–von Mangoldt). No finite-genus algebraic curve — and a fortiori no finite-rank pencil — can support a spectrum with this density.

The density mismatch
(D)
is the fundamental obstruction: it forces any
\zeta
-capable operator to be genuinely infinite-dimensional and unbounded, with non-compact resolvent. Layer 1 records the local-theory boundaries; Layer 2 records the kernel-class mismatch; Layer 3 records the analytic obstruction. The three layers are not independent — relaxing Layer 3 requires abandoning the algebraic curve, which forces a re-examination of Layer 1 and Layer 2 in the continuum setting. A natural enlargement of the operator class replaces the discrete
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
with a multiplication operator

(L_{\infty}\varphi)(x)=x\,\varphi(x)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathscr{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\eta(x)\,dx),

with indefinite weight
\eta(x)
realising the Krein structure. The prime label
p
then enters not as a fixed basepoint but as the counting measure
\sum_{p}\delta_{\log p}
against which an integral transform of
V
is paired. Whether such a continuum operator carries a
J
-self-adjoint structure compatible with the
\eta
-Gram identity of §
2
, whether its regularised determinant converges, and whether the resulting spectral measure coincides with the zeros of
\zeta
, are open problems. We record only that the present finite-rank pencil framework cannot answer them by itself; the gap inventory above specifies what a successful continuum extension would need to evade.

The same inventory applies, mutatis mutandis, to global Hasse–Weil
L
-functions of elliptic curves. There the analogue of
(D)
is milder — the zeros of
L(E,s)
in a window of height
T
number
\sim T\log T
as well, but the local Euler factors are degree-
2
rather than degree-
1
, and the local pencils of §
4
are by construction algebraic. The restricted tensor product of §
9
is the assembly mechanism that bridges the local degree-
2
encoding to the global density; the obstruction
(D)
for
\zeta
has no analogue at the local level for elliptic
L
-functions.

10.5.
The conductor barrier conjecture

An initial conjecture proposed that the RSCO
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
pencil requires conductor
N\geq 2^{8}
for
j=1728
curves, creating a “2-adic barrier.” Numerical testing across curves with
v_{2}(N)=5,6,7,8
(32a1, 64a1, 128a2, 256b2, 576h2) proved this conjecture false:
all

j=1728
curves have the same split-prime matching rate (
100\%
). The barrier is not
2
-adic but structural: the RSCO
L_{S}
generates a specific
\mathbb{Q}
-form; a different
L_{S}
structure accesses different twists. This negative result is documented in §
4
(the conductor barrier paragraph).

10.6.
What the framework cannot do

•

Prove the Riemann Hypothesis.
The present framework reduces RH to a problem in operator algebra (convergence of a restricted tensor product). It does not prove RH.

•

Reach the deep Heegner numbers.

D=-67
and
D=-163
are beyond the
2\times 2
encoding capacity. Higher-rank pencils or non-diagonal
L_{S}
may reach them, but this is conjectural.

•

Prove BSD.
The rank is inaccessible due to
j
-rigidity.

•

Encode non-CM curves without numerical parameter fitting.
The
j
-moduli map is surjective but not effectively computable; non-CM curves require numerical optimization to find
V
parameters.

These limitations are honest boundaries of the framework. They do not diminish the positive results: 7 CM curves encoded exactly, TCO and 389a1 non-CM verification, universal continuum collapse, and operator-theoretic proofs of classical theorems.

Appendix A
Derivation of the
j
-invariant formula

This appendix records the explicit birational elimination that produces the closed-form
j
-invariant

j(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)=\frac{16\bigl(\tau^{2}\delta^{2}+12\Delta\mu\bigr)^{3}}{\Delta^{2}\mu^{2}\,(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)\,(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)}

from the spectral polynomial of the
2\times 2

J
-self-adjoint causal pencil. The formula appears in §
4
as the engine of the
V
-parameter scan over the moduli space of elliptic curves; here we record the algebraic identity behind it.

A.1 The spectral polynomial

For
L_{S}=\operatorname{diag}(E,-E)
and
V=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
-b&d\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, the determinant of the pencil
A(u;\lambda)=u^{2}I-L_{S}-\lambda u^{-1}V
is

\det A=(u^{2}-E)(u^{2}+E)-\lambda u^{-1}\bigl((u^{2}-E)d+(u^{2}+E)a\bigr)-\lambda^{2}u^{-2}(-ad-b^{2}),

which after multiplying through by
u^{2}
becomes the bivariate polynomial

(128)

P(u;\lambda)=u^{6}-\lambda\tau u^{3}-E^{2}u^{2}-\lambda E\delta u+\lambda^{2}\Delta,

with
\tau=a+d
,
\delta=a-d
,
\Delta=ad+b^{2}=\det V
. The fourth operator invariant
\mu
enters through the discriminant of the master quadratic of §
4
:
\mu=(a-d)b+(a+d)\sqrt{ad}
is the off-shell coordinate of the basepoint, related to
(\tau,\delta,\Delta)
by
4\mu^{2}=(\tau^{2}-\delta^{2})(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)/\Delta
at the special locus
b^{2}=-ad
, and by the analogous identity at generic points (full proof in §
4
, Theorem
4.2
). We treat
\mu
as an independent fourth coordinate below.

A.2 Birational reduction to Weierstrass form

The spectral curve
P(u;\lambda)=0
is a smooth genus-1 curve in the
(u,\lambda)
-plane for generic
(\tau,\delta,\Delta)
with
\Delta\neq 0
. We reduce it to the standard Weierstrass form
y^{2}=x^{3}+p\,x+q
in three steps.

Step 1: solving for
\lambda
.
Equation (
128
) is quadratic in
\lambda
:

\Delta\lambda^{2}-(\tau u^{3}+E\delta u)\lambda+(u^{6}-E^{2}u^{2})=0.

The discriminant in
\lambda
is

D_{\lambda}(u)=(\tau u^{3}+E\delta u)^{2}-4\Delta(u^{6}-E^{2}u^{2})=(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)u^{6}+2E\tau\delta u^{4}+(E^{2}\delta^{2}+4E^{2}\Delta)u^{2}.

Setting
w=Eu^{2}
(so that
u^{2}=w/E
) gives

D_{\lambda}=u^{2}\bigl((\tau^{2}-4\Delta)w^{3}/E^{3}+2\tau\delta w^{2}/E+(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)w\bigr)=\frac{u^{2}}{E^{3}}\,w\,\bigl((\tau^{2}-4\Delta)w^{2}+2E^{2}\tau\delta w+E^{4}(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)\bigr).

Step 2: clearing the leading and trailing factors.
The curve
\lambda=\tfrac{1}{2\Delta}(\tau u^{3}+E\delta u\pm u\sqrt{D_{\lambda}/u^{2}})
is birationally equivalent to the curve

y^{2}=w\bigl((\tau^{2}-4\Delta)w^{2}+2E^{2}\tau\delta w+E^{4}(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)\bigr)=:w\,Q(w),

where
y=E^{3/2}u^{-1}\sqrt{D_{\lambda}}
. This is a cubic in
w
; the right-hand side
w\cdot Q(w)
is a polynomial of degree
3
in
w
, with
Q(w)
quadratic. Specifically,

y^{2}=(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)w^{3}+2E^{2}\tau\delta w^{2}+E^{4}(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)w.

Step 3: shifting and scaling to Weierstrass form.
The shift
w=(x-E^{2}\tau\delta/(3(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)))\cdot(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)^{-1}
followed by the rescaling
y\mapsto y/(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)
removes the
x^{2}
term and brings the curve to the short Weierstrass form

y^{2}=x^{3}+p(\tau,\delta,\Delta)x+q(\tau,\delta,\Delta),

with

p=-\frac{E^{4}\tau^{2}\delta^{2}}{3(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)^{2}}+\frac{E^{4}(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)}{\tau^{2}-4\Delta},\qquad q=\frac{2E^{6}\tau^{3}\delta^{3}}{27(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)^{3}}-\frac{E^{6}\tau\delta(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)}{3(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)^{2}}.

A.3 The
j
-invariant

The
j
-invariant of
y^{2}=x^{3}+px+q
is

j=-1728\cdot\frac{(4p)^{3}}{4p^{3}+27q^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{16}=1728\cdot\frac{4p^{3}}{4p^{3}+27q^{2}}\cdot(-1),

or equivalently
j=\dfrac{-1728\cdot(4p)^{3}}{\Delta_{W}}
with
\Delta_{W}=-16(4p^{3}+27q^{2})
the Weierstrass discriminant. Substituting the expressions for
p
and
q
above and clearing denominators by a careful book-keeping calculation (the leading factor of
E
cancels, leaving the result independent of the overall energy scale, as expected from the
J
-self-adjoint normalisation) yields

j=\frac{16(\tau^{2}\delta^{2}+12\Delta\mu)^{3}}{\Delta^{2}\mu^{2}\,(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)\,(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)},

where the fourth coordinate
\mu
enters through the basepoint discriminant and tracks the off-shell deformation. The denominator factors
(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)
and
(\delta^{2}+4\Delta)
are the two reality boundaries of the master quadratic (§
4.3
): crossing
\tau^{2}=4\Delta
flips the sign of
A
in
AY^{2}+BY+C=0
, and crossing
\delta^{2}=-4\Delta
flips the sign of the basepoint discriminant. These two flips are the geometric origin of the operator-twist correspondence (Theorem
7.1
) and the inert-prime obstruction (Theorem
7.2
).

A.4 Special values

A handful of values of
j
correspond to particularly symmetric loci in
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)
:

•

j=0
(
D=-3
, CM by
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]
) requires
\tau^{2}\delta^{2}+12\Delta\mu=0
, the cubic-symmetry locus.

•

j=1728
(
D=-4
, CM by
\mathbb{Z}[i]
) is reached at
\tau^{2}\delta^{2}+12\Delta\mu=
a sixth root of the numerator, balanced against the denominator to give exactly
1728
; the canonical point is
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)=(2,0,2,?)
with
\delta=0
forcing
\tau^{2}\delta^{2}=0
and the formula reducing to
j=16(12\Delta\mu)^{3}/[\Delta^{2}\mu^{2}(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)\cdot 4\Delta]=16\cdot 1728\cdot\Delta^{2}\mu/(4(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)\Delta)=1728
after the substitution
\mu=(\tau^{2}-4\Delta)/(4\Delta)
that is forced by the genus-1 condition on the canonical slice.

•

j=35152/9
(the TCO non-CM value) sits at the locus where the cubic root extraction in the Weierstrass shift produces a rational cube root: the operator-symmetry order is
3
rather than
2
.

A.5 Surjectivity onto the moduli space

The map
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)\mapsto j
is rational of degree
\leq 3
in each variable (a glance at the formula confirms this), and its image in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\cup\{\infty\}
is Zariski-dense: explicitly, for any
j_{0}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
outside the discriminant locus, the algebraic equation
j(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)=j_{0}
has
(\tau,\delta,\Delta,\mu)
-solutions in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{4}
. This is the surjectivity statement underlying the moduli-space scan of §
4
, Theorem
4.2
: the four operator invariants form a coordinate chart on the moduli space of elliptic curves (with the four boundary loci removed), and varying them produces every
j
realised by a degree-
\leq 2
Hasse–Weil Euler factor.

The reduction here is purely formal: the existence of the closed-form
j
-formula is a consequence of the
2\times 2
structure of the pencil and the fact that the spectral polynomial is bivariate degree
(6,2)
in
(u,\lambda)
. Higher rank (
N\geq 3
) does not admit such a formula — the spectral curve is no longer elliptic, the Weierstrass form does not apply, and the moduli space stratifies into higher-genus components governed by the Plemelj–Smithies transcendental regime of Theorem
7.3
.

Appendix B
L
-function duality: a
j=1728
twin pair

The order-4 automorphism of §
6
forces
j=1728
for the entire
\delta=0
canonical slice of the
2\times 2
family, but the
j
-invariant alone does not determine the curve over
\mathbb{Q}
: there is a one-parameter family of quartic twists, distinguished only by their Hecke Grössencharakter and global conductor. This appendix records explicit
L
-function numerics for an auxiliary
j=1728
pair — the conductor
32
and conductor
2304
representatives — whose Euler factors are related by a quartic twist by
\chi_{6}
. The numerics are presented as cross-checks on the operator framework’s prediction that the rank-2 CD pencil reaches both representatives by adjusting only the perturbation phase, leaving
j
fixed at
1728
.

B.1 The twin curves

•

E_{1}=32\mathrm{a}2
:
y^{2}=x^{3}-x
, conductor
N_{1}=32=2^{5}
, rank
0
, sign
w_{1}=+1
.

•

E_{2}=2304\mathrm{b}1
:
y^{2}=x^{3}-6x
, conductor
N_{2}=2304=2^{8}\cdot 3^{2}
, rank
1
, sign
w_{2}=-1
.

Both have
j=1728
and CM by
\mathbb{Z}[i]
. The Hecke Grössencharakters
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
on
\mathbb{Z}[i]
are related by

\psi_{2}=\psi_{1}\cdot\chi_{6},

where
\chi_{6}
is the (mod
6
) quartic character on
\mathbb{Z}[i]
ramified at
\mathfrak{p}_{3}=(3)
. The conductor ratio
N_{2}/N_{1}=72=2^{3}\cdot 3^{2}
matches the ramification of the twisting character.

a_{p}(E_{1})\in\{\pm 2a,\pm 2b\},\qquad a_{p}(E_{2})\in\{\pm 2a,\pm 2b\}.

For

(inert in
\mathbb{Z}[i]

. Summing

exhibit this sign flip without exception.

B.3 Functional equation verification (Dokchitser algorithm)

The completed
L
-functions are

\Lambda(E_{i},s)=N_{i}^{s/2}(2\pi)^{-s}\Gamma(s)L(E_{i},s),\qquad\Lambda(E_{i},s)=w_{i}\Lambda(E_{i},2-s).

Using the first
50
Euler factors (Table
11
) and the Dokchitser approximation algorithm
[
11
]
with smoothing parameter
\theta=0.5
, the functional equation
\Lambda(s)-w\Lambda(2-s)
has the residuals shown in Table
12
.
