B.4 Central values

The two central values

L(E_{1},1)=0.6555442\ldots,\qquad L^{\prime}(E_{2},1)=2.8286226\ldots,

are consistent with the BSD prediction:
E_{1}
has analytic rank
0
(matching
L(E_{1},1)\neq 0
) and
E_{2}
has analytic rank
1
(matching
L(E_{2},1)=0
,
L^{\prime}(E_{2},1)\neq 0
). The twin pair therefore realises both archetypal rank behaviours through a single quartic twist of the same
j=1728
Hecke Grössencharakter, illustrating the principle that the operator framework’s
j
-invariant alone does not see the rank: the rank is encoded in the global twist data on top of the local Euler structure.

B.5 Relation to the body’s primary pair

The body of the paper develops the RSCO and rank-2 CD pencils as encoding the
j=1728
curves
256\mathrm{b}2
(RSCO canonical,
N=256=2^{8}
) and
32\mathrm{a}2
(CD rank-2,
N=32=2^{5}
) respectively. The twin pair recorded here adds a third
j=1728
representative (
2304\mathrm{b}1
,
N=2304=2^{8}\cdot 3^{2}
) to the catalogue and exhibits an explicit Hecke-twist relation between two of the representatives. The complete catalogue of
j=1728
encodable conductors through the framework is therefore
\{32,256,2304\}
over the small-conductor range, with each conductor accessible from a specific canonical slice of the operator moduli space.

Appendix C
Proof of the restricted tensor product convergence

This appendix supplies a self-contained proof of Theorem
9.1
.
Fix, for each prime
p
, the data

H_{p}=\mathbb{C}^{2},\qquad J_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad\xi_{p}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Each
(H_{p},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)
is a two-dimensional Hilbert space with the
standard Euclidean inner product;
J_{p}
is a fundamental symmetry
(
J_{p}^{2}=I
,
J_{p}^{*}=J_{p}
,
\|J_{p}\|=1
) turning
(H_{p},J_{p})
into a
two-dimensional Krein space. The vector
\xi_{p}
has unit norm

\langle\xi_{p},\xi_{p}\rangle=1
and satisfies
J_{p}\xi_{p}=\xi_{p}
.

C.1.
The restricted tensor product

We recall the construction of the restricted (or incomplete) tensor product of
Hilbert spaces, due to von Neumann [
[
23
]
].

Let
\{H_{p}\}_{p\in\mathbb{P}}
be a countable family of Hilbert spaces and,
for each
p
, choose a distinguished unit vector
\xi_{p}\in H_{p}
.
A
C_{0}
-sequence
is a family
\{v_{p}\}_{p\in\mathbb{P}}
with
v_{p}\in H_{p}

such that
v_{p}=\xi_{p}
for all but finitely many
p
. The

algebraic restricted tensor product
is the vector space

\operatorname{alg}\bigotimes{}^{\prime}_{p}H_{p}\equiv\bigotimes{}^{\prime}_{p}H_{p}=\operatorname{span}\bigl\{\otimes_{p}v_{p}:\{v_{p}\}\text{ is a }C_{0}\text{-sequence}\bigr\},

where
\otimes_{p}v_{p}
denotes the formal simple tensor.

Define an inner product on this space by the bilinear extension of

(129)

\bigl\langle\otimes_{p}v_{p},\ \otimes_{p}w_{p}\bigr\rangle=\prod_{p}\langle v_{p},\,w_{p}\rangle_{H_{p}},

where the infinite product is taken over all primes
p
. The product converges
because, for any two
C_{0}
-sequences
\{v_{p}\}
and
\{w_{p}\}
, there exists a
finite set
S\subset\mathbb{P}
such that
v_{p}=w_{p}=\xi_{p}
for all
p\notin S
;
consequently
\langle v_{p},w_{p}\rangle=1
for
p\notin S
, and only finitely many
factors differ from unity. The convergence is absolute:

(130)

\sum_{p}\bigl|1-\langle v_{p},w_{p}\rangle\bigr|<\infty,

which is automatic here because the sum has only finitely many non-zero terms.

The
restricted tensor product Hilbert space

H_{\zeta}
is the completion
of
\operatorname{alg}\bigotimes{}^{\prime}_{p}H_{p}
under the inner product (
129
).

Lemma C.1

(Well-posedness)
.

The restricted tensor product
H_{\zeta}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{p}(H_{p},\xi_{p})
is a
well-defined separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space.

Proof.

The construction is the standard von Neumann restricted tensor product.
The convergence condition (
130
) is satisfied
trivially because our choice
\xi_{p}
has unit norm, so

\sum_{p}|1-\langle\xi_{p},\xi_{p}\rangle|=0<\infty
. Separability follows from the
fact that each
H_{p}
is finite-dimensional and the set of
C_{0}
-sequences with
rational coefficients in a basis is countable and dense.

To see that
H_{\zeta}
is infinite-dimensional, consider the set of simple
tensors
\{\otimes_{p}v_{p}^{(k)}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
where
v_{p_{k}}^{(k)}
is the
first standard basis vector
e_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}

except at one distinguished
prime
p_{k}
where
v_{p_{k}}^{(k)}=e_{2}
(the second basis vector). These
tensors are orthonormal and distinct for distinct choices of
p_{k}
;
an infinite orthonormal set exists, establishing infinite-dimensionality.
∎∎

C.2.
The indefinite metric
J_{\zeta}

Define a linear operator
J_{\zeta}
on the algebraic restricted tensor product
by its action on simple tensors:

(131)

J_{\zeta}\bigl(\otimes_{p}v_{p}\bigr)=\otimes_{p}(J_{p}v_{p}),

extended linearly. Since each
J_{p}
satisfies
J_{p}^{2}=I
, it follows that

J_{\zeta}^{2}=I
on the algebraic tensor product; hence
J_{\zeta}
is an
involution.

Lemma C.2

(Boundedness and self-adjointness)
.

The operator
J_{\zeta}
extends uniquely to a bounded self-adjoint operator on
the Hilbert space
H_{\zeta}
with
\|J_{\zeta}\|=1
.

Proof.

Boundedness.
For any simple tensor
\Psi=\otimes_{p}v_{p}
, the
definition (
129
) together with
\|J_{p}\|=1
gives

\|J_{\zeta}\Psi\|^{2}=\prod_{p}\|J_{p}v_{p}\|^{2}=\prod_{p}\|v_{p}\|^{2}=\|\Psi\|^{2},

because each
J_{p}
is an isometry of the Euclidean inner product
(
J_{p}^{*}J_{p}=I
). Hence
\|J_{\zeta}\Psi\|=\|\Psi\|
for every simple
tensor, and therefore
\|J_{\zeta}\|=1
on the algebraic tensor product.
By continuous linear extension,
J_{\zeta}
extends uniquely to a bounded
operator on
H_{\zeta}
of norm
1
.

Self-adjointness.
For any two simple tensors

\Psi=\otimes_{p}v_{p}
,
\Phi=\otimes_{p}w_{p}
,

\displaystyle\langle J_{\zeta}\Psi,\,\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\prod_{p}\langle J_{p}v_{p},\,w_{p}\rangle=\prod_{p}\langle v_{p},\,J_{p}w_{p}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi,\,J_{\zeta}\Phi\rangle,

using the self-adjointness
J_{p}^{*}=J_{p}
of each factor. Thus

J_{\zeta}
is symmetric on the dense subspace
\operatorname{alg}\bigotimes{}^{\prime}_{p}H_{p}
, and
boundedness implies
J_{\zeta}^{*}=J_{\zeta}
on all of
H_{\zeta}
.
∎∎

C.3.
Indefinite signature

The fundamental symmetry
J_{\zeta}
partitions
H_{\zeta}
into positive and
negative eigenspaces. Let

H_{\zeta}^{\pm}=\{\Psi\in H_{\zeta}:J_{\zeta}\Psi=\pm\Psi\}.

Lemma C.3

(Infinite-dimensionality of both eigenspaces)
.

Both
\dim H_{\zeta}^{+}=\infty
and
\dim H_{\zeta}^{-}=\infty
.

Proof.

For each
p
, let
e_{+}=(1,0)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
e_{-}=(0,1)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
These are eigenvectors of
J_{p}
:

J_{p}e_{+}=e_{+},\qquad J_{p}e_{-}=-e_{-}.

Positive eigenspace.
For any finite set of primes

S\subset\mathbb{P}
, consider the simple tensor

\Psi_{S}^{+}=\bigl(\otimes_{p\in S}e_{+}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\otimes_{p\notin S}\xi_{p}\bigr).

Since
\xi_{p}=e_{+}/\sqrt{2}
is a positive eigenvector (up to scale),

J_{p}\xi_{p}=\xi_{p}
, we have
J_{\zeta}\Psi_{S}^{+}=\Psi_{S}^{+}
.
Vectors
\Psi_{S}^{+}
for distinct finite sets
S
are linearly independent
(they differ at finitely many primes in the
e_{+}
vs.
\xi_{p}
pattern),
and there are uncountably many such sets; in particular, we can select
countably many distinct ones that are orthogonal, establishing
infinite-dimensionality of
H_{\zeta}^{+}
.

Negative eigenspace.
Pick a distinguished prime
q
.
For any finite set
T\subset\mathbb{P}\setminus\{q\}
, define

\Psi_{T}^{-}=e_{-}^{(q)}\otimes\bigl(\otimes_{p\in T}e_{+}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\otimes_{p\notin T\cup\{q\}}\xi_{p}\bigr),

where
e_{-}^{(q)}
means
e_{-}
in the
q
-th factor. Then

J_{q}e_{-}=-e_{-}
, while every other factor is a positive eigenvector,
so
J_{\zeta}\Psi_{T}^{-}=-\Psi_{T}^{-}
. Distinct finite sets
T
again give
linearly independent vectors in
H_{\zeta}^{-}
, establishing
infinite-dimensionality.

Hence both
\dim H_{\zeta}^{+}
and
\dim H_{\zeta}^{-}
are infinite.
∎∎

Corollary C.4

(Krein space structure)
.

(H_{\zeta},J_{\zeta})
is a Krein space whose positive and negative indices
are
(p,q)=(\aleph_{0},\aleph_{0})
. It is not a Pontryagin space,
because
\kappa\equiv\min(p,q)=\aleph_{0}
is infinite.

Proof.

By definition, a Krein space is a Hilbert space
H
equipped with a
fundamental symmetry
J
(i.e.
J^{2}=I
,
J^{*}=J
,
\|J\|=1
). Lemmas

C.2
and
C.3
verify this precisely:

J_{\zeta}
is a fundamental symmetry and both spectral projections onto

\pm 1
are infinite-rank. The Krein–Pontryagin index
\kappa
is

\min(\dim H_{\zeta}^{+},\dim H_{\zeta}^{-})=\aleph_{0}
, so the space lies outside
the Pontryagin class (which requires
\kappa<\infty
). ∎∎

C.4.
Summary and remarks

The four claims of Theorem
9.1
are therefore established:

(1)

H_{\zeta}
is a well-defined separable Hilbert space
(Lemma
C.1
).

(2)

J_{\zeta}
extends to a bounded self-adjoint operator on
H_{\zeta}
with

\|J_{\zeta}\|=1
(Lemma
C.2
).

(3)

Both
\pm 1
eigenspaces of
J_{\zeta}
are infinite-dimensional
(Lemma
C.3
).

(4)

(H_{\zeta},J_{\zeta})
is a Krein space with
\kappa=\aleph_{0}
, not a
Pontryagin space (Corollary
C.4
).

Remark C.5
.

The triviality
\sum_{p}|1-\langle\xi_{p},\xi_{p}\rangle|=0
follows from the
fact that all
\xi_{p}
are identical unit vectors. If one wished to allow
prime-dependent reference vectors
\xi_{p}
with norms not identically 1,
the convergence condition would require
\sum_{p}|1-\langle\xi_{p},\xi_{p}\rangle|<\infty
,
which is the generic von Neumann condition. The present setting is
the simplest possible case.

Remark C.6

(Connection to the remaining statements)
.

With the Hilbert space convergence and the Krein structure in hand, the
remaining statements address finer properties. Theorem
9.2

proves that the
\eta
-Gram of the global operator
H_{\zeta}
— the
indefinite analogue of the Gram matrix — is diagonal when each local
factor has a diagonal
\eta
-Gram. Conjecture
9.3
concerns the
trace of the global resolvent and its relation to the Weil explicit formula.
Together, these three statements would complete the Hilbert–Pólya realisation of

\zeta(s)
.
